Mathematical Modeling and Error Estimation for the Thermal Dunking Problem: A Hierarchical Approach
Theron Guo, Kento Kaneko, Claude Le Bris, Anthony T. Patera

Abstract
We consider the thermal dunking problem, in which a solid body is suddenly immersed in a fluid of different temperature, and study both the temporal evolution of the solid and the associated Biot number—a non-dimensional heat transfer coefficient characterizing heat exchange across the solid-fluid interface. We focus on the small-Biot-number regime. The problem is accurately described by the conjugate heat transfer (CHT) formulation, which couples the Navier-Stokes and energy equations in the fluid with the heat equation in the solid through interfacial continuity conditions. Because full CHT simulations are computationally expensive, simplified models are often used in practice. Starting from the coupled equations, we systematically reduce the formulation to the lumped-capacitance model, a single ordinary differential equation with a closed-form solution, based on two assumptions: time scale separation and a spatially uniform solid temperature. The total modeling error is decomposed into time homogenization and lumping contributions. We derive an asymptotic error bound for the lumping error, valid for general heterogeneous solids and spatially varying heat transfer coefficients. Building on this theoretical result, we introduce a computable upper bound expressed in measurable quantities for practical evaluation. Time scale separation is analyzed theoretically and supported by physical arguments and simulations, showing that large separation yields small time homogenization errors. In practice, the Biot number must be estimated from so-called empirical correlations, which are typically limited to specific canonical geometries. We propose a data-driven framework that extends empirical correlations to a broader range of geometries through learned characteristic length scales. All results are validated by direct numerical simulations up to Reynolds numbers of 10,000. Acknowledgments. This work is supported by ONR Grant N000142312573. We thank Dr Reza Malek-Madani for his support and also for many helpful scientific discussions over the years. Keywords: conjugate heat transfer, lumped capacitance model, small Biot number, asymptotic analysis, error analysis, time homogenization, Nusselt correlations, empirical correlations



1
Introduction

Heat transfer plays a central role in many engineering applications, including heat treatment, electronic cooling, and materials processing
[
2
,
19
]
. The three fundamental physical mechanisms are conduction, convection (forced and natural), and radiation
[
7
,
20
,
25
]
. In many cases, heat exchange occurs between a solid object and a working fluid. Engineers are often concerned with the thermal response of the solid to support design decisions and predict system performance. Although the governing equations—a coupled system of partial differential equations—accurately describe the underlying physics, their solution is computationally expensive and requires detailed material data which may not be readily available. As a result, simplified models that require fewer inputs are often employed for estimation. However, such models are generally motivated heuristically and lack theoretical justification.

In this work, we develop a rigorous mathematical framework for simplified heat-transfer models and derive corresponding error bounds which quantify their accuracy relative to the full conjugate heat-transfer formulation. To begin, we describe in this introduction the specific problem addressed, formulate the central research question, review related literature, and outline the main contributions along with the roadmap of the manuscript.

1.1
Problem of Interest: Thermal Dunking Problem

We consider the
dunking problem
: a passive solid body at initial uniform temperature
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{i}}
is abruptly placed at time
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
in a fluid environment at initial and far-field temperature
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
; we wish to find the temperature evolution of the solid body over the time interval
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu<\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\leq\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
. Note underlined variables refer to dimensional quantities, while non-dimensional variables are unadorned.

1.1.1
A truth mathematical model

The dunking problem is best described by a two-domain mathematical model: the Navier-Stokes/Boussinesq equations and energy equation in the fluid domain
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
; the heat equation (time-dependent conduction) in the solid domain
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}
; and interface conditions enforcing continuity of temperature and heat flux across the fluid-solid boundary
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
. This formulation is commonly referred to as
conjugate heat transfer (CHT) analysis
in the heat transfer literature
[
28
]
, and will hereafter be regarded as the
truth model
. In this work, we neglect the effects of natural convection and thermal radiation
1
1

1

The truth model presented here can be extended to account for additional mechanisms of heat transfer. Natural convection is typically included via a buoyancy term in the Navier-Stokes equations, and thermal radiation can be incorporated by modifying the boundary conditions at the interface to include radiative heat exchange. Although our main results are derived under the assumption of forced convection, they may be extended to include these additional effects.
. We have the following governing equations for the velocity and pressure fields
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}
and
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mup\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}+(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mup\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}+\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\nu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\Delta}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}},

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\cdot\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}},

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu

\displaystyle\text{on }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}\bm{e}_{x}

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}},

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|\to\infty)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}\bm{e}_{x}

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}},

and for the temperature fields
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
and
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
in the fluid and solid domains,

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\left(\frac{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}+\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\Delta}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}},

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\frac{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\cdot(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}})

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}},

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle\text{on }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}

\displaystyle\text{on }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}},

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{i}}

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}},

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|\to\infty)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}.

The far-field velocity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
is assumed constant and, without loss of generality, taken to be directed along the
x
-axis. The unit normal vector
\bm{n}
on
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
is defined to point outward from the solid into the fluid. To ensure a unique solution for the pressure field, the pressure level is fixed by enforcing a zero-mean condition over the fluid domain at all times.

The CHT model requires several parameters: the thermophysical properties of the solid, namely thermal conductivity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}
, density
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}
, and specific heat
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}
; the properties of the fluid, including density
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
, specific heat
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
, thermal conductivity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
, and kinematic viscosity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\nu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
; and the far-field velocity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
as well as the initial and far-field temperatures,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{i}}
and
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
. The fluid properties are assumed constant, while the solid properties may vary spatially. Of particular interest are multi-phase heterogeneous solids, where the material properties are constant within each phase and may exhibit discontinuities across phase interfaces; while such discontinuities are not directly compatible with the strong form, they are naturally accommodated in the weak formulation, which we adopt for the theoretical analysis and numerical solution. We do not consider temperature-dependent thermophysical properties.

After solving the coupled system, we can compute the
heat transfer coefficient

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}
, defined on the fluid-solid interface
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, as

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}\coloneqq-\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}}.

This coefficient is of central importance in engineering applications, as it characterizes the rate of heat exchange between the fluid and solid normalized by the difference in interface and far-field temperatures. It generally varies in both space and time. Practitioners are typically interested in the space-time average,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
. We additionally define the time-dependent spatial average,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
, and the spatially varying temporal average,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}
.

We now describe the non-dimensionalization of the CHT equations. We associate to the solid domain two length scales: a chosen first length scale, denoted extrinsic,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
; and a second length scale, denoted intrinsic,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{L}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\coloneqq|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}|/|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}|
, where
|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}|
and
|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}|
denote the volume and surface area of the solid, respectively. We may then introduce the non-dimensional variables that will be used throughout this work. Lengths are scaled with the extrinsic length scale
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, and hence the non-dimensional spatial coordinate is given by
\bm{x}\coloneqq\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
. Times are scaled with the solid diffusive time scale
(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}})_{\mathrm{avg}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}}
, where
(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}})_{\mathrm{avg}}
denotes the spatial average of
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}
over
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}
and
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}}
is the (essential) infimum of
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}
over
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}
; the resulting non-dimensional time variable (Fourier number) is
t\coloneqq\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}}/((\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}})_{\mathrm{avg}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2})
. Velocity is non-dimensionalized with respect to the free-stream velocity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
, so that
\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\coloneqq\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
; pressure is scaled by
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\nu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, and thus the non-dimensional pressure field reads
p^{\mathrm{CHT}}\coloneqq\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mup\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu/(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\nu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty})
. Temperature is non-dimensionalized relative to the initial and far-field temperatures: in the solid,
u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\coloneqq(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty})/(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{i}}-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty})
, and in the fluid,
u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\coloneqq(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty})/(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{i}}-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty})
.

The non-dimensional CHT model depends on six key input parameters. These are the volumetric specific heat ratio and the thermal conductivity ratio between fluid and solid,

\displaystyle r_{1}\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}{(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}})_{\mathrm{avg}}},\quad r_{2}\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}}};

(1.3)

the non-dimensional thermophysical properties of the solid,

\displaystyle\sigma\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}}{(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}})_{\mathrm{avg}}},\quad\kappa\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}}};

(1.4)

and the Reynolds and Prandtl numbers,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\nu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}},\quad\operatorname{Pr}\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\nu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}/(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}})}.

(1.5)

The non-dimensional CHT equations are given in
Section

2.1.2
.

Finally, we introduce the non-dimensional counterpart of the heat transfer coefficient, the
Nusselt number
,

\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\coloneqq\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}},

along with its spatial, temporal, and space-time averages: respectively
\operatorname{Nu}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
,
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
, and
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
. To quantify the spatial and temporal variations of the local Nusselt number relative to its mean, we define the variation function

\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}\coloneqq\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}/\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}},

and its temporal average,
\overline{\eta}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
. We also define the
Biot number
,

B^{\mathrm{CHT}}\coloneqq\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}},

which is a constant scalar, independent of space and time. It is directly related to the space-time averaged Nusselt number through

B^{\mathrm{CHT}}=r_{2}\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}.

While the Nusselt number reflects the intensity of convective heat transfer in the fluid, the Biot number captures how effectively the solid conducts heat relative to the external convective loading. We have the following relations between the heat transfer coefficient, Nusselt number,
\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}
, and Biot number:

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}r_{2}\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}B^{\mathrm{CHT}}\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}},

(1.6)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}B^{\mathrm{CHT}}.

(1.7)

We focus on problems in the
small-Biot limit
,
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}\rightarrow 0
. In practice, the small-Biot limit frequently arises, in particular for “everyday” artifacts at modest temperatures subject to natural convection and radiation heat transfer, as well as for smaller artifacts (for example, sensors) subject to modest forced convection heat transfer. In contrast, larger systems at very elevated temperatures or subject to brisk forced convection or change-of-phase heat transfer typically yield larger Biot number. An engineering example of a small-Biot application is the process of annealing by natural convection—for instance, to relieve residual stresses.

We are primarily interested in one quantity of interest (QoI): the weighted average temperature of the solid as a function of time, denoted by
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
(dimensional) and
u_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t)
(non-dimensional); their precise definitions are given in
Section

2.1
.

The truth mathematical model, which we have just described above, is rarely invoked in engineering practice, in particular in the context of engineering estimation. The computational cost is prohibitively high, and the data requirements are restrictive: many materials are inherently heterogeneous, and the exact spatial distribution are often unknown without destructive inspection.

1.1.2
Robin heat equations

The CHT model can be recast as a single-domain heat equation defined solely on the solid domain, in which the fluid domain is eliminated and its influence captured through a Robin boundary condition on the interface. We refer to this formulation as the
equivalent Robin heat equation
(RHE).

As we now only consider the solid domain, we omit the subscript “s” and set
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\equiv\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
. The dimensional equations are given by

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\frac{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\cdot(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}})

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}+\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty})

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu

\displaystyle\text{on }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu1\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,

with the average temperature as QoI, as before, denoted by
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
(but now without the subscript “s”). The heat transfer coefficient
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}
now appears as the Robin coefficient in the boundary condition.

The solution to the RHE exactly reproduces the solid temperature field of the full CHT model, i.e.,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}\equiv\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
and
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\equiv\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
. However, since
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}
is not known
a priori
and must be extracted from the coupled fluid-solid solution, the RHE in this form is not directly useful for practical prediction and estimation. Nonetheless, the equations provide a valuable starting point for systematic model simplification. More details on the equivalence are provided in
Section

2.2.1
.

In the non-dimensional setting, the Robin coefficient is given by
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})
, and the equations are

\displaystyle\sigma\frac{\partial u^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\bm{\nabla}\cdot(\kappa\bm{\nabla}u^{\mathrm{CHT}})

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

\displaystyle\kappa\bm{\nabla}u^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}+B^{\mathrm{CHT}}\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})u^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\Omega,

\displaystyle u^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t=0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega.

We denote the corresponding non-dimensional average temperature by
u_{\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t)
. Recall that both
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
and
\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}
are derived from the local Nusselt number
\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})
through the relations
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}=r_{2}\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
and
\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})=\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})/\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
, with
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
being a constant scalar independent of space and time. We can now motivate the small-Biot limit: for
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}\rightarrow 0
, the Robin boundary condition asymptotically approaches a homogeneous Neumann boundary condition, from which it follows that temperature gradients within the solid are small, and the solid temperature remains approximately uniform. We focus on this limit in the present work.

Our theoretical framework is informed by the general behavior of
\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})
. It is observed that
\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
fluctuates in time but quickly settles around a steady mean value. This behavior is illustrated in
Fig.

1.1
, which shows the temporal evolution of the Nusselt number at various locations on a circular cylinder in cross-flow, along with the non-dimensional average solid temperature, for one representative case in the small-Biot regime (
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}=0.0680
with diameter as length scale). This result was obtained numerically using Nek5000
[
11
]
, a spectral-element solver for the CHT formulation; additional details are provided in
Section

2.1.4
. Following an initial sharp drop, the spatially averaged Nusselt number stabilizes by
t\approx 0.3
, with only minor fluctuations thereafter. While a transition occurs near
t\approx 1.5
, the magnitude of
\operatorname{Nu}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
remains largely unchanged after
t\approx 0.3
. During this period, the solid temperature changes very little, indicating a clear
separation of time scales
: the flow equilibrates much faster than the thermal response of the solid.

To quantify this, we define the convective time scale
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\tau\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{conv}}\coloneqq\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
, which becomes
\tau_{\mathrm{conv}}=r_{1}/(r_{2}\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{Pr})
under our non-dimensionalization. For the case shown, this yields
\tau_{\mathrm{conv}}\approx 0.012
. Meanwhile, the average temperature
u_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t)
evolves approximately as a simple exponential decay. Defining the equilibrium time constant
\tau_{\mathrm{eq}}
such that
u_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\tau_{\mathrm{eq}})=\exp(-1)
, we find
\tau_{\mathrm{eq}}\approx 3.77
—over two orders of magnitude larger than
\tau_{\mathrm{conv}}
, indicating a time scale separation. The approximate exponential,

\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{fit}}\coloneqq\exp(-t/\tau_{\mathrm{eq}})

(1.10)

is also shown in
Fig.

1(b)
and yields an accurate approximation of
u_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
. More details on time scales will be provided in
Sections

2.2.2
and
4.2
.

This time scale separation motivates the definition of the
autonomous Robin heat equation
(RHE
a
), in which the Robin coefficient—which characterizes the fluid—is assumed to be time-independent but spatially varying. The governing equations are identical to those of the RHE, except that we restrict the heat transfer coefficient to be fixed in time and only vary in space, here denoted
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
:

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\frac{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\widetilde{T}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\cdot(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\widetilde{T}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,

(1.11a)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\widetilde{T}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\cdot\bm{n}+\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\widetilde{T}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty})

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu

\displaystyle\text{on }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,

(1.11b)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\widetilde{T}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu1\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu.

(1.11c)

In the non-dimensional formulation, the Robin coefficient becomes
B\overline{\eta}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
, where
\overline{\eta}
is assumed to vary only in space.

The Robin coefficient serves as an input to the system and, in principle, can be prescribed arbitrarily, provided that it is positive, i.e.,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu>0
or equivalently
B\overline{\eta}>0
. However, since no choice of the Robin coefficient can make the RHE
a
exactly equivalent to the full CHT formulation, we compute in
Eq.

1.11
an approximation to the true temperature field (obtained with the CHT formulation), which we denote by
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\widetilde{T}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
in the dimensional setting and
\widetilde{u}
in the non-dimensional setting. The corresponding QoIs—i.e., the average temperatures—are denoted by
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\widetilde{T}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{avg}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
and
\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t)
, respectively.

The RHE
a
represents a significant simplification by eliminating the time dependence of the Robin coefficient. However, it requires a spatially varying heat transfer coefficient
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
(or equivalently,
B\overline{\eta}
) as input. Since this quantity varies along the boundary, see
Fig.

1(a)
, estimating it directly in engineering practice is generally impractical. As a result, the RHE
a
formulation, while useful for analysis, is rarely used for estimation.

(a)

Local and average Nusselt number.

(b)

Non-dimensional average temperature over time.

Figure 1.1
:
(a) Nusselt number at the leading edge (LE), trailing edge (TE), side edge (SE), and the spatial average over the surface of a circular cylinder in cross-flow. The superscripts “CHT” are omitted. (b) Evolution of the non-dimensional average solid temperature, which closely follows a simple exponential decay.

1.1.3
Lumped capacitance model

In engineering estimation, a further reduction—now relative to the autonomous Robin heat equation—is desirable. This leads to the widely used
lumped capacitance model
(LCM), which can be derived from the RHE
a
by assuming that the solid maintains a spatially uniform temperature at all times. Denoting this uniform temperature by
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\widetilde{T}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{L}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
, we integrate the governing equation in
Eq.

1.11
over the solid domain and apply the divergence theorem to obtain:

\displaystyle\frac{d\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\widetilde{T}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{L}}}{d\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|}{(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)_{\mathrm{avg}}|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\widetilde{T}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{L}}-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}).

Hence, the LCM is a single ordinary differential equation (ODE) for
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\widetilde{T}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{L}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
, which admits an analytical solution,

\displaystyle(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\widetilde{T}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{L}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty})/(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{i}}-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty})=\exp(-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\tau\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}),

where
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\tau\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}\coloneqq(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)_{\mathrm{avg}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{L}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}
is the associated dimensional time constant. Recall that
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{L}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|/|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|
is the intrinsic length scale. In the non-dimensional setting, the LCM yields a time constant
\tau^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}\coloneqq 1/(B\gamma)
, where
\gamma\coloneqq\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{L}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
is the ratio between extrinsic and intrinsic length scale, and

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t)\coloneqq\exp(-t/\tau^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}).

Comparing this expression with the approximate exponential decay of the average temperature in
Eq.

1.10
, we see that the two expressions differ only in their time constants,
\tau_{\mathrm{eq}}
and
\tau^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}
.

The time constant
\tau^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}
is inversely proportional to the Biot number
B
. We can compare this time constant with the diffusive time scale in the solid,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\tau\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{diff}}\coloneqq(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}})_{\mathrm{avg}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}}
, which characterizes how quickly diffusion acts within the solid, and which, under our nondimensionalization, reduces to
\tau_{\mathrm{diff}}=1
. The ratio of the two time scales is given by
\tau^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}/\tau_{\mathrm{diff}}=1/(B\gamma)
, and shows that for small Biot numbers the cooling of the solid is much slower than the internal diffusion. Hence surface and bulk temperatures equilibrate rapidly relative to the overall thermal response, justifying the uniform temperature assumption for small Biot number. This also means that the LCM is a good approximation only in the small-Biot regime, i.e., as
B\rightarrow 0
. We will study this error in
Section

4.3
.

A key advantage of the LCM is its minimal input requirements: the average volumetric specific heat of the solid,
(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)_{\mathrm{avg}}
; the intrinsic length scale
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{L}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, which only depends on body geometry; and the average heat transfer coefficient
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}
. The former two can, in principle, be determined from the solid geometry and composition. The latter is now only a scalar value, much more amenable for estimation.

1.1.4
Nusselt approximation: empirical correlations

The biggest challenge in using the LCM is finding a good estimate for the average heat transfer coefficient
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}
(or
B)
for a given dunking scenario,
without
solving the CHT. In practice, that means finding a good approximation of the true average Nusselt number
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
, see
Eq.

1.7
.

Historically, such approximations have been derived from so-called
empirical correlations

[
20
,
25
]
, which relate the averaged Nusselt number to the Reynolds and Prandtl numbers (see
Eq.

1.5
for their definitions):

\displaystyle\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\approx f^{\mathcal{S}}(\operatorname{Re},\operatorname{Pr}),

(1.12)

where
f^{\mathcal{S}}
is defined for a family of similar shapes
\mathcal{S}
, such as spheres or circular cylinders of varying diameter. These expressions typically assume a homogeneous solid with constant properties, i.e.,
\kappa=\sigma=1
(see the definitions in
Eq.

1.4
), and neglect the thermophysical property ratios
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
(defined in
Eq.

1.3
). Neglecting the effect on
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
is justified by the observation that, in many common engineering applications, these ratios are small, and the value of
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
shows negligible sensitivity to them.

The functions
f^{\mathcal{S}}
are typically of power-law form, motivated by boundary layer theory
[
33
]
, with coefficients fitted to experimental data. More recently, high-fidelity numerical simulations have also been used to construct such correlations. However, new correlations must be developed for each geometry and flow configuration, making the process labor-intensive. Note that in this work, we use the term correlation interchangeably with empirical correlation, without any statistical interpretation.

1.2
Research Question

Our goal is to develop an error estimate between the LCM and the full CHT model for
general geometries
,
general thermophysical property distributions
, and
general flow conditions
, in the small-Biot regime. First recall that the non-dimensional RHE QoI,
u_{\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B^{\mathrm{CHT}},\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x}))
, is equivalent to the QoI computed with the CHT formulation. Our objective is to estimate the error incurred by using the LCM with an approximate Biot number
\undertilde{B}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

without
solving the full CHT model, i.e.,
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
and
\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})
are unknown. To achieve this, we decompose the total error, given by
|u_{\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B^{\mathrm{CHT}},\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x}))-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;\undertilde{B}^{\mathrm{CHT}})|
, into three contributions:

\displaystyle|u_{\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B^{\mathrm{CHT}},\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x}))

\displaystyle-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;\undertilde{B}^{\mathrm{CHT}})|

\displaystyle\leq|u_{\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B^{\mathrm{CHT}},\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x}))-\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B^{\mathrm{CHT}},\overline{\eta}(\bm{x}))|

(1.13)

\displaystyle+|\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B^{\mathrm{CHT}},\overline{\eta}(\bm{x}))-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B^{\mathrm{CHT}})|

(1.14)

\displaystyle+|\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B^{\mathrm{CHT}})-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;\undertilde{B}^{\mathrm{CHT}})|.

(1.15)

The first error corresponds to a
temporal approximation error
, incurred by replacing the time-varying Robin coefficient
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})
with a time-independent approximation
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})
; the second is the
lumped approximation error
, introduced by replacing the spatially varying RHE
a
solution with the LCM; and the third is the
Biot approximation error
, resulting from substituting the Biot number
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
with an approximate value
\undertilde{B}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
.

1.3
Related Work

Despite the simplicity and utility of the small-Biot lumped approximation, there is relatively little analysis of the associated approximation error. Most engineering textbooks
[
7
,
20
,
25
]
indicate only that
\operatorname{Bi}_{\mathrm{dunk}}
must be
small
. Here,
\operatorname{Bi}_{\mathrm{dunk}}
refers to the Biot number based on the intrinsic length scale
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{L}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, and relates to our Biot number through
\operatorname{Bi}_{\mathrm{dunk}}\coloneqq B/\gamma
, where
\gamma=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{L}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
. In some cases, a rough threshold is suggested, such as
\operatorname{Bi}_{\mathrm{dunk}}\leq 0.1
, but typically with vague qualifiers such as “usually”.

To the best of our knowledge, the only study that systematically examines the full CHT-to-LCM error is the work of Virag et al.
[
35
]
, in which the authors perform a numerical comparison between the LCM and full CHT model for a homogeneous sphere subjected to natural convection, where the flow is hence characterized by the Rayleigh number rather than the Reynolds number. Their parametric study spans four non-dimensional inputs: the initial Rayleigh number, initial Biot number, Prandtl number, and the fluid-to-solid thermal diffusivity ratio.
Their results demonstrate that, for many combinations of these parameters, the CHT-to-LCM error is small. Nevertheless, their study is limited to one specific setting, and does not provide any more generalizable theoretical insights.

Other existing works focus solely on the lumped approximation error. In the work of Gockenbach and Schmidtke
[
15
]
, a rigorous asymptotic error estimate is derived for the particular case of a sphere with homogeneous thermophysical properties and spatially uniform Robin coefficient. In our notation (see
Eq.

1.14
), the result reads:

\displaystyle|\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa=1,\sigma=1,B^{\mathrm{CHT}},\overline{\eta}=1)-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B^{\mathrm{CHT}})|\leq(3/5)B^{\mathrm{CHT}}/(\gamma\exp(1))+O((B^{\mathrm{CHT}})^{2}),\quad\forall t\geq 0.

Recently, we have generalized the bound of Gockenbach and Schmidtke in
[
21
]
to arbitrary geometries with heterogeneous thermophysical properties, while still assuming a spatially uniform Robin coefficient. The bound we derived in
[
21
]
, expressed in our notation here, reads:

\displaystyle|\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B^{\mathrm{CHT}},\overline{\eta}=1)-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B^{\mathrm{CHT}})|\leq\phi(\kappa,\sigma)B^{\mathrm{CHT}}/(\gamma\exp(1))+O((B^{\mathrm{CHT}})^{2}),\quad\forall t\geq 0,

(1.16)

where
\phi(\kappa,\sigma)
is a dimensionless coefficient determined as the solution to an auxiliary linear elliptic partial differential equation. In the special case of a homogeneous sphere, this bound reduces to that of Gockenbach and Schmidtke with
\phi=3/5
. An in-depth analysis of the coefficient
\phi
is also provided, including its mathematical properties, bounds, and numerical evaluations for various geometries. However, because the analysis in
[
21
]
assumes a spatially uniform Robin coefficient—except for some exploratory work on non-uniform Robin coefficients in an Appendix D—the result cannot be directly used to bound the error in the LCM approximation relative to the CHT solution.

Many studies in the literature have focused on estimating the average Nusselt number for particular geometries and flow conditions, usually by proposing empirical correlations derived from experimental data. Classic examples in forced convection include the semi-analytical flat-plate correlation for axial flow
[
33
]
, as well as empirical fits for cylinders
[
8
]
and spheres
[
37
]
under cross-flow. To extend beyond these specific shapes, Culham et al.
[
9
]
proposed a unified correlation for arbitrary cuboids, based on flow path lengths. Similarly, Yovanovich
[
38
]
developed a general correlation for spheroids of varying aspect ratios in cross-flow. For natural convection, Hassani et al.
[
16
]
investigated a wide variety of shapes and introduced a simple correlation using geometric shape factors to account for different geometries. Lienhard
[
24
]
observed that, with an appropriate choice of length scale, the ratio of Nusselt number to Rayleigh number remains nearly constant across many geometries; however, he did not provide a general method for selecting such a length scale.

More recently, high-fidelity numerical simulations have been employed to investigate convective heat transfer in complex geometries. For example, Ke et al.
[
22
]
studied the drag force and average Nusselt number for scalene ellipsoids under forced convection. Hirasawa et al.
[
18
]
analyzed heat transfer in packed beds of spheres. Nada et al.
[
26
]
combined experimental and numerical approaches to examine semi-circular tubes. A convective heat transfer model for disk-shaped particles ranging from flat disks to cylinders was proposed in
[
36
]
, while Richter and Nikrityuk
[
30
,
31
]
considered a broad range of shapes—including cubes, cylinders, spheroids, and cuboids at various angles of attack. This list is not exhaustive but highlights the growing role of simulation in heat transfer studies.

Despite all these contributions in Nusselt number estimation, we are not aware of any systematic studies that examine the influence of the thermophysical property ratios
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
(defined in
Eq.

1.3
). Most existing works either assume these ratios to be very small—an assumption valid for many engineering applications, and under which the Nusselt number has been empirically shown to be largely insensitive to them—or consider only a limited set of specific cases where they are prescribed explicitly. Moreover, nearly all works are restricted to homogeneous solids; even though many engineering applications—such as pebble-bed nuclear reactors and composite systems—involve heterogeneous materials. In such settings, the fluid-solid property ratios may not be small, and layered or multi-material structures might substantially alter the heat transfer behavior.

1.4
Contributions

We make the following contributions:

1.

In
Section

4.3.1
, we derive a sharp asymptotic upper bound for the lumped approximation error in
Eq.

1.14
:

\displaystyle|\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B^{\mathrm{CHT}},\overline{\eta})-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B^{\mathrm{CHT}})|\leq\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})B^{\mathrm{CHT}}/(\gamma\exp(1))+O((B^{\mathrm{CHT}})^{2}),\quad\forall t\geq 0.

This bound extends our previous results in
[
21
]
to the general case of a spatially non-uniform Robin coefficient. The coefficient
\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})
generalizes the corresponding quantity in
Eq.

1.16
, which was derived under the assumption
\overline{\eta}=1
, and is likewise obtained as the solution to an auxiliary linear elliptic partial differential equation, specified in
Eqs.

4.44
and
4.49
. By accounting for spatial variation in
\overline{\eta}
, the present result establishes a direct connection to the full CHT model.

2.

To compute
\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})
from the auxiliary problem requires full knowledge of the spatial fields
\kappa
,
\sigma
, and
\overline{\eta}
, see
Eq.

4.49
. Since
\overline{\eta}
depends on the flow and is generally unknown without solving the CHT problem, the derived error bound is not practical for predictive use. Moreover, even
\kappa
and
\sigma
may be inaccessible in practice—for example, we often do not know the internal structure of the solid without destructive inspection. To overcome this limitation, we derive an upper bound for
\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})
in
Section

4.3.2
,

\displaystyle\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})\leq(\sqrt{\phi(1,1,1)}+\sqrt{\delta^{\sigma}}+\sqrt{\delta^{\overline{\eta}}})^{2},

where
\phi(1,1,1)
is a purely geometric quantity that can be computed from the auxiliary problem with uniform inputs. The correction terms
\delta^{\sigma}
and
\delta^{\overline{\eta}}
, defined in
Eqs.

4.62
and
4.63
, depend on two stability constants—geometric quantities—and on the variances of
\sigma
and
\overline{\eta}
, given by
\int_{\Omega}(\sigma-1)^{2}/|\Omega|
and
\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)^{2}/|\partial\Omega|
respectively. Note that in this work the term “variance” is not used in the statistical sense, but rather as a measure of spatial heterogeneity. The stability constants are determined by solving two eigenvalue problems, defined in
Eqs.

4.64
and
4.65
. The numerical treatment of the eigenvalue problems is outlined in Appendix
B
.

For a heterogeneous solid with piecewise constant material properties, the variance of
\sigma
can be evaluated
without
detailed knowledge of the spatial microstructure; only the volume fraction and thermophysical property of each phase is required. The variance of
\overline{\eta}
, in contrast, depends on the flow and is therefore more difficult to estimate. Nevertheless, since it is a scalar quantity, it may be realistic to estimate it. From a physical standpoint, it is unlikely that the variance would exceed 1.0, as this would imply regions of extremely high heat transfer adjacent to regions of negligible transfer. Based on both archival data and our own simulation results, we observe that for several convex geometries (e.g., spheres, cylinders), the variance of
\overline{\eta}
does not exceed 0.5. Details on this study are provided in Appendix
A
. Moreover, this variance tends to decrease with increasing Reynolds number, possibly due to enhanced mixing and turbulence of the flow. In practice, this suggests that one could estimate the variance for a given geometry under low Reynolds number conditions—where simulations are computationally much easier—and safely reuse this bound for higher Reynolds numbers.

3.

We investigate the temporal approximation error, defined in
Eq.

1.13
, through mathematical analysis and numerical simulations in
Section

4.2
, and find that it remains small when there is a clear separation of time scales between fluid and solid dynamics—that is, when the flow equilibrates much faster than the thermal response of the solid. Motivated by this behavior—and consistent with engineering intuition—we select
\overline{\eta}
as the temporal average of the time-dependent
\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}
. This can be interpreted as a time homogenization step that averages over fast fluctuations. In Appendix
D
, we provide a mathematical proof that shows for a simplified setting—a Robin heat equation with a spatially uniform time-periodic Robin coefficient—that the optimal approximation is indeed given by the temporal average.

4.

A key practical question is how to accurately estimate the average Nusselt number
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
(and thus the Biot number
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
) for arbitrary geometries and flow conditions, thereby minimizing the Biot estimation error in
Eq.

1.15
. In
Section

5.3
, we propose a novel data-driven framework for estimating the average Nusselt number
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
across a wide range of geometries and flow conditions using a single empirical correlation in the form of
Eq.

1.12
. For each geometry, we learn an appropriate length scale
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, inspired by Lienhard’s work in natural convection
[
24
]
, to be used for the evaluation of the correlation. This has two main advantages: (1) improved interpretability, and (2) the ability to extend correlations originally developed for specific shape families to more general geometries. We demonstrate the effectiveness of this approach using a large dataset of spheroidal geometries in
Section

5.3.5
.

5.

We present numerical results in
Section

5.4.1
that highlight the limitations of existing empirical correlations for the Nusselt number; as they typically do not account for solid heterogeneity (
\kappa,\sigma
) nor the thermophysical property ratios between solid and fluid (
r_{1},r_{2}
). In this work, we restrict ourselves to the homogeneous case (
\kappa=\sigma=1
) to systematically investigate the influence of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
. When
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are small, the average Nusselt number
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
exhibits negligible sensitivity to these parameters, and empirical correlations remain reasonably accurate. However, this insensitivity breaks down for larger values of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, and neglecting their effect leads to significant predictive error. We identify threshold values of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
at which the error increases sharply for several representative cases. We also provide material properties of fluid-solid combinations commonly encountered in engineering applications.

1.5
Roadmap

In
Sections

2
and
3
, we present the full set of mathematical models considered in this work: the conjugate heat transfer (CHT) model, the Robin heat equation (RHE), the autonomous Robin heat equation (RHE
a
), and the lumped capacitance model (LCM). We also introduce the isothermal wall formulation (ISO), which was not discussed in this introductory section. We discuss their derivation, non-dimensionalization, and define all quantities of interest. An illustrative example is considered to compare the models. In
Section

4
, we turn to our main theoretical contribution: the model error analysis between the CHT model and the LCM. We provide detailed proofs and complement the analysis with numerical experiments.
Section

5
focuses on the Nusselt number. We develop a data-driven framework for estimating the average Nusselt number of general convex geometries by extending the applicability of empirical correlations through learned characteristic length scales, and demonstrate the approach on a family of spheroids. Finally, in
Section

6
, we summarize our results and offer perspectives on broader implications. Additional technical details on the numerical implementation and simulation studies are provided in the Appendices. Some proofs, previously derived in
[
21
]
and used in this work, are not repeated here.

2
Foundational Mathematical Models

We present three mathematical models that describe the physics of the dunking problem: the conjugate heat transfer (CHT) formulation, the autonomous Robin heat equation (RHE
a
), and the isothermal wall (ISO) formulation. The CHT model, previously introduced in
Section

1.1.1
, serves as the high-fidelity or “truth” model. The RHE
a
, discussed in
Section

1.1.2
, and the ISO model, which we now introduce, are simplifications of the full model.

Regarding notation, dimensional quantities are indicated with an underline, while non-dimensional quantities are left unadorned. Vector-valued variables are written in boldface. We also introduce the normalized integral

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{S}(\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.65973pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\bullet$}}}}})\coloneqq\frac{1}{|S|}\int_{S}(\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.65973pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\bullet$}}}}}),

where
|S|
denotes the measure of the set
S
.

2.1
Conjugate Heat Transfer Formulation (CHT)

The CHT model describes a coupled fluid-solid system, governed by the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations and advection-diffusion equation in the fluid, the heat equation in the solid, and interface conditions enforcing continuity in the heat flux and temperature. We make the following assumptions:

•

Effects from natural convection and radiation are negligible.

•

Pressure variations in the flow do not affect thermodynamic properties (density and temperature).

•

Fluid and solid properties do not vary substantially over the temperature range dictated by the far-field and the initial solid temperatures, and are therefore assumed constant with respect to temperature.

•

Viscous dissipation in the fluid is negligible.

•

The fluid is assumed incompressible, i.e., the velocity field is divergence-free.

•

The solid domain is stationary.

•

No heat sources are present.

2.1.1
Dimensional equations

Let
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
denote the fluid domain with boundary
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
and
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}
the solid domain with boundary
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}
. Both domains are separated by the fluid-solid interface
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\cap\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}
. The time
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
is defined from
[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}]
where
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
is the final time. The typical setup that we consider is shown schematically in
Fig.

2.1
. As we consider an external flow, the outer boundaries of the fluid domain are assumed to be sufficiently far away from the solid domain such that they do not affect the flow around the solid. The inflow boundary is denoted by
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{in}}
, where the fluid enters the domain with far-field velocity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
and far-field temperature
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
. The outflow boundaries are defined as
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{out}}\coloneqq\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\setminus(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{in}}\cup\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
.

Figure 2.1
:
Schematic of the fluid-solid system. The solid domain is denoted by
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}
and the fluid domain by
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
. The interface between the two domains is denoted by
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
.

The fluid has constant material properties: density
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
, kinematic viscosity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\nu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
, heat capacity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
, and thermal conductivity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
. The solid domain is heterogeneous, characterized by a spatially varying volumetric heat capacity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
and thermal conductivity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
. The fluid is moving in the far-field upstream with constant velocity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
in the
\bm{e}_{x}
direction. The initial temperatures of the fluid and solid are given by
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
and
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{i}}
.

The velocity and pressure fields in the fluid are governed by the incompressible Navier-Stokes equations, while the temperature fields in both fluid and solid are described by a coupled advection-diffusion equation (in the fluid) and heat conduction equation (in the solid). The incompressible Navier-Stokes equations are given by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}+(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mup\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}+\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\nu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\Delta}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}},

(2.1a)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\cdot\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}},

(2.1b)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}\bm{e}_{x}

\displaystyle\text{on }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{in}},

(2.1c)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{0}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu

\displaystyle\text{on }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,

(2.1d)

\displaystyle-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mup\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}\bm{n}+\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\nu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}+(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}})^{T})\cdot\bm{n}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{0}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu

\displaystyle\text{on }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{out}},

(2.1e)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}\bm{e}_{x}

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}.

(2.1f)

To ensure a unique solution for the pressure field, the pressure level is fixed by enforcing a zero-mean condition over the fluid domain at all times. The heat equations within the fluid and the solid are respectively given by the following advection-diffusion and heat equations:

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\left(\frac{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}+\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\Delta}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}},

(2.2a)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\frac{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\cdot(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}})

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}},

(2.2b)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}

\displaystyle\text{on }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{in}},

(2.2c)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle\text{on }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,

(2.2d)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}

\displaystyle\text{on }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,

(2.2e)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}},

(2.2f)

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu0\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{i}}

\displaystyle\text{in }\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}.

(2.2g)

The unit normal vector
\bm{n}
on
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
is defined to point from the solid domain into the fluid domain. The Navier-Stokes equations in
Eq.

2.1
do not depend on the temperature and can be solved independently for the velocity and pressure field. After inserting the velocity field into
Eq.

2.2a
,
Eq.

2.2
can be solved for the temperatures in fluid and solid. The quantity of interest (QoI) is the average temperature in the solid domain, defined as

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\int_{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)}\int_{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu).

The weighting factor
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
acts as a thermal mass density and
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
is thus related to the thermal energy in the solid.

2.1.2
Non-dimensional equations

The dimensional formulation involves nine input parameters:

•

Fluid properties: density
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
, kinematic viscosity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\nu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
, specific heat
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
, thermal conductivity
