\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
,

•

Solid properties: volumetric specific heat
(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}})(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
, thermal conductivity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
,

•

Flow and thermal conditions: far-field velocity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
and temperature
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
, initial solid temperature
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{i}}
.

To reduce this number and to generalize the solution across a wider class of problems, it is common practice to introduce a non-dimensional formulation. We first define two characteristic length scales associated with the solid domain:

•

Intrinsic length scale:
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{L}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}|/|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}|
, where
|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}|
and
|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}|
denote the volume and surface area of the solid domain, respectively.

•

Extrinsic length scale:
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, which can be chosen arbitrarily. Common choices include the diameter, or volume-equivalent sphere diameter.

We then define the solid diffusive time scale with

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\tau\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{diff}}\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}})_{\mathrm{avg}}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}}},

(2.3)

and

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}}\coloneqq\essinf_{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}},\quad(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}})_{\mathrm{avg}}\coloneqq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}.

Subsequently, we write the non-dimensional time and space variables as

\displaystyle\bm{x}\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu},\quad\bm{\nabla}\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{-1}},\quad t\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\tau\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{diff}}},\quad{t}_{\mathrm{f}}\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\tau\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{diff}}},

as well as the non-dimensional domains and boundaries

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}\coloneqq\left\{\bm{x}\ \middle|\ \bm{x}=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu},\ \mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\right\},\quad\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}\coloneqq\left\{\bm{x}\ \middle|\ \bm{x}=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu},\ \mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\right\},\quad\Gamma\coloneqq\left\{\bm{x}\ \middle|\ \bm{x}=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu},\ \mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\right\},

\displaystyle\partial\Omega_{\mathrm{in}}\coloneqq\left\{\bm{x}\ \middle|\ \bm{x}=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu},\ \mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{in}}\right\},\quad\partial\Omega_{\mathrm{out}}\coloneqq\left\{\bm{x}\ \middle|\ \bm{x}=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu},\ \mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{out}}\right\}.

Moreover, we define the non-dimensional solid material properties

\displaystyle\kappa(\bm{x})\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}}},\quad\sigma(\bm{x})\coloneqq\frac{(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}})(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)}{(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}})_{\mathrm{avg}}},

(2.4)

which fulfill

\displaystyle\inf_{\bm{x}\in\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}}\kappa(\bm{x})=1,\quad\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}}\sigma(\bm{x})=1;

(2.5)

the fluid-to-solid thermophysical property ratios

\displaystyle r_{1}\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}{(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}})_{\mathrm{avg}}},\quad r_{2}\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}}};

(2.6)

and the Reynolds and Prandtl numbers

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\nu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}},\quad\operatorname{Pr}\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\nu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}/(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}})}.

(2.7)

Note that the Prandtl number is a material parameter of the fluid, while the Reynolds number depends on the chosen length scale.

Finally, with the non-dimensional velocity, pressure, and temperature fields defined as

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{v}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}}

\displaystyle,\quad p^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mup\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\nu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu},

(2.8)

\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{i}}-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}}

\displaystyle,\quad u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{i}}-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}},

(2.9)

we can write the non-dimensional incompressible Navier-Stokes equations,

\displaystyle r_{1}(r_{2})^{-1}\frac{\partial\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial t}+\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{Pr}(\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{\nabla})\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=-\operatorname{Pr}\bm{\nabla}p^{\mathrm{CHT}}+\operatorname{Pr}\bm{\Delta}\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{f}},

(2.10a)

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}\cdot\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{f}},

(2.10b)

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=\bm{e}_{x}

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\Omega_{\mathrm{in}},

(2.10c)

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=\bm{0}

\displaystyle\text{on }\Gamma,

(2.10d)

\displaystyle-p^{\mathrm{CHT}}\bm{n}+(\bm{\nabla}\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}+(\bm{\nabla}\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}})^{T})\cdot\bm{n}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\Omega_{\mathrm{out}},

(2.10e)

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t=0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=\bm{e}_{x}

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{f}},

(2.10f)

and the non-dimensional advection-diffusion equation and heat equation,

\displaystyle r_{1}\frac{\partial u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial t}+\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{Pr}r_{2}\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=r_{2}\bm{\Delta}u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{f}},

(2.11a)

\displaystyle\sigma\frac{\partial u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\bm{\nabla}\cdot(\kappa\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}})

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{s}},

(2.11b)

\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\Omega_{\mathrm{in}},

(2.11c)

\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle\text{on }\Gamma,

(2.11d)

\displaystyle r_{2}\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}

\displaystyle=\kappa\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}

\displaystyle\text{on }\Gamma,

(2.11e)

\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t=0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{f}},

(2.11f)

\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t=0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\text{on }\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}.

(2.11g)

The QoI is the non-dimensional weighted average temperature in the solid domain, defined as

\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}}\sigma(\bm{x})}\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}}\sigma(\bm{x}){u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}(t,\bm{x})=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}}\sigma(\bm{x}){u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}(t,\bm{x}),

(2.12)

where we used
Eq.

2.5
in the last equality.

The non-dimensional formulation has six parameters:

•

Two dimensionless groups characterizing the flow:
\operatorname{Re}
and
\operatorname{Pr}
,

•

Two fluid-to-solid thermophysical property ratios:
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
,

•

Two non-dimensional solid properties:
\kappa(\bm{x})
and
\sigma(\bm{x})
.

2.1.3
Heat transfer coefficient, Nusselt number, and Biot number

We can associate to our CHT solution a
heat transfer coefficient
to characterize the heat exchange between fluid and solid. It is defined on the interface
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
as the heat flux normalized by the difference in far-field temperature and interface temperature,

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\coloneqq-\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\nabla}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}}.

(2.13)

The larger the heat transfer coefficient, the more heat is transferred, per unit temperature difference, between fluid and solid. Inserting the non-dimensional temperature field, we can rewrite the heat transfer coefficient in
Eq.

2.13
as

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)=-\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}\frac{\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}}{u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}},

and define its non-dimensional counterpart, the
Nusselt number
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}=-\frac{\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}}{u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}},

(2.14)

which is of central importance and generally depends on all six non-dimensional parameters, i.e.,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})=\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x};\operatorname{Re},\operatorname{Pr},r_{1},r_{2},\kappa(\bm{x}),\sigma(\bm{x})).

For many engineering applications, the space-time averaged heat transfer coefficient or Nusselt number is of interest,

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Gamma\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{0}^{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muh\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu),

(2.15)

\displaystyle\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{0}^{{t}_{\mathrm{f}}}\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{0}^{{t}_{\mathrm{f}}}\left(-\frac{\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}}{u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}\right).

(2.16)

We further define the spatial and temporal average of the Nusselt number with,

\displaystyle\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{0}^{{t}_{\mathrm{f}}}\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nu}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma}\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x}).

Another important engineering quantity is the non-dimensional
Biot number
, which is a constant scalar and closely related to the space-time averaged Nusselt number, defined as

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}}=r_{2}\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}.

(2.17)

Of particular interest is the limit of small Biot number, which has several implications for the heat transfer behavior of the solid. This will be discussed in more detail in
Section

2.2
. Lastly, we can define the
variation function

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})\coloneqq\frac{\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})}{\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}},

(2.18)

which is a measure for the variation of the heat transfer on the interface, with

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{0}^{{t}_{\mathrm{f}}}\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}=1,

(2.19)

and
\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}=1
if
\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
does not depend on space and time; we also define the temporal average as

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\bm{x})\coloneqq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{0}^{{t}_{\mathrm{f}}}\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x}).

Note, that the variation function does not depend on the choice of length scale
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
.

2.1.4
Example: Cross-flow around a circular cylinder (CHT)

We conclude this section with a numerical example: a cross-flow over a circular cylinder of diameter
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
in two dimensions with geometry shown in Figure
2.2
. This example has already been briefly discussed in
Section

1.1.2
.
We choose the diameter of the cylinder as extrinsic length scale, i.e.,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, and solve the non-dimensional system. The parameters considered are given in
Table

2.1
, and the properties of the solid are assumed to be uniform, i.e.,
\sigma(\bm{x})=\kappa(\bm{x})=1
for all
\bm{x}\in\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}
. The chosen values of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
do not correspond to any specific fluid-solid pair; they are selected so that the CHT problem exhibits the qualitative behavior typical of common fluid-solid heat-transfer problems. The Prandtl number corresponds to air at standard conditions. We compute the CHT solution with the spectral element solver Nek5000
[
11
]
, using 7th-order polynomials for spatial discretization and BDF3 for time stepping until
{t}_{\mathrm{f}}=5.0
. We use an adaptive time step that maintains a CFL condition of around 0.4. Verification studies for the numerical results are provided in Appendix
C.1
.

Figure 2.2
:
Cross-flow over a circular cylinder of diameter
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
in two dimensions (not to scale). The flow enters the domain from the left, as illustrated in
Fig.

2.1
.

Table 2.1
:
Flow and material parameters used in the CHT example.

\operatorname{Pr}

\operatorname{Re}

r_{1}

r_{2}

0.71

143

0.0132

0.01085

A few snapshots of the temperature field around the cylinder are shown in
Fig.

2.3
. After initial boundary layer formation, the wake becomes instable and vortices start shedding. To analyze the results, three spatial locations on the cylinder surface
\Gamma
are defined, see
Fig.

3(a)
: leading edge (LE), trailing edge (TE), and side edge (SE). The leading edge is the point where the flow first impinges on the cylinder, the trailing edge is the point where the flow leaves the cylinder, and the side edge is the point on the cylinder surface that is perpendicular to the flow direction.

(a)

t=0.013

(b)

t=0.2

(c)

t=1.5

(d)

t=2.5

Figure 2.3
:
Temperature fields from the CHT solution at different time steps.

The Nusselt numbers at LE, TE, SE are plotted over time in
Fig.

4(a)
, together with the spatially averaged Nusselt number
\operatorname{Nu}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
. We can make several observations:

•

Temporal behavior:
The Nusselt number initially drops sharply for
t\leq 0.1
, falling from essentially infinity to a finite value. This initial phase is dominated by pure conduction before boundary layers have developed and will be discussed in more detail in
Section

4.2.1
. For
0.1\leq t\leq 1.5
, thermal and velocity boundary layers develop, and a wake begins to form behind the solid. The Nusselt number stabilizes at around
t\approx 0.3
. Beyond
t\geq 1.5
, the wake becomes unstable and vortices start to shed, leading to an oscillatory Nusselt number, and a transition in the Nusselt number. However, despite the transition and the fluctuations, the magnitude of the spatially averaged Nusselt number,
\operatorname{Nu}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
, remains nearly unchanged after
t\approx 0.3
.

•

Spatial behavior:
The Nusselt number is highest at the leading edge (LE), where the flow first contacts the cylinder. The values at the side edge (SE) and trailing edge (TE) are significantly lower and exhibit fluctuations due to the alternating nature of vortex shedding.

Fig.

4(b)
shows the Nusselt number as a function of the angular coordinate
\theta\in[-\pi,\pi)
, measured around the cylinder starting from the trailing edge (TE), at selected time steps. Initially, the Nusselt number is nearly uniform along the surface. As time progresses, spatial variations emerge due to boundary layer development. The temporally averaged Nusselt number
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
over the surface is also shown, highlighting the high spatial variation of the Nusselt number. The same data are shown as a polar plot in
Fig.

2.5
, which clearly highlights the spatial non-uniformity around the cylinder surface.

(a)

(b)

Figure 2.4
:
(a) Nusselt number at LE, TE, SE and spatial average over time. (b) Nusselt number measured in angular coordinate
\theta
at different time steps and temporal average. The superscripts “CHT” are omitted for brevity.

:
Nusselt number over time, and temporal average, presented as a polar plot.
