Figure 2.6
:
(a) Average temperature over time with CHT and exponential fit. (b) Coefficient of variation of the temperature over time.

The average temperature,
u_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t)
, is shown in
Fig.

6(a)
. Despite the unsteady and complex nature of the flow, the temperature decreases smoothly and monotonically over time. Its temporal evolution closely resembles an exponential decay. For interpretation, we can define an approximate solution using

\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{fit}}(t)\coloneqq\exp\left(-\frac{t}{\tau_{\mathrm{eq}}}\right),

(2.20)

where
\tau_{\mathrm{eq}}
represents the equilibration time constant, defined implicitly by
u_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\tau_{\mathrm{eq}})=\exp(-1)
. For the present case, we obtain
\tau_{\mathrm{eq}}=3.77
. The corresponding exponential approximation from
Eq.

2.20
is also shown in
Fig.

6(a)
, and it closely matches the CHT QoI.

During the time required for the Nusselt number to develop and stabilize (up to
t\approx 0.3
), the average temperature remains nearly unchanged. This reveals a
time scale separation
between the fluid and solid dynamics. The fluid is characterized by a convective time scale, defined in dimensional terms as
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\tau\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{conv}}=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
, which under our non-dimensionalization (see
Eq.

2.3
) becomes

\displaystyle\tau_{\mathrm{conv}}\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\tau\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{conv}}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\tau\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{diff}}}=r_{1}/(r_{2}\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{Pr}).

(2.21)

For the parameters considered, we obtain
\tau_{\mathrm{conv}}=0.012
. In comparison, the equilibration time scale
\tau_{\mathrm{eq}}
is about 300 times larger, confirming a pronounced separation of fluid and solid time scales. More details on this will be provided in
Section

4.2
.

It is an interesting fact that the Nusselt number becomes stationary even though the temperature field itself is clearly time-dependent. The Nusselt number was defined in
Eq.

2.14
as the heat flux at the interface normalized by the temperature difference between the solid surface and the far-field fluid. While the temperature field
u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
continues to evolve over time (affecting the denominator), the normal heat flux
\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}
(the numerator) varies in a nearly proportional manner. As a result, the ratio remains approximately constant after the initial transient phase, leading to an effectively stationary Nusselt number.

(a)

t=0.013

(b)

t=0.2

(c)

t=1.5

(d)

t=2.5

Figure 2.7
:
Temperature fields normalized by mean value at different time steps.

To study the spatial uniformity of the temperature field within the solid, we define the coefficient of variation (CV) as

\displaystyle\text{CV}(t)\coloneqq\frac{\sqrt{\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}}(u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})-u_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t))^{2}}}{u_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t)}.

Its value is plotted in
Fig.

6(b)
. We can see that the coefficient increases as the boundary layer develops, but remains relatively low for all times (below 0.015), indicating a fairly uniform temperature distribution within the solid. We can see a small peak around
t=1.75
, which corresponds to the onset of vortex shedding. We also visualize the temperature field normalized by its mean value at a few time steps in
Fig.

2.7
. We see that all values remain reasonably close to 1 (between 0.97 and 1.01), indicating only slight variations from the mean.

We can also quantify the uniformity of the temperature field by considering the ratio of the equilibration time scale to the diffusive time scale (equal to
1
in our normalization). In this case,
\tau_{\mathrm{eq}}/\tau_{\mathrm{diff}}=3.77
, indicating that the average temperature of the solid decays much more slowly than diffusion acts within it. As a result, internal gradients are rapidly smoothed out, and the temperature field remains essentially uniform.

Lastly, we evaluate the Biot number. We find
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}=6.27
and
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}=r_{2}\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}=0.0680
, which is reasonably small. As will be discussed in
Section

2.2
, a
small Biot number
implies a roughly uniform temperature distribution within the solid, which is consistent with our observations.

Comment 1
.

The periodic behavior of the Nusselt number observed in
Fig.

4(a)
is a consequence of the highly idealized setup: a cross-flow over a two-dimensional circular cylinder. In three-dimensional configurations, the Nusselt number generally exhibits random and chaotic fluctuations for higher Reynolds numbers. Nevertheless, its spatially averaged value remains relatively stable over time. Three-dimensional illustrative examples are provided in
Section

5
.

2.2
Autonomous Robin Heat Equation (RHE
a
)

Due to the high computational cost of solving the full CHT model and the extensive data requirements, particularly the spatial distributions of heterogeneous material properties, simplified formulations are often necessary. One such model is the
autonomous Robin heat equation
(RHE
a
), which assumes a time-independent heat transfer coefficient—or equivalently, a time-independent Nusselt number—hence the term “autonomous”. In this section, we first show that the CHT model can be equivalently recast as a Robin boundary value problem posed solely on the solid domain. This equivalence forms the foundation for both the subsequent model simplifications and the error analysis presented in
Section

4
. We then simplify the problem to obtain the RHE
a
, derive its weak formulation, and revisit the numerical example from the previous section.

2.2.1
Robin heat equation

To motivate a Robin heat equation for the solid, we begin by rearranging the boundary condition
Eq.

2.11e
,

\displaystyle\kappa\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}-r_{2}\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{on }\Gamma.

(2.22)

We focus on the left-hand side and rewrite it step by step:

\displaystyle\kappa\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}-r_{2}\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}

\displaystyle=\kappa\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}-r_{2}\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}\frac{u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}

\displaystyle=\kappa\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}+r_{2}\underbrace{\left(-\frac{\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}}{u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}\right)}_{=\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=\kappa\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}+r_{2}\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}},

where we used
Eq.

2.11d
and the definition of the local Nusselt number in
Eq.

2.14
.

Using now
Eqs.

2.16
,
2.17
and
2.18
, we can express the the result in terms of the Biot number and the variation function:

\displaystyle\kappa\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}+r_{2}\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=\kappa\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}+r_{2}\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\frac{\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}

\displaystyle=\kappa\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}+\underbrace{r_{2}\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}_{=B^{\mathrm{CHT}}}\underbrace{\frac{\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}}_{=\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}

\displaystyle=\kappa\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}+B^{\mathrm{CHT}}\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}.

Combining this expression with
Eq.

2.22
, we finally obtain

\displaystyle\kappa\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{n}+B^{\mathrm{CHT}}\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}=0\quad\text{on }\Gamma.

(2.23)

Eq.

2.23
represents a Robin boundary condition on the interface
\Gamma
with spatially and temporally varying Robin coefficient
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})
. Along with
Eqs.

2.11b
and
2.11g
, we can write the following boundary value problem for the solid domain only:

\displaystyle\sigma\frac{\partial u_{\mathrm{s}}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\bm{\nabla}\cdot(\kappa\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{s}})

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{s}},

(2.24a)

\displaystyle\kappa\bm{\nabla}u_{\mathrm{s}}\cdot\bm{n}+B^{\mathrm{CHT}}\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})u_{\mathrm{s}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\Gamma,

(2.24b)

\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{s}}(t=0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}.

(2.24c)

The solution to
Eq.

2.24
is equivalent to the temperature solution of the CHT model restricted to the solid domain, i.e.,
u_{\mathrm{s}}\equiv u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
. The effect of the fluid is fully captured by the Biot number
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
and the variation function
\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})
.

We can now pose the abstract non-dimensional
Robin heat equation
(RHE) for the solid:

\displaystyle\sigma\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\bm{\nabla}\cdot(\kappa\bm{\nabla}u)

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

(2.25a)

\displaystyle\kappa\bm{\nabla}u\cdot\bm{n}+B\eta(t,\bm{x})u

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\Omega,

(2.25b)

\displaystyle u(t=0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega.

(2.25c)

As the formulation no longer involves a fluid domain, we do not use any subscripts, and we use
\partial\Omega
to denote the fluid-solid interface. The effect of the fluid is modeled through the Biot number
B
and the variation function
\eta(t,\bm{x})
, which are now inputs to the RHE, and can be chosen arbitrarily. As before, we choose the weighted average temperature as QoI,

\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{avg}}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Omega}\sigma u(t,\bm{x}).

(2.26)

Comparing
Eq.

2.25
with
Eq.

2.24
, we see that their solutions become identical for
B=B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
and
\eta=\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}
, i.e.,
u\equiv u_{\mathrm{s}}\equiv u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
. Consequently, the average temperatures are also identical,
u_{\mathrm{avg}}\equiv u_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
.

Looking at the Robin boundary condition in
Eq.

2.25b
, we can now understand the
small-Biot
number limit: as
B\to 0
, the Robin boundary condition reduces to a homogeneous Neumann condition (
\kappa\bm{\nabla}u\cdot\bm{n}=0
), corresponding to vanishing interfacial heat flux. Consequently, temperature gradients are small, and the field is nearly uniform.

2.2.2
Time scale considerations and simplification

In the example presented in
Section

2.1.4
, we observed a
time scale separation
between fluid and solid: while the Nusselt number was essentially fully developed—in the sense that its temporal variations had become small—the solid temperature had not changed significantly. It was also observed that the Nusselt number was spatially non-uniform, see
Fig.

2.5
. This motivates the definition of the
autonomous Robin heat equation
(RHE
a
), which assumes a time-independent but spatially varying Robin coefficient:

\displaystyle\sigma\frac{\partial\widetilde{u}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\bm{\nabla}\cdot(\kappa\bm{\nabla}\widetilde{u})

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

(2.27a)

\displaystyle\kappa\bm{\nabla}\widetilde{u}\cdot\bm{n}+B\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})\widetilde{u}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\Omega,

(2.27b)

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}(t=0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

(2.27c)

where
\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})
is now a time-independent variation function, and the QoI is defined as

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Omega}\sigma\widetilde{u}(t,\bm{x}).

As there is no longer an equivalence between CHT and RHE
a
, we adorn the temperature field with a tilde to distinguish it from the RHE and CHT solutions, and to emphasize that it is an approximation to the true temperature field (even if
B=B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
).

If the true
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
and
\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})
(and, hence,
\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
) were known, the natural choice would be to take
B=B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
and
\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})
as the time average of the true variation function, i.e.,

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})=\overline{\eta}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(\bm{x})=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{0}^{{t}_{\mathrm{f}}}\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x}),\quad\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})=1.

(2.28)

This way, a good approximation of the true Nusselt number,
\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
, is typically achieved, i.e.,
B\overline{\eta}\approx B^{\mathrm{CHT}}\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}=r_{2}\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
. This is illustrated in
Fig.

2.8
, where we replot
Fig.

4(a)
(cross-flow over a cylinder in 2D) with the temporal average at each point shown as dashed lines. Notice the stiff behavior during short times: in
Section

4.2.1
, we provide an analysis on its effect on the average temperature. The temporal averaging can be interpreted as a time homogenization procedure that averages over fast fluctuations. In Appendix
D
, we demonstrate for a model Robin problem—no initial transient, and a sinusoidal Robin coefficient in time and independent of space—that the optimal approximation is, not unexpectedly, given by the time average of the Robin coefficient.

Figure 2.8
:
A replot of
Fig.

4(a)
, but now with temporal averages in dashed lines.

The autonomous formulation offers several advantages, including a corresponding time-independent eigenvalue problem, presented and utilized for the derivation of error estimates in
Section

4.3.1
, and a more efficient numerical solution. Compared to the full RHE, the RHE
a
is more practical, as it requires only the temporally averaged Nusselt number
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}(\bm{x})
(from which
B
and
\overline{\eta}
are derived; see
Eqs.

2.17
,
2.18
and
2.28
), which is easier to estimate than
\operatorname{Nu}(t,\bm{x})
. However, even
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}(\bm{x})
remains challenging to determine in practice due to its spatial variation, and the thermophysical properties
\kappa
and
\sigma
are often unknown without destructive testing.

2.2.3
Weak formulation

We next formulate the weak form of the RHE
a
, which serves both as the basis (i) for theoretical purposes, and for rigorous treatment of heterogeneous materials, and (ii) for the foundation for finite element spatial discretization.

The weak formulation of
Eq.

2.27
is: find
\widetilde{u}(t)\in H^{1}(\Omega)
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\sigma\,\frac{\partial\widetilde{u}}{\partial t}\,v+\int_{\Omega}\kappa\,\bm{\nabla}\widetilde{u}\cdot\bm{\nabla}v+B\int_{\partial\Omega}\overline{\eta}\,\widetilde{u}\,v=0,\quad\forall v\in H^{1}(\Omega),

subject to initial condition
\widetilde{u}(t=0,\cdot)=1
. It is assumed that
{t}_{\mathrm{f}},B>0
, and

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\quad\sigma(\bm{x})\geq\sigma_{\min}>0\text{ for }\bm{x}\in\Omega,

\displaystyle\quad\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Omega}\sigma=1;

(2.29)

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle\in L^{\infty}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\quad\essinf_{\bm{x}\in\Omega}\kappa(\bm{x})=1;

(2.30)

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}

\displaystyle\in L^{r}(\partial\Omega),\;r\geq 1,

\displaystyle\quad\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})\geq 0\text{ for }\bm{x}\in\partial\Omega,

\displaystyle\quad\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})=1.

(2.31)

For
w,v\in H^{1}(\Omega)
, we can introduce the following bilinear forms:

\displaystyle a_{0}(w,v;\kappa)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}\kappa\,\bm{\nabla}w\cdot\bm{\nabla}v,

(2.32)

\displaystyle a_{1}(w,v;\overline{\eta})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\partial\Omega}\overline{\eta}\,w\,v,

(2.33)

\displaystyle a(w,v;\kappa,B,\overline{\eta})

\displaystyle\coloneqq a_{0}(w,v;\kappa)+Ba_{1}(w,v;\overline{\eta}),

(2.34)

\displaystyle m(w,v;\sigma)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}\sigma\,w\,v.

(2.35)

The weak form can then be restated as: given the problem data
\{\Omega,{t}_{\mathrm{f}},\kappa,\sigma,B,\overline{\eta}\}
, find
\widetilde{u}(\cdot,t)\in H^{1}(\Omega)
for
t\in(0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]
such that

\displaystyle m\left(\frac{\partial\widetilde{u}}{\partial t},v;\sigma\right)+a(\widetilde{u},v;\kappa,B,\overline{\eta})

\displaystyle=0,\quad\forall v\in H^{1}(\Omega),

(2.36)

subject to initial condition
\widetilde{u}(t=0,\cdot)=1
. The QoI can be expressed as

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t)

\displaystyle=m(\widetilde{u}(\cdot,t),1;\sigma).

(2.37)

2.2.4
Example: Cross-flow around a circular cylinder (
\mathrm{RHE}_{a}
)

We revisit the example introduced in
Section

2.1.4
, and solve it with RHE
a
. The Biot number
B=0.068
and the temporally averaged variation function
\overline{\eta}
are extracted from the CHT solution, with
\overline{\eta}
shown in
Fig.

9(a)
. The simulation is performed using DOLFINx
[
5
]
, with a BDF2 time-stepping scheme, a fixed timestep of
\Delta t=0.0025
, and a total of 2000 timesteps. The resulting average temperature obtained from the RHE
a
model is shown in
Fig.

9(b)
, alongside the CHT result. The two curves show excellent agreement, confirming the time scale separation.

(a)

Time-averaged variation

(b)

Average temperature

Figure 2.9
:
(a) Time-averaged variation
\overline{\eta}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
computed with CHT. (b) Average temperature over time with CHT and RHE
a
.

2.3
Isothermal Wall (ISO) Formulation

To accurately approximate the CHT result with the RHE
a
, an accurate estimate of the
B
and
\overline{\eta}
is crucial. One way to obtain these quantities is through the isothermal wall (ISO) formulation. While the RHE
a
model represents a solid-side reduction of the CHT problem, the ISO formulation can be viewed as its fluid-side counterpart: it models the solid as an isothermal boundary and solves only the fluid equations.

This approximation is valid only when there is a clear
time scale separation
between fluid and solid dynamics, and the
Biot number is small
. In the small-Biot limit, the Robin boundary condition in
Eq.

2.23
approaches a Neumann condition, implying that the temperature within the solid remains nearly uniform. This uniformity was empirically demonstrated in
Section

2.1.4
, particularly in
Fig.

6(b)
, where the coefficient of variation remained below 1.5% throughout the simulation. In the same example, the solid temperature evolved much more slowly than the fluid field. This motivates the assumption of a uniform and quasi-stationary solid temperature adopted in the ISO formulation.

The ISO formulation remains computationally expensive, as it still requires solving the Navier-Stokes equations. However, ISO avoids the disparate time scales present in CHT. Moreover, ISO serves as the foundation for subsequent approximations of the Nusselt number—such as empirical correlations, discussed later in
Section

5
—which are considerably less expensive to evaluate.

2.3.1
Governing equations

In the ISO formulation, the solid is no longer explicitly modeled but represented as an isothermal boundary condition for the fluid. The incompressible Navier-Stokes equations in
Eq.

2.10
remain unchanged and are used to compute the velocity and pressure fields in the fluid. The thermal problem, however, is simplified: an advection-diffusion equation is solved only in the fluid domain, subject to an isothermal boundary condition representing the solid. The governing equations for the temperature field are given in non-dimensional form below:

\displaystyle r_{1}\frac{\partial u^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}}{\partial t}+\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{Pr}r_{2}\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{\nabla}u^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}

\displaystyle=r_{2}\bm{\Delta}u^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{f}},

(2.38a)

\displaystyle u^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\Omega_{\mathrm{in}},

(2.38b)

\displaystyle u^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle\text{on }\Gamma,

(2.38c)

\displaystyle u^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}(t=0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}.

(2.38d)

We retain the same non-dimensionalization as in the CHT formulation to enable direct comparison. The resulting equations involve the parameters
\operatorname{Re}
,
\operatorname{Pr}
,
r_{1}
, and
r_{2}
, while the solid-specific parameters
\kappa
and
\sigma
no longer appear. Since the solid domain is entirely absent in the ISO formulation,
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
act purely as scaling factors inherited from the original non-dimensionalization. If the equations were non-dimensionalized based on fluid properties, i.e., by redefining the diffusive time scale in
Eq.

2.3
with
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
and
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}
, both
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
would equal one and disappear from the formulation. To make this clear, we define
\hat{t}=t\,r_{2}/r_{1}
and
\hat{u}^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}(\hat{t},\cdot)=u^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}(t,\cdot)
and insert into
Eq.

2.38a
to obtain

\displaystyle r_{1}\frac{\partial\hat{u}^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}}{\partial\hat{t}}\frac{\partial\hat{t}}{\partial t}+\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{Pr}r_{2}\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{\nabla}\hat{u}^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}=r_{2}\bm{\Delta}\hat{u}^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}\quad\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}.

As
\partial\hat{t}/\partial t=r_{2}/r_{1}
, the
r_{1}
cancels out and we can divide through by
r_{2}
to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\hat{u}^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}}{\partial\hat{t}}+\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{Pr}\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\cdot\bm{\nabla}\hat{u}^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}=\bm{\Delta}\hat{u}^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}\quad\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{f}}.

2.3.2
Nusselt number and Biot number

Given a geometry, Reynolds and Prandtl numbers, the ISO problem in
Eq.

2.38
can be solved to compute an approximation of the true Nusselt number, Biot number, and variation function. Analogously to the CHT formulation, these quantities can be defined on the interface
\Gamma
within the ISO framework as follows:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{ISO}}(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\frac{\bm{\nabla}u^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}\cdot\bm{n}}{u^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}},

(2.39)

\displaystyle\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{0}^{{t}_{\mathrm{f}}}\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{ISO}}(t,\bm{x}),

(2.40)

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{ISO}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq r_{2}\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}},

(2.41)

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathrm{ISO}}(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{ISO}}(t,\bm{x})}{\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}},

(2.42)

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}^{\mathrm{ISO}}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{0}^{{t}_{\mathrm{f}}}\eta^{\mathrm{ISO}}(t,\bm{x}).

(2.43)

As there is no solid domain in the ISO formulation, the Nusselt number (and its derived quantities) depend only on the Reynolds and Prandtl numbers, i.e.,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{ISO}}(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{ISO}}(t,\bm{x};\operatorname{Re},\operatorname{Pr}).

The fluid-to-solid thermophysical ratios
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
and the solid properties
\kappa
and
\sigma
have no influence (the final time
{t}_{\mathrm{f}}
needs to be adapted accordingly with
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
).
For small
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, the ISO formulation provides a satisfactory approximation of the CHT problem, i.e.,
\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{ISO}}\approx\operatorname{Nu}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
, as has been empirically observed in many engineering applications. This will be examined in more detail in
Section

5.4.1
.

2.3.3
Triangular system

The ISO model provides a practical way to approximate the Nusselt number for use in the RHE
a
model, which can then be solved to estimate the solid temperature field. The procedure is as follows:

1.

Given a geometry, Reynolds and Prandtl numbers, solve the ISO problem in
Eq.

2.38
for
u^{\mathrm{ISO}}_{\mathrm{f}}
.

2.

With
r_{2}
, compute
B^{\mathrm{ISO}}
and
\overline{\eta}^{\mathrm{ISO}}
using
Eqs.

2.39
,
2.40
,
2.41
,
2.42
and
2.43
.

3.

Set
B=B^{\mathrm{ISO}}
and
\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})=\overline{\eta}^{\mathrm{ISO}}(\bm{x})
.

4.

With
\kappa
and
\sigma
, solve the RHE
a
problem in
Eq.

2.36
to obtain the solid temperature field
\widetilde{u}(t,\bm{x})
and compute the average temperature
\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t)
with
Eq.

2.37
.

We refer to this approach as the
triangular system
, as it involves solving two decoupled problems in sequence: first ISO and then RHE
a
. The triangular system is expected to be accurate when the assumptions of
small-Biot
and
time scale separation
hold.

Comment 2
.

Instead of solving RHE
a
, one could also consider solving the full RHE with a time-dependent Robin coefficient
B^{\mathrm{ISO}}\eta^{\mathrm{ISO}}(t,\bm{x})
. However, the gain in accuracy would be marginal, since time scale separation is assumed, implying
\eta^{\mathrm{ISO}}(t,\bm{x})\approx\overline{\eta}^{\mathrm{ISO}}(\bm{x})
. If the underlying CHT problem does not exhibit such separation, the triangular system cannot be expected to yield accurate results even with
\eta^{\mathrm{ISO}}(t,\bm{x})
. Moreover, the computational cost would be significantly higher, as a time-dependent Robin coefficient leads to a varying left-hand side that must be reassembled at each timestep.

2.3.4
Example: Cross-flow around a circular cylinder (ISO)

We apply the triangular system to the cross-flow around a circular cylinder example introduced in
Section

2.1.4
. The ISO model is solved using Nek5000
[
11
]
with the same parameters and numerical settings as for the CHT case in
Section

2.1.4
. The spatially averaged Nusselt number obtained with ISO is shown together with the CHT result in
Fig.

10(a)
. In addition,
\overline{\eta}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
is plotted as a function of the angular coordinate
\theta
(defined in
Section

2.1.4
) in
Fig.

10(b)
, alongside the CHT reference. Both plots demonstrate that, although the ISO model exhibits slight deviations, it provides an excellent approximation of the true Nusselt number for this case. For the Nusselt number, we obtain
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}=6.25
, in close agreement with the CHT value
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}=6.27
. For the Biot number, we find
B^{\mathrm{ISO}}=0.0678
, which is likewise very close to the CHT value
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}=0.0680
.
Subsequently, the values of
B^{\mathrm{ISO}}
and
\overline{\eta}^{\mathrm{ISO}}
are used as inputs to the RHE
a
model, which is solved with DOLFINx
[
5
]
under the same settings as in
Section

2.2.4
. The resulting average temperature, shown in
Fig.

2.11
alongside the CHT reference, exhibits excellent agreement between the two curves.

(a)

(b)

Figure 2.10
:
(a) Spatial averaged Nusselt number computed with CHT and ISO over time. (b) Temporally averaged variation measured in angular coordinate
\theta
for CHT and ISO.

Figure 2.11
:
Average temperature computed with CHT and triangular system (ISO + RHE
a
).

2.4
Summary

In
Section

2
, several models were presented for the dunking problem. Below we summarize each of them in terms of their inputs and underlying assumptions.

Conjugate heat transfer (CHT) model:

The CHT model serves as the reference, or truth, model for the dunking problem. In its non-dimensional form, it involves six input parameters: the Reynolds number
\operatorname{Re}
, the Prandtl number
\operatorname{Pr}
, the fluid-to-solid volumetric heat capacity ratio
r_{1}
, the fluid-to-solid thermal conductivity ratio
r_{2}
, and the solid thermophysical properties
\kappa
and
\sigma
. While it is the most complete and accurate formulation, it is computationally expensive and requires detailed knowledge of the thermophysical properties of the solid, which may not be readily available.

Autonomous Robin heat equation (RHE
a
):

The RHE
a
model is a simplification of the CHT model, based on the assumption of
time scale separation
between fluid and solid dynamics. It requires four input parameters: the solid thermophysical properties
\kappa
and
\sigma
, the fluid-to-solid thermal conductivity ratio
r_{2}
, and a time-independent but spatially varying Nusselt number
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}
that encapsulates the fluid effects. The RHE
a
solution is much less expensive to compute than the CHT. However, like the CHT model, it still requires knowledge of
\kappa
and
\sigma
, and in addition the time-independent Nusselt number must be provided.

Isothermal wall (ISO) formulation:

The ISO model is another simplification of the CHT model that requires only two input parameters: the Reynolds number and the Prandtl number. The formulation rests on two assumptions: a
small-Biot
number, implying a nearly spatially uniform temperature field within the solid, and
time scale separation
between fluid and solid. For problems satisfying these assumptions, the ISO model offers a more efficient way to estimate the Nusselt number required by the RHE
a
model. However, as the formulation does not take into account the solid properties
\kappa
and
\sigma
nor the thermophysical property ratios
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, it is unclear for which property ranges the ISO-predicted Nusselt number accurately approximates the CHT value; this question is examined in
Section

5.4.1
. Owing to the relatively high computational cost of ISO simulations, practitioners often rely on
empirical correlations
—simplified approximations of the ISO-derived Nusselt number—which are discussed in detail in
Section

5
.

3
Lumped Capacitance Model (LCM)

The lumped capacitance model (LCM) is a simple and widely used approach for estimating the thermal response of a solid body immersed in a fluid. It can be derived from the RHE
a
model by assuming, in addition to time scale separation, an exactly spatially uniform temperature field. It is therefore only valid for problems in the small Biot limit. In this section, we derive the non-dimensional form of the LCM, present its dimensional counterpart, and revisit the example of cross-flow around a circular cylinder.

3.1
Non-Dimensional Form

To enforce the spatial uniformity of the temperature within the weak form in
Eq.

2.36
, we can select a test and trial space that contains only the constant value, i.e.,
X^{\text{L}}\coloneqq\operatorname{span}\{1_{\Omega}\}
. The problem becomes: find
\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t)\in X^{\text{L}}
such that

\displaystyle m\left(\frac{\partial\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}}{\partial t},v;\sigma\right)+a(\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}},v;\kappa,B,\overline{\eta})

\displaystyle=0,\quad\forall v\in X^{\text{L}}

with initial condition
\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(0)=1
, and the bilinear forms were defined in
Eqs.

2.35
,
2.34
,
2.32
and
2.33
. As
\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t)
and
v
do not depend on space, they can be pulled out of the space integrals, and we can directly integrate to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}}{dt}\int_{\Omega}\sigma+B\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}\int_{\partial\Omega}\overline{\eta}

\displaystyle=0.

With the properties of
\sigma
and
\overline{\eta}
in
Eqs.

2.29
and
2.31
, we find

\displaystyle\frac{d\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}}{dt}|\Omega|+B|\partial\Omega|\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}

\displaystyle=0.

We can then write the analytical solution of the ODE as

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t)=\exp\left(-B\gamma t\right),

(3.1)

where the geometric factor
\gamma
is defined as

\displaystyle\gamma\coloneqq\frac{|\partial\Omega|}{|\Omega|}.

(3.2)

We can also represent
\gamma
in terms of dimensional quantities as

\displaystyle\gamma=\frac{|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|}=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{L}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}.

(3.3)

where
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{L}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
and
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
are the intrinsic and extrinsic length scale, respectively. We can rewrite
Eq.

3.1
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t)=\exp\left(-\frac{t}{\tau^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}}\right),

(3.4)

where

\displaystyle\tau^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}=\frac{1}{B\gamma}

(3.5)

is the nondimensional equilibration time constant. It is clear from
Eqs.

3.4
and
3.5
that the LCM solution only requires knowledge of the body volume
|\Omega|
, the surface area
|\partial\Omega|
, and the Biot number
B
. As compared to the RHE
a
problem, the LCM does not require knowledge of the time-independent variation
\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})
nor the detailed geometry of the solid domain
\Omega
. Moreover, material inhomogeneities in the solid,
\kappa
and
\sigma
, do not have any effect on the solution.

3.2
Dimensional Form

The LCM solution in
Eq.

3.4
can be expressed in dimensional form as

\displaystyle\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\widetilde{T}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{L}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{i}}-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mut\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\tau\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}}\right),

(3.6)

where

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\tau\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}\coloneqq\frac{|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|}{|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|}\frac{(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)_{\mathrm{avg}}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}}

(3.7)

is the dimensional equilibration time constant. The dimensional solution in
Eqs.

3.6
and
3.7
requires only the volume
|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|
, surface area
|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|
, the volume-averaged heat capacity
(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)_{\mathrm{avg}}
, the averaged HTC
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}
, and the far-field and initial solid temperatures,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
and
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muT\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{i}}
. The volume, surface area and volume-averaged heat capacity, far-field and initial solid temperatures are typically known, while the heat transfer coefficient can be estimated, as discussed in
Section

5
.

3.3
Example Revisited: Cross-Flow Around a Circular Cylinder (LCM)

We revisit the cross-flow around a circular cylinder considered in
Section

2.1.4
. The problem was non-dimensionalized using
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
as the characteristic length. The geometric factor
\gamma
can be computed with
Eq.

3.3
,

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=\frac{|\partial\Omega|}{|\Omega|}=\frac{|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|}=\frac{\pi\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}}{\tfrac{1}{4}\pi\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}}=4.

(3.8)

Using the Biot number obtained with the ISO model in
Section

2.3.4
,
B^{\mathrm{ISO}}=0.0678
, we find a time constant

\displaystyle\tau^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}=\frac{1}{B^{\mathrm{ISO}}\gamma}=3.69,

which is in very close agreement with the exponential fit of the CHT solution in
Eq.

2.20
, where
\tau_{\mathrm{eq}}=3.77
was found.

It is remarkable that the LCM, which requires only the volume, surface area, and an estimated Biot number (here obtained via ISO), can approximate the full CHT solution—a coupled system of PDEs with six inputs—so closely. Its accuracy rests on two key assumptions: approximate spatial uniformity of the solid temperature, valid when the Biot number is small (here
B^{\mathrm{ISO}}=0.0678
), and separation of fluid and solid time scales.

However, these assumptions are often only qualitatively stated in engineering practice. For many fluid-solid combinations, it is unclear whether the Biot number is sufficiently small or whether a true time scale separation exists. In the next section, we examine these assumptions in detail and make them more precise through mathematical analysis and numerical simulations.

4
Model Error Estimation

We are interested in developing an error bound for the CHT-to-LCM error, given by

\displaystyle|u_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,r_{1},r_{2},\operatorname{Re},\operatorname{Pr})-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;\undertilde{B})|.

(4.1)

Here,
u_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
denotes the CHT QoI defined in
Eq.

2.12
, and
\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}
the LCM QoI defined in
Eq.

3.1
. The CHT model depends on six dimensionless input parameters: the nondimensional solid properties
\kappa
and
\sigma
from
Eq.

2.4
; the fluid-to-solid property ratios
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
from
Eq.

2.6
; and the Reynolds and Prandtl numbers
\operatorname{Re}
and
\operatorname{Pr}
from
Eq.

2.7
. The LCM model depends only on the Biot number. Since the true Biot number
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
is typically unknown in practice, we introduce an approximate value
\undertilde{B}
, representing an estimate of
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
(e.g., obtained via the ISO model or empirical correlations).

In
Section

2.2
, it was shown that the CHT problem can be equivalently reformulated as a RHE problem, and that the corresponding QoIs are identical:

\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B^{\mathrm{CHT}},\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x}))\equiv u_{\mathrm{s,avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,r_{1},r_{2},\operatorname{Re},\operatorname{Pr}),

where
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
and
\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x})
denote the truth Biot number and variation function defined in
Eqs.

2.17
and
2.18
. Accordingly, the error in
Eq.

4.1
can be rewritten as

\displaystyle|u_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B^{\mathrm{CHT}},\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\bm{x}))-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;\undertilde{B})|.

For brevity, we omit the superscripts “CHT” for the remainder of this section and simply write
B\equiv B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
and
\eta\equiv\eta^{\mathrm{CHT}}
. With this convention, we now consider the maximum-in-time error and apply the triangle inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}|u_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\eta(t,\bm{x}))-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;\undertilde{B})|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}|u_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\eta(t,\bm{x}))-\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\overline{\eta}(\bm{x}))|

(4.2)

\displaystyle+\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}|\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\overline{\eta}(\bm{x}))-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B)|

(4.3)

\displaystyle+\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}|\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B)-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;\undertilde{B})|.

(4.4)

Here, the three terms correspond to:

•

(
4.2
): the error due to
temporal approximation
(replacing
\eta(t,\bm{x})
by
\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})
),

•

(
4.3
): the error due to
lumping
(replacing RHE
a
by LCM), and

•

(
4.4
): the error due to
Biot approximation
(replacing
B
by an estimate
\undertilde{B}
).

We first review the current engineering practice for assessing the applicability of the LCM. We then, one by one, discuss strategies to quantify and bound the errors in
Eqs.

4.2
,
4.3
and
4.4
, and provide supporting numerical results.

For some of the proofs, we will use the following estimate for exponential functions: For all
z\geq 0
and
0\leq\epsilon<1
, the inequality

\displaystyle\big|\exp(-z(1-\epsilon))-\exp(-z)\big|\leq\frac{|\epsilon|}{\exp(1)}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})

(4.5)

holds. A proof of this elementary estimate is provided for instance in
[
21
, Appendix C.17]
.

4.1
Current Engineering Practice

Because of its simplicity and minimal input requirements, the LCM is widely used in engineering practice as a quick estimate. This naturally raises the question: How accurate is the LCM, and under what conditions is it applicable? Standard engineering textbooks such as
[
25
,
20
]
typically propose the following criterion:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Bi}_{\mathrm{dunk}}\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{L}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}}}=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\overline{h}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muk\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s,inf}}}\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{L}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}=\frac{B}{\gamma}\leq 0.1,

(4.6)

where
\operatorname{Bi}_{\mathrm{dunk}}
denotes the Biot number defined with respect to the intrinsic length scale
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{L}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
. The heuristic condition in
Eq.

4.6
suggests that the LCM will be a reliable approximation when the Biot number is small. Although supported by empirical evidence in many cases, it lacks rigorous theoretical justification, and indeed, in some cases,
Eq.

4.6
can be very misleading. We intend to address this gap in the following sections.

4.2
Error Due to Temporal Approximation

In this section, we analyze the temporal approximation error in
Eq.

4.2
,

\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}|u_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\eta(t,\bm{x}))-\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\overline{\eta}(\bm{x}))|,

arising from replacing the time-dependent
\eta(t,\bm{x})
with a time-independent
\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})
. We first examine the short-time stiff behavior of the RHE and study its effect on the averaged temperature, before discussing the separation of fluid and solid time scales. Finally, we present numerical results supporting the theoretical findings.

4.2.1
Initial transient and stability

Looking back at
Fig.

4(a)
, we observe that the Nusselt number exhibits a very stiff behavior in short-time: it essentially starts at infinity and decays rapidly to a finite value. This raises the question of whether such transient behavior can contribute large errors. We first present a proposition that characterizes the initial stiff behavior using analytical solutions. We then establish uniform bounds for the weighted averaged temperature of the RHE and derive an energy bound that suggests stability.

Proposition 4.1
.

For very short times

t\ll C_{x}\tau_{\mathrm{conv}},

where
C_{x}
depends on the distance from the leading edge as well as on the local regularity of the domain (e.g., curvature and corners) and the convective time was defined in
Eq.

2.21
, the following short-time asymptotic behavior is expected:

\displaystyle\left.u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}\right|_{\Gamma}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{1+\sqrt{r_{1}r_{2}}},

(4.7)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nu}(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi t}}\,\sqrt{\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}}.

(4.8)

Proof.

The result is obtained by treating both the solid and the fluid as semi-infinite solids in perfect thermal contact and solving the corresponding one-dimensional transient conduction problem. For simplicity, the solid is assumed homogeneous, i.e.,
\kappa=1
and
\sigma=1
. The analysis is valid only for sufficiently short times, before convective boundary layers have developed, i.e.,
t\ll\tau_{\mathrm{conv}}
.

We first analyze the fluid side. Consider transient conduction into a semi-infinite medium occupying
\psi>0
, where
\psi
denotes the coordinate normal to the interface
\Gamma
. At
\psi=0
, a surface temperature
u_{\Gamma}
is suddenly imposed at
t=0
. The governing equation for the fluid (in non-dimensional form; compare with
Eq.

2.11a
for
\bm{v}^{\mathrm{CHT}}=0
) is

\displaystyle r_{1}\frac{\partial u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=r_{2}\frac{\partial^{2}u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial\psi^{2}}\quad\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{f}},

(4.9a)

with initial and boundary conditions

\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(0,\psi)=0,\qquad u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,0)=u_{\Gamma},\qquad u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\psi\to\infty)=0.

(4.10)

Introducing the similarity variable

\xi=\psi/(2\sqrt{(r_{2}/r_{1})t})

and setting
u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\psi)=f(\xi)
reduces the PDE to an ODE, whose solution—with the boundary conditions applied—is

\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\psi)=u_{\Gamma}\!\left[1-\erf\!\left(\frac{\psi}{2\sqrt{(r_{2}/r_{1})t}}\right)\right],

(4.11)

where
\erf
denotes the error function.
The corresponding non-dimensional heat flux at the surface
\psi=0
is

\displaystyle q_{f}(t,0)=-r_{2}\left.\frac{\partial u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial\psi}\right|_{\psi=0}=u_{\Gamma}\sqrt{\frac{r_{1}r_{2}}{\pi t}}.

(4.12)

Hence, using
Eq.

2.14
, the local Nusselt number becomes

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nu}(t,\bm{x})=\frac{\left.\frac{\partial u_{\mathrm{f}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial\psi}\right|_{\psi=0}}{u_{\Gamma}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi t}}\sqrt{\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}},

(4.13)

which proves
Eq.

4.8
.

For the solid, the conduction problem (compare with
Eq.

2.11b
with
\kappa=\sigma=1
) reads

\displaystyle\frac{\partial u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{2}u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial\psi^{2}}\quad\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{s}},

(4.14a)

with initial and boundary conditions

\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(0,\psi)=1,\qquad u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,0)=u_{\Gamma},\qquad u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\psi\to\infty)=1.

(4.15)

Using the similarity variable
\xi=\psi/(2\sqrt{t})
and setting
u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\psi)=g(\xi)
gives

\displaystyle u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}(t,\psi)=1+(1-u_{\Gamma})\erf\!\left(\frac{\psi}{2\sqrt{t}}\right).

(4.16)

The corresponding heat flux at
\psi=0
is

\displaystyle q_{s}(t,0)=-\left.\frac{\partial u_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}}{\partial\psi}\right|_{\psi=0}=(1-u_{\Gamma})\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi t}}.

(4.17)

Enforcing continuity of heat flux between fluid and solid,
q_{f}(t,0)=q_{s}(t,0)
, yields

\displaystyle u_{\Gamma}=\frac{1}{1+\sqrt{r_{1}r_{2}}},

(4.18)

which proves
Eq.

4.7
.
∎

What
4.1
shows is that, for very short times, the Nusselt number starts spatially uniform at a very large value (that depends on
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
) and decays with the inverse square root of time. This explains the stiff behavior observed in
Fig.

4(a)
, and also explains the spatial uniformity of the Nusselt number at very short times, see
Fig.

2.5
. To study the effects of the initial behavior on the solution, we now establish uniform bounds for the weighted averaged temperature of the RHE.

Let
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
be solutions of the RHE in
Eq.

2.25
with parameters

\{\Omega,{t}_{\mathrm{f}},\kappa,\sigma,B_{1},\eta_{1}(t,\bm{x})\}
and

\{\Omega,{t}_{\mathrm{f}},\kappa,\sigma,B_{2},\eta_{2}(t,\bm{x})\}
, respectively, and
B_{1}
,
B_{2}
,
\eta_{1}
,
\eta_{2}
non-negative. Define
w\coloneqq u_{1}-u_{2}
. Then the following bounds hold for all
(t,\bm{x})\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]\times\Omega
:

\displaystyle 0\leq u_{1}(t,\bm{x})\leq 1,\quad 0\leq u_{2}(t,\bm{x})\leq 1,\quad|w(t,\bm{x})|\leq 1,

(4.19)

which follow from the maximum principle for parabolic equations; see, e.g.,
[
10
]
.
As
Eq.

2.25
is linear, subtracting the equations satisfied by
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
, multiplying by
w
, and integrating over space and time yield

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\sigma w^{2}(\cdot,t)+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\int_{\Omega}\kappa|\bm{\nabla}w|^{2}+\int_{\partial\Omega}B_{1}\eta_{1}w^{2}\right)dt^{\prime}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\partial\Omega}(B_{2}\eta_{2}-B_{1}\eta_{1})u_{2}w\,dt^{\prime},

(4.20)

for all
t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]
.

As we are looking to bound the weighted averaged temperature
u_{\mathrm{avg}}
defined in
Eq.

2.26
, we relate it to the
L^{2}
-norm appearing in
Eq.

4.20
. For any
w\in H^{1}(\Omega)
, the weighted average
w_{\mathrm{avg}}
as defined in
Eq.

2.26
satisfies

\displaystyle w_{\mathrm{avg}}^{2}=\left(\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\int_{\Omega}\sigma w\right)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{|\Omega|^{2}}\int_{\Omega}\sigma\cdot\int_{\Omega}\sigma w^{2}=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\int_{\Omega}\sigma w^{2},

(4.21)

where
Eq.

2.29
was used in the last step. By combining the bounds in
Eq.

4.19
together with
Eqs.

4.20
and
4.21
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{|\Omega|}{2}w_{\mathrm{avg}}(t)^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\int_{\Omega}\kappa|\bm{\nabla}w|^{2}+\int_{\partial\Omega}B_{1}\eta_{1}w^{2}\right)dt^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|B_{2}\eta_{2}-B_{1}\eta_{1}\right\|_{L^{1}((0,t);L^{1}(\partial\Omega))},

(4.22)

where
w_{\mathrm{avg}}(t)
is the difference between the weighted averaged temperatures corresponding to
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
.

By setting
(B_{1},\eta_{1})=(B,\eta(t,\bm{x}))
and

(B_{2},\eta_{2})=(B,\overline{\eta}(\bm{x}))
,
we obtain a direct estimate for the temporal approximation error in
Eq.

4.2
,
Since the integral terms on the left-hand side of
Eq.

4.22
are nonnegative,
we can immediately deduce the bound

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}|u_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\eta(t,\bm{x}))-\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\overline{\eta}(\bm{x}))|\\
&\quad\leq\sqrt{\frac{2B}{|\Omega|}\left\|\eta(t,\bm{x})-\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})\right\|_{L^{1}((0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}});L^{1}(\partial\Omega))}}.\end{aligned}

(4.23)

The estimate shows that perturbations of the Robin coefficient in

L^{1}((0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}});L^{1}(\partial\Omega))
influence the solid temperature in a controlled manner,
as measured by the
L^{2}
-norm of the averaged temperature.
At short times, the norm is dominated by the initial transient,
but over longer time horizons, this effect is averaged out.
Consistent with this analysis, numerical results confirm that for large time scale separation,
the initial behavior has only a minor influence on the averaged temperature. This will be studied quantitatively in
Section

4.2.3
.

4.2.2
Time scale separation between fluid and solid and time averaging

Having discussed the initial transient behavior and its effects, we now develop physical arguments to assess the degree of time scale separation between the fluid and solid. In the case of time scale separation, the fluid flow and Nusselt number develop quickly and become stationary long before the solid temperature changes significantly. From the perspective of the solid, the fluid thus appears quasi-stationary, and it is reasonable to approximate
\eta(t,\bm{x})
by its time average
\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})
,

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{0}^{{t}_{\mathrm{f}}}\eta(t,\bm{x}).

(4.24)

To quantify the degree of time scale separation, we compare the fluid convective and solid equilibration time scales. The non-dimensional convective time scale was given in
Eq.

2.21
.
In the small-Biot regime, the solid temperature evolution is well described by the LCM, whose characteristic equilibration time—the time for the solid to cool to
1/\exp(1)
of its initial temperature—is

\displaystyle\tau^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}=\frac{1}{B\gamma},

as previously defined in
Eq.

3.5
.
The ratio of the two time scales is then

\displaystyle\frac{\tau^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}}{\tau_{\mathrm{conv}}}=\frac{r_{2}}{r_{1}}\frac{\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{Pr}}{B\gamma}=\frac{1}{r_{1}}\frac{\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{Pr}}{\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}\gamma},

(4.25)

where
Eq.

2.17
was used in the last step. A large value of this ratio indicates that the solid dynamics are much slower than the fluid dynamics, implying strong time scale separation.

In the small-Biot limit and for fixed Reynolds and Prandtl numbers, time scale separation depends primarily on
r_{1}
, while
r_{2}
affects it only indirectly through its influence on
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}
. The effects of
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
, and
\operatorname{Re}
on
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}
will be analyzed in
Section

5.4.1
.
It remains unclear how large the ratio in
Eq.

4.25
must be for the temporal approximation error to become negligible. We present numerical examples in
Section

4.2.3
, where we vary
r_{1}
, to study this empirically.

Comment 3
.

Strictly speaking, the argument based on
Eq.

4.25
assumes that the LCM accurately predicts the equilibration time of the CHT model. Since the LCM itself presupposes time scale separation, this assumption is not exact. Nevertheless, the ratio in
Eq.

4.25
provides a useful measure of time scale separation in the small-Biot regime, as will be confirmed empirically in
Section

4.2.3
.

Comment 4
.

It should be noted that the temporal average in
Eq.

4.24
used for
\overline{\eta}
is not necessarily optimal. In Appendix
D
, we present a proof for a simplified setting—neglecting the initial transient, and prescribing a spatially uniform time-periodic
\eta
—which demonstrates that the average is indeed optimal. The general case, however, remains unresolved and is left for future work.

4.2.3
Numerical results

We study the cylinder cross-flow case from
Section

2.1.4
for varying
r_{1}
to illustrate the effect of time scale separation on the temporal approximation error
Eq.

4.2
, which we denote here as

\displaystyle E_{t}\coloneqq\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}|u_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\eta(t,\bm{x}))-\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\overline{\eta}(\bm{x}))|.

(4.26)

The setup is identical to
Section

2.1.4
, except that
r_{1}
is varied. For each
r_{1}
, we compute the Nusselt number and average temperature with CHT using Nek5000; the results are shown in
Fig.

4.1
. For larger
r_{1}
values, vortex shedding occurs later in time or not at all within the chosen time horizon, and the Nusselt number continues to decrease without stabilizing. The corresponding average temperature decays more rapidly at early times—since the Nusselt number has higher values initially—but more slowly at later times, reflecting the lower Nusselt values. This behavior can partially be explained with the short time behavior, see
4.1
: for larger
r_{1}
, the initial Nusselt number is larger (see
Eq.

4.8
), leading to a more rapid initial decrease in the average temperature.

(a)

Spatially averaged Nusselt number

(b)

Average solid temperature

Figure 4.1
:
Effect of
r_{1}
on (a) the spatially averaged Nusselt number and (b) the average solid temperature.

For each case we also compute
B
and
\overline{\eta}(\bm{x})
, and solve the RHE
a
in DOLFINx
[
5
]
. Interestingly, the Biot number remains small and varies only weakly with
r_{1}
across the entire range. This highlights that using the LCM, which neglects the influence of
r_{1}
, can lead to large errors even when the Biot number is small.

The temporal error
E_{t}
is plotted against
r_{1}
in
Fig.

4.2
. As expected, the error increases with
r_{1}
, i.e., as time scale separation becomes less pronounced, and the initial transient effects become more significant. We highlight the case
r_{1}=0.0132
in red color, which corresponds to the example in
Section

2.1.4
. The error for this case is
E_{t}=$0.010\,307\,143\,115\,081\,074$
. We also provide all computed values of
B
,
\tau^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}/\tau_{\mathrm{conv}}
and
E_{t}
in
Table

4.1
. We can see that for this problem, the temporal approximation error is below 1% for
\tau^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}/\tau_{\mathrm{conv}}\gtrsim 300
.

:

E_{t}
as a function of
r_{1}
. The red point corresponds to the example in

2.1.4
.
