4.3
Error Due to Lumping

In this section, we derive an upper bound for the error in
Eq.

4.3
due to lumping (uniform temperature assumption),

\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}|\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\overline{\eta})-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B)|.

The key ingredients of the derivation are a generalized eigenvalue problem associated with the autonomous RHE
a
model in
Eq.

2.27
, and an asymptotic expansion of its first eigenvalue.

4.3.1
Error bound

We first introduce the generalized eigenvalue problem (we henceforth omit the adjective “generalized” in our exposition) associated to the autonomous RHE
a
model in
Eq.

2.27
and the corresponding Rayleigh quotient. For given
(\kappa,\sigma,B,\overline{\eta})
, find
(\psi_{j},\lambda_{j})
for
j=1,2,\ldots
solution to

\displaystyle-\bm{\nabla}\cdot(\kappa\,\bm{\nabla}\psi_{j})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{j}\,\sigma\,\psi_{j}\,

in

\displaystyle\Omega,

(4.27a)

\displaystyle\kappa\,\bm{\nabla}\psi_{j}\cdot\bm{n}+B\,\overline{\eta}\,\psi_{j}

\displaystyle=0

on

\displaystyle\partial\Omega,

(4.27b)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\sigma\,\psi_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=1.

(4.27c)

We can write the weak form of
Eq.

4.27
as: find
(\psi_{j},\lambda_{j})\in H^{1}(\Omega)\times\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\kappa\,\bm{\nabla}\psi_{j}\cdot\bm{\nabla}v+B\int_{\partial\Omega}\overline{\eta}\,\psi_{j}\,v=\lambda_{j}\int_{\Omega}\sigma\,\psi_{j}\,v\quad\forall v\in H^{1}(\Omega),

(4.28)

with normalization

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\sigma\,\psi_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=1.

Using the bilinear forms
a_{0}
and
a_{1}
defined in
Eqs.

2.32
and
2.33
, we can write the weak form in
Eq.

4.28
more compactly as: find
(\psi_{j},\lambda_{j})\in H^{1}(\Omega)\times\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle a_{0}(\psi_{j},v;\kappa)+Ba_{1}(\psi_{j},v;\overline{\eta})=\lambda_{j}m(\psi_{j},v;\sigma)\quad\forall v\in H^{1}(\Omega)

with normalization

\displaystyle m(\psi_{j},\psi_{j};\sigma)=1.

(4.29)

The corresponding Rayleigh quotient is

\displaystyle R(v;\kappa,\sigma,B,\overline{\eta})\coloneqq\frac{a_{0}(v,v;\kappa)+Ba_{1}(v,v;\overline{\eta})}{m(v,v;\sigma)}.

(4.30)

From the Rayleigh quotient, it follows that the eigenvalues are non-negative for
B\geq 0
and the properties of
\sigma,\kappa,\overline{\eta}
defined in
Eqs.

2.31
,
2.30
and
2.29
. We sort them in increasing order, hence
0\leq\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\ldots
, and the corresponding eigenfunctions form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\Omega)
with respect to the inner product induced by
m(\cdot,\cdot;\sigma)
. With the eigenpairs, we can expand the solution to the autonomous RHE
a
problem in
Eq.

2.27
as

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}(t,\bm{x};\kappa,\sigma,B,\overline{\eta})=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|\Omega|\psi_{j}(\bm{x})m(1,\psi_{j};\sigma)\exp(-\lambda_{j}t),

(4.31)

and the QoI as

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\overline{\eta})=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|\Omega|m(1,\psi_{j};\sigma)^{2}\exp(-\lambda_{j}t),

(4.32)

see
[
21
, Proposition 2.2 and 2.3]
for more details.
Furthermore, we have the following lemma for the coefficients of the first term in
Eq.

4.32
.

Lemma 4.1
.

The coefficient of the first term in
Eq.

4.32
can be expanded as

\displaystyle|\Omega|m(1,\psi_{1};\sigma)^{2}=1-B^{2}\Upsilon+\mathcal{O}(B^{3}),

(4.33)

where

\displaystyle\Upsilon\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}\sigma\psi^{\prime 0}_{1}\psi^{\prime 0}_{1}.

The proof is given in
[
21
, Appendix C.5]
.
In the small-Biot limit, the first eigenvalue
\lambda_{1}
dominates the long-time solution in
Eq.

4.32
. To analyze this regime, i.e., as
B\to 0
, we introduce an asymptotic expansion of the first eigenvalue in powers of
B
around
B=0
:

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(B)=\lambda_{1}^{0}+\lambda_{1}^{\prime 0}B+\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime 0}B^{2}+\mathcal{O}(B^{3}).

(4.34)

We use the superscript
0
to denote the case
B=0
, and the prime denotes differentiation with respect to
B
. In the following, we find expressions for
\lambda_{1}^{0}
,
\lambda_{1}^{\prime 0}
, and
\lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime 0}
.

From the Rayleigh quotient in
Eq.

4.30
, and setting
B=0
, we directly find the first eigenvalue and eigenfunction,

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{0}=0,\quad\psi_{1}^{0}=|\Omega|^{-1/2}.

(4.35)

Lemma 4.2
.

The first and second derivatives of the first eigenvalue at
B=0
are given by

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{\prime 0}

\displaystyle=\gamma,

(4.36)

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime 0}

\displaystyle=-2a_{0}(\psi^{\prime 0}_{1},\psi^{\prime 0}_{1};\kappa).

(4.37)

Proof.

Starting from the weak form in
Eq.

4.28
, we consider the sensitivity equation obtained by differentiating
Eq.

4.28
with respect to
B
and evaluating at
B=0
for the first eigenpair
(\psi_{1},\lambda_{1})\in H^{1}(\Omega)\times\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle a_{0}(\psi^{\prime 0}_{1},v;\kappa)+a_{1}(\psi_{1}^{0},v;\overline{\eta})=\lambda_{1}^{\prime 0}m(\psi_{1}^{0},v;\sigma)\quad\forall v\in H^{1}(\Omega).

(4.38)

We can similarly repeat for the normalization condition in
Eq.

4.29
to obtain

\displaystyle m(\psi_{1}^{0},\psi^{\prime 0}_{1};\sigma)=0,

(4.39)

which is equal to

\displaystyle m(1,\psi^{\prime 0}_{1};\sigma)=\int_{\Omega}\sigma\psi^{\prime 0}_{1}=0,

(4.40)

because of
Eq.

4.35
.
By choosing the test function as
v=\psi_{1}^{0}
in
Eq.

4.38
and using
Eqs.

4.29
,
2.31
and
4.35
, we find

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{\prime 0}=a_{1}(\psi_{1}^{0},\psi_{1}^{0};\overline{\eta})=|\Omega|^{-1}\int_{\partial\Omega}\overline{\eta}=\gamma,

(4.41)

which proves
Eq.

4.36
. Next, we can insert
v=\psi^{\prime 0}_{1}
into
Eq.

4.38
to find the relation

\displaystyle a_{0}(\psi^{\prime 0}_{1},\psi^{\prime 0}_{1};\kappa)+a_{1}(\psi_{1}^{0},\psi^{\prime 0}_{1};\overline{\eta})=0,

(4.42)

where we used
Eq.

4.39
. To find the second derivative, we differentiate
Eq.

4.28
twice with respect to
B
and evaluate at
B=0
with
\lambda_{1}^{0}=0
and
\lambda_{1}^{\prime 0}=\gamma
to obtain

\displaystyle a_{0}(\psi^{\prime\prime 0}_{1},v;\kappa)+2a_{1}(\psi^{\prime 0}_{1},v;\overline{\eta})=\lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime 0}m(\psi_{1}^{0},v;\sigma)+2\gamma m(\psi^{\prime 0}_{1},v;\sigma)\quad\forall v\in H^{1}(\Omega).

Now with
v=\psi_{1}^{0}
and using
Eqs.

4.29
,
4.35
,
4.42
and
4.39
, we find

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime 0}=2a_{1}(\psi^{\prime 0}_{1},\psi_{1}^{0};\overline{\eta})=-2a_{0}(\psi^{\prime 0}_{1},\psi^{\prime 0}_{1};\kappa),

(4.43)

which concludes the proof.
∎

We now define the coefficient
\phi
as

\displaystyle\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})\coloneqq a_{0}(\psi^{\prime 0}_{1},\psi^{\prime 0}_{1};\kappa)=\int_{\Omega}\kappa|\bm{\nabla}\psi^{\prime 0}_{1}|^{2},

(4.44)

which can be related to
\lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime 0}
with
Eq.

4.43
,

\displaystyle\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})=-\frac{\lambda_{1}^{\prime\prime 0}}{2},

(4.45)

and we can write the asymptotic expansion of the first eigenvalue in
Eq.

4.34
as

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(B)=\gamma B-\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})B^{2}+O(B^{3}).

(4.46)

The coefficient
\phi
is of central importance for the error analysis, as seen below. To evaluate
\phi
, we need to solve the sensitivity equation, derived in
Eqs.

4.38
and
4.40
, and rewritten here: find
\psi^{\prime 0}_{1}\in H^{1}(\Omega)
such that

\displaystyle a_{0}(\psi^{\prime 0}_{1},v;\kappa)=|\Omega|^{-1/2}L(v;\sigma,\overline{\eta}),\quad\forall v\in H^{1}(\Omega),

with normalization condition

\displaystyle m(\psi^{\prime 0}_{1},1;\sigma)=0,

and where the linear form
L
is defined as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} L(v;\sigma,\overline{\eta})&\coloneqq\gamma m(1,v;\sigma)-a_{1}(1,v;\overline{\eta})\\
&=\gamma\int_{\Omega}\sigma v-\int_{\partial\Omega}\overline{\eta}v.\end{aligned}

(4.47)

We now define the function space

\displaystyle Z_{0}(b)\coloneqq\left\{\,w\in H^{1}(\Omega)\ \big|\ \int_{\Omega}bw=0\,\right\},

with which we can rewrite the sensitivity problem as: find
\psi^{\prime 0}_{1}\in Z_{0}(\sigma)
such that

\displaystyle a_{0}(\psi^{\prime 0}_{1},v;\kappa)=|\Omega|^{-1/2}L(v;\sigma,\overline{\eta})\quad\forall v\in Z_{0}(\sigma),

(4.48)

or expanded explicitly,

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\kappa\bm{\nabla}\psi^{\prime 0}_{1}\cdot\bm{\nabla}v=-|\Omega|^{-1/2}\int_{\partial\Omega}\overline{\eta}v\quad\forall v\in Z_{0}(\sigma),

where the first term on the right hand side vanishes due to the definition of
Z_{0}(\sigma)
. The associated strong form is given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} -\bm{\nabla}\cdot(\kappa\bm{\nabla}\psi^{\prime 0}_{1})&=0&\quad&\text{in }&\Omega,\\
\kappa\bm{\nabla}\psi^{\prime 0}_{1}\cdot\bm{n}&=-|\Omega|^{-1/2}\overline{\eta}&\quad&\text{on }&\partial\Omega,\\
\int_{\Omega}\sigma\psi^{\prime 0}_{1}&=0.&\end{aligned}

(4.49)

The numerical solution of this problem was discussed in
[
21
]
(for
\overline{\eta}=1
).
We now get to the central result of this section.

Proposition 4.2

(
Lumping error
)
.

The error due to lumping is bounded asymptotically from above with

\displaystyle\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}|\widetilde{u}_{\rm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\overline{\eta})-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B)|\leq\frac{\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})}{\gamma\exp(1)}B+O(B^{2}),

(4.50)

where the coefficient
\phi>0
was defined in
Eq.

4.44
.

Proof.

We start by writing the difference between the RHE
a
in
Eq.

4.32
and LCM solution in
Eq.

3.4
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{\rm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\overline{\eta})-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|\Omega|m(1,\psi_{j};\sigma)^{2}\exp(-\lambda_{j}t)-\exp(-\gamma Bt)

\displaystyle=\exp(-\lambda_{1}t)-\exp(-\gamma Bt)+|\Omega|\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}m(1,\psi_{j};\sigma)^{2}\exp(-\lambda_{j}t),

where we inserted
Eq.

4.33
for the first term of the series and truncated terms of order
B^{2}
.
As
\lambda_{j}\geq 0
, we can bound the last term with

\displaystyle|\Omega|\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}m(1,\psi_{j};\sigma)^{2}\exp(-\lambda_{j}t)

\displaystyle\leq|\Omega|\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}m(1,\psi_{j};\sigma)^{2}

\displaystyle=|\Omega|\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}m(1,\psi_{j};\sigma)^{2}-|\Omega|m(1,\psi_{1};\sigma)^{2}

\displaystyle=|\Omega|\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}m(1,\psi_{j};\sigma)^{2}-(1-B^{2}\Upsilon+\mathcal{O}(B^{3})),

where we again used
Eq.

4.33
. From Parseval’s identity, we know

\displaystyle|\Omega|\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}m(1,\psi_{j};\sigma)^{2}=1,

and it follows that

\displaystyle|\Omega|\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}m(1,\psi_{j};\sigma)^{2}\exp(-\lambda_{j}t)

\displaystyle\leq B^{2}\Upsilon+\mathcal{O}(B^{3}).

Now, by inserting the asymptotic expansion of the first eigenvalue in
Eq.

4.46
and truncating terms of order
B^{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{\rm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\overline{\eta})-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B)

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-\lambda_{1}t)-\exp(-\gamma Bt)+\mathcal{O}(B^{2})

\displaystyle=\exp(-(\gamma B-\phi B^{2})t)-\exp(-\gamma Bt)+\mathcal{O}(B^{2})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\gamma Bt\left(1-\frac{\phi B}{\gamma}\right)\right)-\exp(-\gamma Bt)+\mathcal{O}(B^{2}).

Finally, we use the exponential bound in
Eq.

4.5
with
z=\gamma Bt
and
\epsilon=\phi B/\gamma
to find the bound in
Eq.

4.50
.
∎

4.3.2
Upper bound for
\phi

We can use
Eq.

4.50
to bound the lumping error given knowledge of the coefficient
\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})
. However, to compute
\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})
,
Eq.

4.49
needs to be solved, for which the spatial distributions of the material properties
\kappa
and
\sigma
as well as the time-independent variation
\overline{\eta}
are required—quantities that might be unknown in applications. In this section, we derive an upper bound for
\phi
that is less restrictive: it only requires knowledge of the geometry
\Omega
and the variances of
\sigma
and
\overline{\eta}
, given by

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Omega}(\sigma-1)^{2},

(4.51)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)^{2}

(4.52)

which are scalar derived quantities.

Before we state the main result, we first derive an upper bound for
\phi
that removes the dependency of
\phi
on
\kappa
.

Lemma 4.3
.

The coefficient
\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})
is bounded from above with

\displaystyle\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})\leq\phi(1,\sigma,\overline{\eta}).

(4.53)

Proof.

First, define
\xi\in Z_{0}(\sigma)
and
\xi^{1}\in Z_{0}(\sigma)
as the solutions to
Eq.

4.48
for a general
\kappa
and
\kappa=1
respectively, i.e.,

\displaystyle a_{0}(\xi,v;\kappa)

\displaystyle=|\Omega|^{-1/2}L(v;\sigma,\overline{\eta})\quad\forall v\in Z_{0}(\sigma),

(4.54)

\displaystyle a_{0}(\xi^{1},v;1)

\displaystyle=|\Omega|^{-1/2}L(v;\sigma,\overline{\eta})\quad\forall v\in Z_{0}(\sigma).

(4.55)

Moreover, we have with
Eq.

4.44
that

\displaystyle\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})=a_{0}(\xi,\xi;\kappa),

(4.56)

\displaystyle\phi(1,\sigma,\overline{\eta})=a_{0}(\xi^{1},\xi^{1};1).

(4.57)

By inserting
v=\xi
into
Eq.

4.54
and
v=\xi^{1}
into
Eq.

4.55
, we obtain the following relations:

\displaystyle|\Omega|^{-1/2}L(\xi;\sigma,\overline{\eta})=\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta}),

(4.58)

\displaystyle|\Omega|^{-1/2}L(\xi^{1};\sigma,\overline{\eta})=\phi(1,\sigma,\overline{\eta}).

(4.59)

We can now introduce the energy functional associated with
Eq.

4.48
,

\displaystyle J(v;\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}a_{0}(v,v;\kappa)-|\Omega|^{-1/2}L(v;\sigma,\overline{\eta}).

(4.60)

As the solution
\xi^{1}
minimizes
J(v;1,\sigma,\overline{\eta})
,we can write

\displaystyle J(\xi^{1};1,\sigma,\overline{\eta})

\displaystyle\leq J(\xi;1,\sigma,\overline{\eta}).

The left term can be rewritten with
Eqs.

4.57
and
4.59
as

\displaystyle J(\xi^{1};1,\sigma,\overline{\eta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}a_{0}(\xi^{1},\xi^{1};1)-|\Omega|^{-1/2}L(\xi^{1};\sigma,\overline{\eta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\phi(1,\sigma,\overline{\eta})-\phi(1,\sigma,\overline{\eta})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\phi(1,\sigma,\overline{\eta}).

Since
\kappa\geq 1
, see
Eq.

2.30
, the right term can be rewritten with
Eqs.

4.56
and
4.58
as

\displaystyle J(\xi;1,\sigma,\overline{\eta})

\displaystyle\leq J(\xi;\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}a_{0}(\xi,\xi;\kappa)-|\Omega|^{-1/2}L(\xi;\sigma,\overline{\eta})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta}).

Combining both results and dividing by
-1/2
on both sides yields
Eq.

4.53
.
∎

Proposition 4.3
.

The coefficient
\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})
is bounded from above with

\displaystyle\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})\leq(\sqrt{\phi(1,1,1)}+\sqrt{\delta^{\sigma}}+\sqrt{\delta^{\overline{\eta}}})^{2},

(4.61)

where
\phi(1,1,1)
depends only on the geometry
\Omega
. The corrections,
\delta^{\sigma}
and
\delta^{\overline{\eta}}
, are defined as

\displaystyle\delta^{\sigma}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\gamma^{2}}{\mu}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int_{\Omega}(\sigma-1)^{2},

(4.62)

\displaystyle\delta^{\overline{\eta}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\gamma}{\Lambda}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)^{2}.

(4.63)

Here,
\mu
and
\Lambda
correspond to eigenvalues defined as

\displaystyle\mu\coloneqq\inf_{w\in Z_{0}(1)}\frac{a_{0}(w,w)}{\int_{\Omega}w^{2}},

(4.64)

\displaystyle\Lambda\coloneqq\inf_{w\in Z_{0}(1)}\frac{a_{0}(w,w)}{\int_{\partial\Omega}w^{2}},

(4.65)

which are also purely geometric quantities. We note that the stability constants
\gamma^{2}/\mu
and
\gamma/\Lambda
are scale invariant. The numerical treatment of the eigenvalue problems is outlined in Appendix
B
.

Proof.

We start the proof by using
4.3
to remove the dependence on
\kappa
,

\displaystyle\phi(\kappa,\sigma,\overline{\eta})\leq\phi(1,\sigma,\overline{\eta}).

For the remainder of this proof, we thus set
\kappa=1
and write, for brevity, hereafter

\displaystyle a_{0}(v,w)

\displaystyle\equiv a_{0}(v,w;1),

\displaystyle\phi(\sigma,\overline{\eta})

\displaystyle\equiv\phi(1,\sigma,\overline{\eta}).

We start by defining

\displaystyle\phi(\sigma,\overline{\eta})\coloneqq a_{0}(\psi^{\prime 0}_{1},\psi^{\prime 0}_{1}),

where
\psi^{\prime 0}_{1}\in Z_{0}(\sigma)
is the solution to
Eq.

4.48
,

\displaystyle a_{0}(\psi^{\prime 0}_{1},v)=|\Omega|^{-1/2}L(v;\sigma,\overline{\eta}),\quad\forall v\in Z_{0}(\sigma).

We also define
\xi\in Z_{0}(1)
which solves the same problem but in a different space,

\displaystyle a_{0}(\xi,v)=|\Omega|^{-1/2}L(v;\sigma,\overline{\eta}),\quad\forall v\in Z_{0}(1).

(4.66)

Both
\psi^{\prime 0}_{1}
and
\xi
are related via a constant shift,

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime 0}_{1}=\xi-\int_{\Omega}\sigma\xi,

and, thus, it holds,

\displaystyle\phi(\sigma,\overline{\eta})=a_{0}(\psi^{\prime 0}_{1},\psi^{\prime 0}_{1})=a_{0}(\xi,\xi),

(4.67)

since
a_{0}
only depends on gradients. We can now split the right hand side in
Eq.

4.66
with
Eq.

4.47
into three parts,

\displaystyle a_{0}(\xi,v)=|\Omega|^{-1/2}\left(\gamma\int_{\Omega}(\sigma-1)v+\gamma\int_{\Omega}v-\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)v-\int_{\partial\Omega}v\right),\quad\forall v\in Z_{0}(1),

as well as
\xi=\overline{\xi}+\widehat{\xi}+\widetilde{\xi}
, where
\overline{\xi},\widehat{\xi},\widetilde{\xi}\in Z_{0}(1)
, to obtain three separate problems:

\displaystyle a_{0}(\overline{\xi},v)

\displaystyle=|\Omega|^{-1/2}\left(\gamma\int_{\Omega}v-\int_{\partial\Omega}v\right)=|\Omega|^{-1/2}L(v;1,1),\quad\forall v\in Z_{0}(1),

(4.68)

\displaystyle a_{0}(\widehat{\xi},v)

\displaystyle=|\Omega|^{-1/2}\gamma\int_{\Omega}(\sigma-1)v,\quad\forall v\in Z_{0}(1),

(4.69)

\displaystyle a_{0}(\widetilde{\xi},v)

\displaystyle=-|\Omega|^{-1/2}\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)v,\quad\forall v\in Z_{0}(1).

(4.70)

The first equation in
Eq.

4.68
does not depend on
\sigma
nor
\overline{\eta}
and can be directly solved for
\overline{\xi}
given a geometry
\Omega
, which yields

\displaystyle\phi(1,1)\coloneqq a_{0}(\overline{\xi},\overline{\xi}).

(4.71)

Choosing
v=\widehat{\xi}
in the second equation in
Eq.

4.69
, successively applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and inserting the definition of
\mu
in
Eq.

4.64
gives

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} a_{0}(\widehat{\xi},\widehat{\xi})&=|\Omega|^{-1/2}\gamma\int_{\Omega}(\sigma-1)\widehat{\xi}\\
&\leq|\Omega|^{-1/2}\gamma\sqrt{\int_{\Omega}(\sigma-1)^{2}}\sqrt{\int_{\Omega}\widehat{\xi}^{2}}\\
&\leq|\Omega|^{-1/2}\gamma\sqrt{\int_{\Omega}(\sigma-1)^{2}}\sqrt{a_{0}(\widehat{\xi},\widehat{\xi})\sup_{w\in Z_{0}(1)}\frac{\int_{\Omega}w^{2}}{a_{0}(w,w)}}\\
&=|\Omega|^{-1/2}\gamma\sqrt{\int_{\Omega}(\sigma-1)^{2}}\sqrt{a_{0}(\widehat{\xi},\widehat{\xi})\mu^{-1}}.\end{aligned}

(4.72)

By dividing both sides of
Eq.

4.72
by
\sqrt{a_{0}(\widehat{\xi},\widehat{\xi})}
, squaring and inserting
Eq.

4.62
, we obtain

\displaystyle a_{0}(\widehat{\xi},\widehat{\xi})\leq\delta^{\sigma}.

(4.73)

Similarly, choosing
v=\widetilde{\xi}
in the third equation in
Eq.

4.70
, successively applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and inserting the definition of
\Lambda
in
Eq.

4.65
yields

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} a_{0}(\widetilde{\xi},\widetilde{\xi})&=-|\Omega|^{-1/2}\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)\widetilde{\xi}\\
&\leq|\Omega|^{-1/2}\sqrt{\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)^{2}}\sqrt{\int_{\partial\Omega}\widetilde{\xi}^{2}}\\
&\leq|\Omega|^{-1/2}\sqrt{\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)^{2}}\sqrt{a_{0}(\widetilde{\xi},\widetilde{\xi})\sup_{w\in Z_{0}(1)}\frac{\int_{\partial\Omega}w^{2}}{a_{0}(w,w)}}\\
&=|\Omega|^{-1/2}\sqrt{\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)^{2}}\sqrt{a_{0}(\widetilde{\xi},\widetilde{\xi})\Lambda^{-1}},\end{aligned}

(4.74)

and dividing both sides of
Eq.

4.74
by
\sqrt{a_{0}(\widetilde{\xi},\widetilde{\xi})}
, squaring, and inserting
Eq.

4.63
, yields

\displaystyle a_{0}(\widetilde{\xi},\widetilde{\xi})\leq\delta^{\overline{\eta}}.

(4.75)

Starting now from
Eq.

4.67
, and inserting
\xi=\overline{\xi}+\widehat{\xi}+\widetilde{\xi}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \phi(\sigma,\overline{\eta})&=a_{0}(\xi,\xi)\\
&=a_{0}(\overline{\xi}+\widehat{\xi}+\widetilde{\xi},\overline{\xi}+\widehat{\xi}+\widetilde{\xi})\\
&=a_{0}(\overline{\xi},\overline{\xi})+a_{0}(\widehat{\xi},\widehat{\xi})+a_{0}(\widetilde{\xi},\widetilde{\xi})+2a_{0}(\overline{\xi},\widehat{\xi})+2a_{0}(\overline{\xi},\widetilde{\xi})+2a_{0}(\widehat{\xi},\widetilde{\xi}).\end{aligned}

(4.76)

The mixed terms in
Eq.

4.76
can be bounded with the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality:

\displaystyle a_{0}(\overline{\xi},\widehat{\xi})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{a_{0}(\overline{\xi},\overline{\xi})}\sqrt{a_{0}(\widehat{\xi},\widehat{\xi})},

(4.77)

\displaystyle a_{0}(\overline{\xi},\widetilde{\xi})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{a_{0}(\overline{\xi},\overline{\xi})}\sqrt{a_{0}(\widetilde{\xi},\widetilde{\xi})},

(4.78)

\displaystyle a_{0}(\widetilde{\xi},\widehat{\xi})

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{a_{0}(\widetilde{\xi},\widetilde{\xi})}\sqrt{a_{0}(\widehat{\xi},\widehat{\xi})}.

(4.79)

Inserting
Eqs.

4.77
,
4.78
and
4.79
together with
Eqs.

4.71
,
4.73
and
4.75
into
Eq.

4.76
concludes the proof:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \phi(\sigma,\overline{\eta})&=a_{0}(\overline{\xi},\overline{\xi})+a_{0}(\widehat{\xi},\widehat{\xi})+a_{0}(\widetilde{\xi},\widetilde{\xi})+2a_{0}(\overline{\xi},\widehat{\xi})+2a_{0}(\overline{\xi},\widetilde{\xi})+2a_{0}(\widehat{\xi},\widetilde{\xi})\\
&\leq\left(\sqrt{a_{0}(\overline{\xi},\overline{\xi})}+\sqrt{a_{0}(\widehat{\xi},\widehat{\xi})}+\sqrt{a_{0}(\widetilde{\xi},\widetilde{\xi})}\right)^{2}\\
&\leq\left(\sqrt{\phi(1,1)}+\sqrt{\delta^{\sigma}}+\sqrt{\delta^{\overline{\eta}}}\right)^{2}.\end{aligned}

∎

Comment 5
.

Instead of solving the eigenvalue problems in
Eqs.

4.64
and
4.65
to obtain
\mu
and
\Lambda
, it is possible to derive lower bounds for these quantities. For example, for convex domains, the Payne-Weinberger inequality
[
27
]
can be used to bound
\mu
from below with

\displaystyle\mu\geq\frac{\pi^{2}}{\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}^{2}},

where
\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}
is the diameter of the domain
\Omega
, defined as the largest distance between any two points in
\Omega
. Similar lower bounds for
\Lambda
can be found in
[
14
]
.

To evaluate the bound in
Eq.

4.61
, only the geometry
\Omega
(from which
\phi(1,1,1)
,
\mu
and
\Lambda
can be computed) and the variances in
Eqs.

4.51
and
4.52
are required. Importantly, no detailed knowledge of the spatial distributions of
\kappa
,
\sigma
, or
\overline{\eta}
is needed; it suffices to know only the derived quantities.
For the special case of composite materials, where the solid is composed of
n
materials of known volume fraction
v_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}=1
and known constant density
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{i}
and constant heat capacity
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
, the variance of
\sigma
can be directly evaluated,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Omega}(\sigma-1)^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}\left(\frac{(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)_{i}}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}v_{j}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)_{j}}-1\right)^{2}.

(4.80)

However, evaluating the variance of
\eta
is significantly more challenging, as it depends on the external flow conditions.

In the following two sections, we study the tightness of the bounds in
Eqs.

4.61
and
4.50
with numerical examples for
given
domain,
\kappa
,
\sigma
, and
\overline{\eta}
. In Appendix
A
, we provide a numerical study on the values of
\overline{\eta}
for various geometries under realistic flow conditions coming from CHT solutions and experimental data, and provide guidance on estimating the
\eta
-variance in practice.

4.3.3
Numerical examples for homogeneous solids

In this section, we consider only homogeneous solids,
\sigma=1
and
\kappa=1
, to study how the shape of
\overline{\eta}
affects the lumping error. The influence of geometry on
\phi
for
\overline{\eta}=1
has been analyzed in detail in
[
21
]
. In the examples presented here, we study the influence of
\overline{\eta}\not=1
on
\phi
, as well as the upper bound derived for different domains and
\overline{\eta}
.

We consider the four two-dimensional geometries shown in
Fig.

4.3
—a disk, a square, an equilateral triangle, and a cross. For each geometry, we compute the relevant geometric quantities: the coefficient
\phi(1,1,1)
, and the scale-independent stability constants
\gamma^{2}/\mu
and
\gamma/\Lambda
. The computations are performed with DOLFINx
[
5
]
, as described in Appendix
B
, and the results are summarized in
Table

4.2
. For the disk, the analytical values
\phi(1,1,1)=1/2
and
\gamma/\Lambda=2
are known
[
21
,
14
]
, and they agree with our numerical results.

Figure 4.3
:
Geometries used in the numerical study of the LCM error.

:
Comparison of
\phi
constants for various two-dimensional geometries: the coefficient
\phi(1,1,1)
defined in
Eq.

4.44
, the stability constant
\gamma^{2}/\mu
in
Eq.

4.62
, and the stability constant
\gamma/\Lambda
in
Eq.

4.63
.

We consider four functions for
\overline{\eta}
, defined in non-dimensionalized coordinates (
x,y
) scaled by the domain diameter,
\operatorname{diam}(\Omega)\coloneqq\max_{\bm{x},\bm{y}\in\Omega}\|\bm{x}-\bm{y}\|_{2}
, with the origin at the centroid:

(i)

Constant function:
\overline{\eta}=c
.

(ii)

Linear function:
\overline{\eta}(x,y)=c(1+x+y)
.

(iii)

Sinusoidal function:
\overline{\eta}(x,y)=c(1+\sin(10\pi x)\sin(5\pi y))
.

(iv)

Step function:

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}(x,y)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }y<0,\\
2c&\text{if }y\geq 0.\end{cases}

(4.81)

For each domain and function,
c>0
is chosen such that
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}\overline{\eta}=1
.

The variance
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)^{2}
, together with the corresponding
\delta^{\overline{\eta}}
, the upper bound
\phi^{\text{ub}}
, and the actual value of
\phi
, are reported in
Tables

4.3
,
4.4
,
4.5
and
4.6
for each domain and choice of
\overline{\eta}
. In all cases,
\phi^{\text{ub}}
is about 2-4 times larger than the actual value of
\phi
, indicating that the bound is reasonably tight for our purposes.

Since the variance of
\overline{\eta}
may be unknown in practice, we also report
\phi^{\text{ub,est}}
, obtained by assuming a variance of
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)^{2}=1
. In this case,
\phi^{\text{ub,est}}
can be up to ten times larger than the actual
\phi
. A variance of 1, however, is likely an overestimate for realistic applications, as it implies that the standard deviation of
\overline{\eta}
is comparable to its mean—corresponding to regions with very high heat transfer alongside regions with none. The step function defined in
Eq.

4.81
is one example with such a variance on some domains. In Appendix
A
, we analyze convex geometries under realistic flow conditions and find that the variance is typically much smaller than 1, which brings
\phi^{\text{ub,est}}
significantly closer both to
\phi^{\text{ub}}
and the actual value of
\phi
.

\delta^{\overline{\eta}}
. To compute

.

for each

which we denote here by

E_{l}\coloneqq\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}|\widetilde{u}_{\mathrm{avg}}(t;\kappa,\sigma,B,\overline{\eta}(\bm{x}))-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B)|

For RHE

where
\tau^{\mathrm{L}}_{\mathrm{eq}}
was defined in

. The error
E_{l}

,

, and

, is shown in

for two example cases. We observe that the bound based on
\phi
is asymptotically tight for small

. The results are qualitatively the same for the other geometries and
\overline{\eta}

4.3.4
Numerical example for a layered solid

In the previous examples, we considered homogeneous solids, i.e.,
\sigma=\kappa=1
. The error bound with
\phi
in
Eq.

4.50
and the upper bound for
\phi
derived in
Eq.

4.61
also extend to heterogeneous solids. In this section, we present one example with a heterogeneous solid to demonstrate that the error bound in
Eq.

4.50
remains valid for heterogeneous materials, and that the upper bound in
Eq.

4.61
still bounds
\phi
from above.

We consider the two-dimensional cross domain shown in
Fig.

4.3
, divided along its vertical centerline into two regions of equal thickness, each corresponding to a different material, and apply the step function for
\overline{\eta}
defined in
Eq.

4.81
. This setup is identical to that of
Fig.

4(b)
, except that the solid is now heterogeneous rather than homogeneous. The parameter
\sigma
is assumed piecewise constant, taking values
\sigma_{0}
and
2\sigma_{0}
in the two layers. Imposing
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}\sigma=1
gives
\sigma_{0}=2/3
, yielding a variance of
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}(\sigma-1)^{2}=1/9
. Note here that to compute the variance, the detailed spatial distribution of
\sigma
is not required, and the variance can be computed with
Eq.

4.80
. We solve the RHE
a
problem, using the same settings as before, for various
B/\gamma
values, and compute the actual error
E_{l}
between the RHE
a
and LCM solutions. We plot the results in
Fig.

4.5
, where we also include the error bounds based on
\phi
,
\phi^{\text{ub}}
, and
\phi^{\text{ub,est}}
. We observe that the error bound based on
\phi
is again asymptotically tight for small
B
, and that both
\phi^{\text{ub}}
and
\phi^{\text{ub,est}}
bound
\phi
from above.

Figure 4.5
:
Comparison of
\phi
,
\phi^{\text{ub}}
,
\phi^{\text{ub,est}}
and actual error
E_{l}
for the cross specimen with two layers. The green curve (
\phi^{\text{ub}}
) and the red curve (
\phi^{\text{ub,est}}
) overlap, since the variance of the step-function
\overline{\eta}
is close to 1.

Comparing
Fig.

4.5
with
Fig.

4(b)
, we observe that the actual error
E_{l}
, as well as
\phi
,
\phi^{\text{ub}}
, and
\phi^{\text{ub,est}}
all increase for the layered material. This is expected, since
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Omega}(\sigma-1)^{2}=1/9
here, whereas it was zero in
Fig.

4(b)
.

4.4
Error Due to Biot Approximation

In this section, we analyze the error arising from the Biot approximation in
Eq.

4.4
, namely

\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}\big|\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B)-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;\undertilde{B})\big|.

In general, we do not know the true Biot number
B
and must rely on an estimate
\undertilde{B}
.
Applying the exponential inequality in
Eq.

4.5
to our setting gives

\displaystyle\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}\big|\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B)-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;\undertilde{B})\big|

\displaystyle=\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}\Big|\exp(-B\gamma t)-\exp(-\undertilde{B}\gamma t)\Big|

\displaystyle=\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}\Big|\exp\!\left(-\undertilde{B}\gamma t\left(1-\frac{B-\undertilde{B}}{\undertilde{B}}\right)\right)-\exp(-\undertilde{B}\gamma t)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|B-\undertilde{B}|}{\undertilde{B}\exp(1)}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\left(\frac{B-\undertilde{B}}{\undertilde{B}}\right)^{2}\right),

(4.82)

where we set
z=\undertilde{B}\gamma t
and
\epsilon=(B-\undertilde{B})/\undertilde{B}
. Hence, the error induced by the Biot approximation scales linearly with the relative error in the Biot number,
|B-\undertilde{B}|/\undertilde{B}
.

4.5
Example Revisited: Cross-Flow Around a Circular Cylinder

We conclude this chapter by revisiting the cross-flow around a circular cylinder from
Section

2.1.4
one last time, now evaluating all error sources in
Eqs.

4.2
,
4.3
and
4.4
:

•

The
temporal error
in
Eq.

4.2
has already been assessed in
Section

4.2.3
as
E_{t}=$0.010\,307\,143\,115\,081\,074$
, see
Fig.

4.2
.

•

Using ISO, we obtained a Biot number of
0.0678
, compared to the actual value
0.0680
. The
Biot approximation error
in
Eq.

4.82
is bounded by

\frac{|B-\undertilde{B}|}{\undertilde{B}\exp(1)}=$0.001\,081\,998\,35$.

•

For the
lumping error
, we use the time-averaged variation function
\overline{\eta}
from the CHT solution and compute
\phi=$1.105\,302\,566\,847\,047\,5$
with
Eqs.

4.49
and
4.44
. The variance in this case is
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)^{2}=0.316
(much smaller than 1), which gives the upper bound
\phi^{\text{ub}}=$2.256\,650\,480\,672\,011$
with
Eq.

4.61
. The values of
\phi(1,1,1)
and
\gamma/\Lambda
have been provided for the disk (which is equivalent to the cylinder in 2D) in
Table

4.2
. Evaluating our error bound in
Eq.

4.50
with
\phi
and
\phi^{\text{ub}}
for the given
B=0.0680
gives
0.006\,912\,5
and
0.014\,112\,96
. We also solve the RHE
a
and find the actual error
E_{l}=$0.006\,823\,082\,401\,541\,114$
. Thus, the bound with
\phi
is very tight, while the bound with
\phi^{\text{ub}}
is about twice as large.

We can also evaluate the heuristic criterion, used in engineering practice and defined in
Eq.

4.6
, to assess the validity of the LCM. We obtain

\displaystyle\frac{B}{\gamma}=0.01695<0.1,

with
\gamma=4
from
Eq.

3.8
, and, hence, the LCM would be deemed valid in this case. The criterion works well here, since the value of
\phi
is small for this problem.

In this example, the total error is dominated by the temporal approximation error. This contribution is typically ignored in engineering practice, where time scale separation is assumed (
r_{1}
does not appear in ISO nor LCM). However, in
Section

4.2.3
, we showed numerically that the temporal errors can become very large even in small Biot cases.

If
r_{1}
is sufficiently small, then the temporal approximation error becomes negligible. Similarly, if the Biot number can be accurately estimated, the Biot approximation error becomes small. The dominant contribution would then be the lumping error, which can be bounded
a priori
with
Eqs.

4.50
and
4.61
, provided that good estimates of
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)^{2}
and
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Omega}(\sigma-1)^{2}
are available.

5
About the Nusselt Number

A key practical challenge in applying the LCM is finding an approximate Biot number
\undertilde{B}
that closely matches the true Biot number
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
for a given problem. Traditionally, this is achieved by estimating the averaged Nusselt number
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
through
empirical correlations
, from which
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
is computed via
Eq.

2.17
. Note that, as mentioned in the introduction, we use the terms correlation and empirical correlation interchangeably, without any statistical interpretation.

In
Section

2.3
, we have introduced the ISO formulation and the corresponding Nusselt number
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}
, and demonstrated in a representative case—characterized by large time scale separation and a small Biot number—that
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}
can accurately approximate
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
. While the CHT Nusselt
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
generally depends on
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
,
\kappa
,
\sigma
, as well as
\operatorname{Re}
and
\operatorname{Pr}
, the ISO Nusselt number
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}
depends only on
\operatorname{Re}
and
\operatorname{Pr}
.

Compared to the full CHT model, the ISO formulation is computationally more efficient. However, because it still requires solving the Navier-Stokes equations, ISO simulations remain expensive, particularly at high Reynolds numbers. To enable rapid estimation of the Nusselt number, approximations of the ISO-based Nusselt number are employed—so-called empirical correlations. These correlations are typically expressed as power-law functions of the Reynolds and Prandtl numbers and are calibrated for a specific geometry family (e.g., sphere, cylinder, flat plate).

Historically, empirical correlations have been derived through experimental measurements covering wide ranges of Reynolds and Prandtl numbers. However, due to the high cost and complexity of such experiments, they are often limited to a few canonical geometries. In recent years, numerical simulations have also been used to generate data for correlation development, allowing more complex geometries to be considered. However, simulations are typically restricted to low to intermediate Reynolds numbers, as fully resolving turbulence is prohibitively expensive and turbulence models introduce additional uncertainties. For those reasons, empirical correlations are generally only available for a limited set of geometries and flow conditions.

Realistic geometries encountered in practice often deviate from these canonical shapes for which correlations exist. To still obtain reasonable estimates, a practitioner would first identify the geometry family that best matches the shape of interest and then use the corresponding correlation to estimate the Nusselt number. However, most correlations are only valid for a narrow range of geometries and flow conditions, making it challenging to apply them to complex shapes encountered in real-world applications.

In this section, we propose a framework to systematically extend the validity of existing empirical correlations to much broader classes of geometries by assigning suitably chosen length scales to each geometry, thereby making existing correlations more
universal
. We begin in
Section

5.1
by introducing the concept of
length scale functions
, which formalizes the notion of a characteristic length for general geometries. In
Section

5.2
, we then review empirical correlations with a few well-known examples. Subsequently, in
Section

5.3
, we present a data-driven approach for learning length scale functions from data and apply this methodology to a large dataset of spheroids, demonstrating that a single correlation originally defined for a sphere can adequately approximate the Nusselt numbers across the entire spheroid family. Finally, in
Section

5.4
, we contextualize the numerical results by comparing CHT and ISO Nusselt numbers across different parameter regimes and examining realistic solid-fluid material property ratios.

In the following (up to
Section

5.4
), we assume that we are in the regime where the ISO Nusselt number
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}
closely approximates the CHT Nusselt number
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
, and therefore focus on ISO. Since we are primarily concerned with the space-time averaged Nusselt number, we denote
\operatorname{Nu}\equiv\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}
and, for brevity, refer to it simply as the Nusselt number throughout this section. For the time-dependent, spatially averaged Nusselt number, we will omit the superscript and simply write
\operatorname{Nu}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}(t)
instead of
\operatorname{Nu}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}(t)
. We also note a minor abuse of notation: dimensional quantities such as the coordinates
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muy\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muz\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, or other dimensional parameters, are occasionally treated as elements of sets like
\mathbb{R}
or compared using inequalities (e.g.,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu>0
) for notational convenience.

For simplicity, we restrict our attention to flows over the class of convex domains
\mathcal{C}
that are centered at the origin, symmetric with respect to the coordinate planes, and may be rotated about the
z
-axis. Since the flow direction is taken along the
x
-axis, such a rotation can equivalently be interpreted as a flow at an angle of attack within the
x
-
y
plane on a non-rotated body.

5.1
Length Scale Functions

We introduce the concept of a
length scale function
, denoted by
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, which maps a domain
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mathcal{C}
to a characteristic length
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
. The resulting
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
is a positive real number with the physical dimension of length. The function
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
is required to satisfy the following properties:

1.

Positivity:

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)>0

for all admissible domains
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
;

2.

Invariance:

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mathbf{R}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu+\mathbf{t})=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)

for any rigid-body motion with rotation
\mathbf{R}
and translation
\mathbf{t}
;

3.

Linear scaling:

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(c\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)=c\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)

for any scalar
c>0
.
Here,
c\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
denotes the isotropically scaled domain

c\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\coloneqq\{\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{c}}+c(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{c}})\mid\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\,\},

where
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{c}}
is the centroid of
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
.

There are many common examples for length scale functions. A few examples are provided here:

•

The diameter function
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\text{diam}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\coloneqq\max_{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{y}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{x}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{y}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|
;

•

The equivalent spherical diameter
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\text{sphere}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\coloneqq\left(6|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|/\pi\right)^{1/3}
;

•

The square root of the surface area
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\text{area}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\coloneqq\sqrt{|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|}
;

•

The volume to surface area ratio
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\text{vs}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\coloneqq|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|/|\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\partial\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu|=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{L}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
.

Each length scale function may be more or less suitable for a given problem, depending on the geometry and flow conditions. For convection problems, the characteristic length should be related to the length of the flow path over the body. While this choice is straightforward for simple geometries such as spheres and cylinders (e.g., the diameter), it becomes nontrivial for complex or irregular shapes.

5.2
Empirical Correlations

As mentioned above, practitioners typically estimate the Biot number using empirical correlations, which approximate the ISO Nusselt number. They are generally defined for a parameterized family of geometries, as we now describe.

We consider convex parameterized geometries denoted by

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\alpha}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\bm{\beta})\in\mathcal{C}
,
where

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\alpha}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1},\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2},\ldots,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{M})

represents a vector of dimensional geometric parameters
(e.g., lengths, radii, or thicknesses),
and

\bm{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\ldots,\beta_{N})

collects other non-dimensional parameters
(e.g., rotation angles, aspect ratios).
Varying
(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\alpha}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\bm{\beta})
defines a family of geometries

\mathcal{S}=\{\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\alpha}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\bm{\beta})\mid(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\alpha}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\bm{\beta})\in\mathcal{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{M}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}\,\}\subset\mathcal{C},

where
\mathcal{A}
denotes the admissible parameter space.
A specific geometry corresponding to a particular parameter set

(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\alpha}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(i)},\bm{\beta}^{(i)})

is written as

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(i)}=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\bm{\alpha}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(i)},\bm{\beta}^{(i)})
.

The Nusselt number for the family of geometries
\mathcal{S}
depends on the flow properties, as well as on the geometric parameters. From dimensional analysis, it follows that, for a chosen length scale function
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
and a given
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mathcal{S}
, the Nusselt number can be expressed as a function of the non-dimensional arguments

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nu}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]=f^{\mathcal{S}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]\!\left(\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)],\,\operatorname{Pr},\,\underbrace{\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}},\,\ldots,\,\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{M}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}}}_{\text{$M-1$ terms}},\,\beta_{1},\,\ldots,\,\beta_{N}\right),

(5.1)

where the argument list follows directly from the Buckingham
\pi
theorem. Consequently, only
M-1
independent ratios of the dimensional parameters appear in the expression, obtained by normalizing all dimensional quantities with
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}
. In
Eq.

5.1
, we make explicit the dependence on the characteristic length
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
in the definitions of the Reynolds and Nusselt numbers with square brackets. The ratios of the dimensional parameters do not depend on the length scale.

Empirical correlations approximate this functional dependence through an empirical function
\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nu}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]\approx\undertilde{\operatorname{Nu}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]\coloneqq\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]\!\left(\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)],\,\operatorname{Pr},\,\underbrace{\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}},\,\ldots,\,\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{M}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}}}_{\text{$M-1$ terms}},\,\beta_{1},\,\ldots,\,\beta_{N}\right),

(5.2)

where the undertilde in
\undertilde{\operatorname{Nu}}
emphasizes that it represents an approximation to the actual Nusselt number. The correlation function
\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]
is typically obtained by fitting to experimental or computational data and informed by boundary layer theory, and will depend on the choice of length scale function
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
.

The construction and use of empirical correlations naturally follow an offline-online decomposition. The offline phase involves the expensive process of generating data—either experimentally or through high-fidelity simulations—and subsequently fitting the correlation function
\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]
. The online phase then consists of rapidly evaluating this fitted correlation for new parameter values to estimate the Nusselt number.

Empirical correlations have been developed for many canonical configurations, including flow over flat plates, cylinders, and spheres. Their accuracy typically lies within a relative error of 10–20%, depending on the specific correlation and flow regime
[
20
]
. The limited accuracy arises from several factors, including measurement uncertainties, surface roughness, variations in flow conditions—where even small perturbations in the incoming flow can lead to markedly different behaviors in turbulent regimes—and the restricted datasets used for fitting. Moreover, each correlation is valid only within specific ranges of Reynolds and Prandtl numbers, and geometrical parameters. Nevertheless, these correlations often provide sufficiently accurate results for an engineering estimate at a low computational cost.

In the following, we briefly review a few well-known experimental correlations, and discuss current computational approaches.

5.2.1
Experimental correlations

Due to the complexity and high computational costs of simulating the Navier-Stokes equations, experiments have historically been the primary source of data. Many groups have studied for example flat plates, cylinders and spheres in cross-flow, and developed empirical correlations for the Nusselt number based on experimental measurements. A comprehensive review of such correlations can be found in
[
20
]
. We provide here a few well-known correlations for the flow over a flat plate, circular cylinder and a sphere.

Flat plate aligned with the flow

For the specific case of an infinitely wide flat plate aligned with the flow,
the geometry family can be expressed as

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{fp}}=\{\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{fp}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\mid\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu>0\,\},

where each member corresponds to an idealized plate of length
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
in the
streamwise (
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
) direction, zero thickness in the
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muy\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
-direction,
and infinite width in the
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muz\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
-direction,

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{fp}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)=[-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu/2,\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu/2]\times\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}.

The corresponding length scale function

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{fp}}

is defined as

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{fp}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{fp}})=\max_{(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muy\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muz\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\in\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{fp}}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu-\min_{(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muy\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muz\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\in\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{fp}}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,

which extracts the total extent of the domain in the streamwise direction.
For any
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{fp}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{fp}}
, this yields

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{fp}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{fp}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu))=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
.
The corresponding empirical correlation (for
M=1
,
N=0
) then depends only on the Reynolds and Prandtl numbers (compare
Eq.

5.2
):

\displaystyle\undertilde{\operatorname{Nu}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]=\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{fp}}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]\bigl(\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu],\,\operatorname{Pr}\bigr).

Assuming laminar flows (
\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]\leq 1\times 10^{5}
), the Nusselt number for this configuration can be computed in closed form with boundary layer theory
[
33
]
as

\displaystyle\undertilde{\operatorname{Nu}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]=\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{fp}}}_{\mathrm{lam}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu](\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu],\operatorname{Pr})=0.664\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]^{1/2}\operatorname{Pr}^{1/3},

which is valid for
\operatorname{Pr}\geq 0.6
, and where we introduce the subscript “lam” for laminar. The exponents of
1/2
on the Reynolds number and
1/3
on the Prandtl number are characteristic of laminar boundary layers, in which the boundary layer thickness grows proportionally to
\sqrt{x}
with the distance
x
from the leading edge
[
33
]
.
For turbulent flows, a correlation for the averaged Nusselt number (neglecting the transitional region) based on experimental data is given by
[
25
]

\displaystyle\undertilde{\operatorname{Nu}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]=\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{fp}}}_{\mathrm{turb}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu](\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu],\operatorname{Pr})=0.664\,\operatorname{Re}_{\mathrm{tr}}^{1/2}\operatorname{Pr}^{1/3}+0.037\left(\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]^{4/5}-\operatorname{Re}_{\mathrm{tr}}^{4/5}\right)\operatorname{Pr}^{0.6},

where
\operatorname{Re}_{\mathrm{tr}}
denotes the transitional Reynolds number, typically of order
\mathcal{O}(10^{5})
, and the subscript “turb” stands for turbulent.
In the turbulent regime, the boundary layer growth follows a different scaling, and the corresponding Reynolds exponent in the correlation changes to 4/5.

Circular cylinder in cross-flow

For the case of a circular cylinder of diameter
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
exposed to a uniform cross-flow,
the geometry family can be defined as

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{cyl}}=\{\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{cyl}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\mid\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu>0\,\},

where each member represents an idealized cylinder of infinite length in the

z
-direction and diameter
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
in the
x-y
plane,

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{cyl}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)=\{\,(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muy\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muz\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\mid\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}+\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muy\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}\leq(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu/2)^{2},\ \mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muz\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mathbb{R}\,\}.

We define the corresponding length scale function

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{cyl}}

as

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{cyl}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{cyl}})=2\max_{(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muy\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muz\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\in\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{cyl}}}\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}+\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muy\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}}

which extracts the diameter of the domain in the cross-flow direction.
For any
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{cyl}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{cyl}}
, this gives

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{cyl}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{cyl}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu))=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
.
The corresponding empirical correlation (for
M=1
,
N=0
) then depends only on the Reynolds and Prandtl numbers (compare
Eq.

5.2
):

\displaystyle\undertilde{\operatorname{Nu}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]=\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{cyl}}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]\bigl(\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu],\,\operatorname{Pr}\bigr).

A widely used expression for this configuration is the Churchill-Bernstein correlation
[
8
]
, given by

\displaystyle\undertilde{\operatorname{Nu}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]=\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{cyl}}}_{\mathrm{cb}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu](\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu],\operatorname{Pr})=0.3+\frac{0.62\,\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]^{1/2}\operatorname{Pr}^{1/3}}{\left[1+(0.4/\operatorname{Pr})^{2/3}\right]^{1/4}}\left[1+\left(\frac{\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]}{282000}\right)^{5/8}\right]^{4/5},

valid for
\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]<10^{7}
and
0.7<\operatorname{Pr}<500
. As many other correlations exist for cylinders, we use the subscript “cb” to specify the Churchill-Bernstein correlation. The bracketed terms in the expression become significant primarily at high Prandtl and Reynolds numbers. Note again the
1/2
exponent on the Reynolds number and the
1/3
exponent on the Prandtl number—these arise because the local flow around the cylinder resembles that over a flat plate. Furthermore, the
4/5
Reynolds exponent reappears in the turbulent regime, reflecting the same scaling behavior as observed for the flat plate.

The data underlying the Churchill-Bernstein correlation were experimentally obtained for finite-length cylinders with large length-to-diameter ratios, but the correlation is commonly assumed to remain valid for the limiting case of an infinite cylinder. There also exist other correlations specifically developed for finite-length cylinders that account for end effects, see, e.g.,
[
20
]
.

Sphere in uniform flow

For the case of a sphere of diameter
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
,
the geometry family can be defined as

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{sph}}=\{\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{sph}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\mid\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu>0\,\},

where each member represents a sphere of diameter
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
,

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{sph}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)=\{\,(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muy\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muz\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\mid\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}+\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muy\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}+\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muz\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}\leq(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu/2)^{2}\,\}.

We define the corresponding length scale function

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{sph}}

as

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{sph}}=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{diam}},

where
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{diam}}
denotes the diameter function introduced earlier in
Section

5.1
, which extracts the diameter of the domain.
For any
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{sph}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{sph}}
, this yields

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{sph}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{sph}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu))=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
.
Since the geometry is fully characterized by a single dimensional parameter (
M=1
,
N=0
), the corresponding empirical correlation again only depends on the Reynolds and Prandtl numbers,

\displaystyle\undertilde{\operatorname{Nu}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]=\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{sph}}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]\bigl(\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu],\,\operatorname{Pr}\bigr).

A well-known example for this case is the Ranz-Marshall correlation
[
29
]
,

\displaystyle\undertilde{\operatorname{Nu}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]=\undertilde{f}_{\mathrm{rm}}^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{sph}}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]\bigl(\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu],\operatorname{Pr}\bigr)=2+0.6\,\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]^{1/2}\operatorname{Pr}^{1/3}.

(5.3)

which was originally developed for small droplets at low Reynolds numbers (
\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]\leq 200
).
Nevertheless, it provides reasonable estimates (within 10% error) even up to
\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu]\approx 10^{4}

[
18
]
, as confirmed also in our own simulations; see
Section

5.3.4
.

Here we again observe the
1/2
exponent on the Reynolds number and the
1/3
exponent on the Prandtl number, consistent with the boundary layer theory for a flat plate. The constant term of 2 arises from the purely diffusive contribution to the Nusselt number, which can be derived analytically for a sphere at zero Reynolds number
[
20
]
.

5.2.2
Computational correlations

The experimental correlations presented in
Section

5.2.1
are all defined for geometry families with only a single dimensional parameter (i.e.,
M=1
) and no non-dimensional parameters (i.e.,
N=0
).
Performing experiments over broad ranges of geometries and flow conditions is often prohibitively expensive or even infeasible.
Consequently, empirical correlations for more complex shapes with multiple dimensional or non-dimensional parameters remain scarce.

Recent advances in computational power and numerical methods have made it increasingly practical to compute Nusselt numbers for complex geometries and flow conditions using computational fluid dynamics (CFD).
Such simulations enable systematic studies of configurations that are difficult to realize experimentally and can support the development of correlations that incorporate additional geometric descriptors.
However, CFD-based studies are typically restricted to laminar or weakly turbulent regimes, as fully resolving turbulence remains computationally prohibitive.
Turbulence models, while widely used, introduce additional uncertainties whose quantitative accuracy is often difficult to assess.

It is therefore crucial to reuse and systematically extend existing correlations wherever possible. The following sections develop a framework to achieve precisely this: by selecting a suitable length scale function, we extend the validity of existing correlations to a much broader class of geometries.

5.3
Towards Universal Correlations

In
[
24
]
, it was observed that many empirical correlations for different geometry families in natural convection share a common functional form, differing primarily in their choice of characteristic length scale.
This observation suggests that by identifying a suitable length scale function, one could formulate a single, universal correlation applicable to a broad class of geometries.
This also implies that correlations developed for simple canonical shapes could, in principle, be applied to more complex geometries through an appropriate length scale function.

We propose here a way to find such length scale functions in forced convection. The key concept is to utilize a length scale transformation and automatically learn the length scale function from data, obtained either experimentally or from numerical simulations.

5.3.1
Transformation of length scales

Given a geometry family
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{C}
and domain
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mathcal{S}
, let
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}
and
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}
denote two length scale functions that map
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
to a length scale. A Reynolds number defined with respect to
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
, denoted
\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]
, can be transformed into a corresponding Reynolds number based on
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
as

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\nu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\nu\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}}\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)}=q^{-1}\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)],

(5.4)

where

\displaystyle q\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)}

(5.5)

is the ratio of the two length scales. As
q
is a ratio of two length scales, and length scale functions scale linearly with the domain,
q
is dimensionless and independent of
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
.

Analogously, an (approximate) Nusselt number defined with
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
can be transformed into a Nusselt number defined with
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nu}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]=q^{-1}\operatorname{Nu}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)].

(5.6)

We can now use
Eqs.

5.4
,
5.5
and
5.6

to transform the input and output of a given correlation function

\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)](\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)],\operatorname{Pr},\ldots)

from the characteristic length scale
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
to
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
:

\displaystyle\undertilde{\operatorname{Nu}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]

\displaystyle=q^{-1}\,\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]\!\left(q\,\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)],\,\operatorname{Pr},\,\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}},\,\ldots,\,\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{M}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}},\,\beta_{1},\,\ldots,\,\beta_{N}\right).

(5.7)

This transformation provides a direct means to convert correlations between different length scales. It serves as the key ingredient for learning suitable length scale functions from data to extend existing correlations, as we will discuss next.

5.3.2
General idea to learn length scale functions

Assume that we wish to estimate the Nusselt number for a geometry family

\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{C}

characterized by
M
dimensional parameters

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1},\ldots,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{M}

and
N
dimensionless parameters

\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{N}
.
From
Eq.

5.1
, the Nusselt number for a given

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mathcal{S}
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nu}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]=f^{\mathcal{S}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]\!\left(\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)],\,\operatorname{Pr},\,\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{M}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}},\,\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{N}\right),

(5.8)

where
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}
denotes a chosen length scale function.

Suppose now that we are given an ansatz correlation

\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}^{1}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1}(\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.65973pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\bullet$}}}}})](\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1}(\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.65973pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\bullet$}}}}})],\operatorname{Pr})

that has been fitted for a geometry family

\mathcal{S}^{1}\subset\mathcal{C}

with a single geometrical parameter
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{1}_{1}

and a corresponding length scale function
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1}
,
such as those presented in
Section

5.2.1
for simple canonical geometries. We use the superscript “ref” to indicate that this length scale function was chosen when constructing the correlation for the geometry family
\mathcal{S}^{1}
.
