We would now like to reuse this correlation for the geometry family
\mathcal{S}
.
However, directly applying

\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}^{1}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)](\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)],\operatorname{Pr})

with
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mathcal{S}

will, in general, not yield accurate results,
as the two families
\mathcal{S}^{1}
and
\mathcal{S}

may exhibit substantially different flow behaviors.
Instead, we seek to
learn
a suitable length scale function

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}
such that the correlation

\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}^{1}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)](\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)],\operatorname{Pr})

becomes applicable to
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mathcal{S}
as well.

For
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mathcal{S}
, we express the
unknown
length scale function
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}
with
Eq.

5.5
in terms of a
known
length scale function

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}
as

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\coloneqq q\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu),

where
q
is a function to be determined from data.
To identify
q
, we first employ the transformation introduced in
Eq.

5.7

to express the ansatz correlation

\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}^{1}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)](\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)],\operatorname{Pr})

in terms of the characteristic length
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
, yielding

\displaystyle\undertilde{\operatorname{Nu}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]=q^{-1}\,\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}^{1}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]\!\left(q\,\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)],\,\operatorname{Pr}\right).

(5.9)

Using the transformed correlation
Eq.

5.9
to approximate the general Nusselt relation in
Eq.

5.8
yields

\displaystyle f^{\mathcal{S}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]\!\left(\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)],\,\operatorname{Pr},\,\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}},\,\ldots,\,\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{M}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}},\,\beta_{1},\,\ldots,\,\beta_{N}\right)\approx q^{-1}\,\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}^{1}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]\!\left(q\,\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)],\,\operatorname{Pr}\right).

(5.10)

Comparing the arguments, we observe that the left hand side of
Eq.

5.10

depends on additional geometric parameters

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1},\ldots,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{M}/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{N}

that are not present on the right hand side.
To account for this discrepancy, the length scale ratio
q
must depend on these parameters,

\displaystyle q\!\left(\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{M}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}},\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{N}\right),

(5.11)

so that their influence on the Nusselt number is effectively absorbed into the definition of
q
.
For each geometry
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mathcal{S}
,
the corresponding value of
q
automatically determines an appropriate length scale

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)=q\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)

with which to evaluate the correlation

\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}^{1}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)](\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)],\,\operatorname{Pr})
.

This represents the key idea of the proposed framework:
for every
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mathcal{S}
, we seek a parameter-dependent length scale ratio
q
—independent of the Reynolds and Prandtl numbers—that transforms a chosen and known length scale
\mathcal{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
into a new one,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
,
to be used in the ansatz correlation
\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}^{1}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)](\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)],\,\operatorname{Pr})
.
Although the ansatz correlation may have been originally developed for a simple geometry family
\mathcal{S}^{1}
with length scale function
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1}
,
the learned length scale function
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}
extends its applicability to a broader class of geometries
\mathcal{S}
,
thereby making the correlation more
universal
.
To determine
q
, one must generate simulation or experimental data for the Nusselt number
across the parameter space of
\mathcal{S}
,
as will be demonstrated in the following section.

Comment 6
.

In the discussion above, we considered an ansatz correlation with a single geometric parameter for simplicity.
The same principle, however, extends naturally to more general correlations that involve multiple dimensional and non-dimensional parameters.
In such cases, the length scale ratio
q
would be identified jointly with the additional parameters of the chosen ansatz correlation based on available data.

5.3.3
Learning length scale functions from data

Assume we are given a geometry family
\mathcal{S}
and a dataset obtained for multiple instances of this family, corresponding to different parameter combinations
(\bm{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu},\bm{\beta})
.
For each geometry, simulations are performed at several Reynolds numbers to compute the corresponding Nusselt numbers, while the Prandtl number
\operatorname{Pr}
is held fixed. The resulting dataset can be represented as tuples

\bigl(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(i)},\,\operatorname{Re}^{(i,k)},\,\operatorname{Nu}^{(i,k)}\bigr),\qquad\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(i)}=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\bm{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}^{(i)},\bm{\beta}^{(i)})\in\mathcal{S},

where

\operatorname{Re}^{(i,k)}\coloneqq\operatorname{Re}\!\bigl[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(i)})\bigr]^{(k)},\qquad\operatorname{Nu}^{(i,k)}\coloneqq\operatorname{Nu}\!\bigl[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(i)})\bigr]^{(k)}.

Here,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}
denotes a
known
length scale function. The indices
i=1,\ldots,N_{\Omega}
enumerate the geometry instances, and
k=1,\ldots,N_{i}
enumerate Reynolds-Nusselt samples for each geometry.

For each tuple
(i,k)
, we solve for
q^{(i,k)}
as the minimizer of

\displaystyle q^{(i,k)}\coloneqq\argmin_{q}\left[\operatorname{Nu}^{(i,k)}-q^{-1}\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}^{1}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(i)})]\!\left(q\,\operatorname{Re}^{(i,k)},\,\operatorname{Pr}\right)\right]^{2},

(5.12)

where
\undertilde{f}^{\mathcal{S}^{1}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.65973pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\bullet$}}}}})](\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.65973pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\bullet$}}}}})],\,\operatorname{Pr})
is the chosen ansatz correlation associated with the shape family
\mathcal{S}^{1}
and
unknown
length scale function
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}
, which, for simplicity, is assumed to depend on only a single dimensional parameter (i.e.,
M=1
,
N=0
) here.

Because the data will, in general, not fit the ansatz perfectly, the pointwise values
q^{(i,k)}
will vary with
k
(i.e., across different Reynolds numbers), even though, by construction,
q
should be independent of Reynolds number, see
Eq.

5.11
. To obtain a single representative
q^{(i)}
for geometry
i
, we average the
q^{(i,k)}
over the sampled Reynolds numbers in log-space using a trapezoidal rule. Sorting
\{\operatorname{Re}^{(i,k)}\}_{k=1}^{N_{i}}
in ascending order, we set

\displaystyle q^{(i)}=\frac{1}{\log\operatorname{Re}^{(i,N_{i})}-\log\operatorname{Re}^{(i,1)}}\sum_{k=1}^{N_{i}-1}\frac{q^{(i,k)}+q^{(i,k+1)}}{2}\,\bigl(\log\operatorname{Re}^{(i,k+1)}-\log\operatorname{Re}^{(i,k)}\bigr),

(5.13)

and assign this value to the geometry
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{(i)}
.

Finally, the set

\{(\bm{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}^{(i)},\bm{\beta}^{(i)},q^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{N_{\Omega}}

can be used in a second-stage regression to construct a continuous surrogate

\tilde{q}\!\left(\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{M}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}},\bm{\beta}\right)

over the parameter space.
The learned length scale function is then given by

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\bm{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu},\bm{\beta}))=\tilde{q}\!\left(\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{M}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}},\bm{\beta}\right)\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu(\bm{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\alpha\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu},\bm{\beta})).

(5.14)

Comment 7
.

Note, in this work, we consider a fixed Prandtl number
\operatorname{Pr}
, but there is nothing preventing us from collecting data for different
\operatorname{Re}
and
\operatorname{Pr}
numbers and learning
q
with
Eq.

5.10
. We would expect universality, i.e., no strong dependence of
q
on
\operatorname{Re}
and
\operatorname{Pr}
, for most practical cases with
\operatorname{Pr}>0.6
.

5.3.4
Proof of concept: Sphere

To demonstrate the approach, we consider the Ranz-Marshall correlation defined in
Eq.

5.3
for a sphere of diameter
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
as the ansatz correlation function
\undertilde{f}_{\mathrm{rm}}^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{sph}}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)](\operatorname{Re}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)],\,\operatorname{Pr})
. Here,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1}
corresponds to the diameter function
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{diam}}
introduced in
Section

5.1
, which returns the sphere diameter
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
for any spherical domain. We test whether the proposed framework can recover this reference length scale,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\approx\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)
, given simulation data of a sphere generated using a length scale function
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}
.

We select the length scale function
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{area}}
, defined in
Section

5.1
, which returns the square root of the surface area of the domain, and we denote the resulting characteristic length scale as
\sqrt{A}\coloneqq\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{area}}(\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.65973pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\bullet$}}}}})
. Our goal is therefore to test whether

\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)}=\frac{q\,\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}\approx 1.

To generate simulation data, we solve the flow over the sphere with the ISO formulation with
\operatorname{Pr}=0.71
and
\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]
ranging from
100
to
10^{4}
using Nek5000
[
11
]
. The simulations employ seventh-order polynomials and a BDF2 time integrator with adaptive time stepping, targeting a CFL number of 0.45. For high Reynolds numbers, a filter is applied to attenuate the highest mode by 5% and the second-highest by 1.25%, which is required to ensure long-time stability
[
12
]
. Each case is advanced until a statistically steady state is reached; the algorithm used to detect steady state is described in Appendix
E
. A corresponding mesh convergence study is provided in Appendix
C.2
.

For each Reynolds number, the spatially averaged Nusselt number is shown in
Fig.

5.1
, where time is normalized by the final time
{t}_{\mathrm{f}}
determined from the steady-state criterion. The space-time averaged Nusselt number is indicated by a grey dashed line for each
\operatorname{Re}
. As expected, the Nusselt number increases with increasing Reynolds number and exhibits random oscillations that become more pronounced at higher
\operatorname{Re}
. However, these fluctuations remain small compared to the mean value.

Figure 5.1
:
Spatially averaged Nusselt number for a sphere in uniform flow for various Reynolds numbers, using
\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
as length scale. The space-time averaged Nusselt numbers are indicated by the grey dashed lines.

With the space-time averaged Nusselt numbers for each Reynolds number, we first compare them with the Ranz-Marshall correlation (transformed to length scale
\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
with
Eq.

5.7
) in
Fig.

2(a)
. The correlation slightly overpredicts the Nusselt number, with average deviations of 7%, consistent with the findings of
[
18
]
where errors of up to 10% were reported. Subsequently, we solve the minimization problem in
Eq.

5.12
to obtain
q^{(k)}
, where
k
indexes the Reynolds number samples (we omit the geometry index
i
as there is only one domain), and the resulting ratio
q^{(k)}\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
is plotted in
Fig.

2(b)
. The average
q\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=1.12
is computed with
Eq.

5.13
and also plotted in
Fig.

2(b)
(indicated by the black dashed line). We observe that
q^{(k)}\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
is consistently greater than one. The deviation arises from the intrinsic approximation error of the Ranz-Marshall correlation which overpredicts the Nusselt number, and thus the learned length scale
q\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
cannot perfectly recover the diameter.

Using the learned
q
, we can now evaluate the Ranz-Marshall correlation with
q\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
as the length scale. The modified correlation is also shown as a dashed line in
Fig.

2(a)
. The average deviations from the simulation data are reduced to 2%, demonstrating that the learned length scale improves the correlation’s accuracy.

(a)

Nusselt number in
\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}

(b)

Learned length scales
q^{(k)}\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu

Figure 5.2
:
(a) Comparison of simulation results in Nek5000 with the Ranz-Marshall correlation for a sphere. Ranz-Marshall correlation with learned
q
is also shown. (b) Learned length scales
q^{(k)}\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
plotted over Reynolds number.

5.3.5
Numerical study: Spheroids

In
Section

5.3.4
, we demonstrated that the Ranz-Marshall correlation describes our simulation data for the sphere well, and the accuracy is further improved with the learned length scale
q\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
. We now extend this numerical study to a broader class of geometries—
rotated spheroids
—to investigate whether the Ranz-Marshall correlation can describe this entire family when equipped with learned length scales.

We consider rotated spheroids: the geometry family can be defined as

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{spheroid}}=\{\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{spheroid}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mua\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mub\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\theta)\mid\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mua\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu>0,\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mub\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu>0,\,\theta\in[0,\pi/2]\,\},

where each member represents a spheroid with semi-major axis
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mua\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
,
semi-minor axis
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mub\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, and rotation angle
\theta
about the
z
-axis, corresponding to the angle of attack relative to the incoming flow direction.
The parameterized geometries, centered at the origin, are defined as

\displaystyle\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{spheroid}}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mua\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mub\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\theta)=\left\{(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muy\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muz\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\ \bigg|\ \frac{(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\cos\theta+\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muy\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\sin\theta)^{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mua\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}}+\frac{(-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\sin\theta+\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muy\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\cos\theta)^{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mub\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}}+\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muz\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mub\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{2}}\leq 1\right\}.

(5.15)

Each geometry is characterized by two dimensional parameters (
M=2
)
and one non-dimensional parameter (
N=1
).
According to
Eq.

5.1
, only one non-dimensional combination
of the dimensional parameters can influence the Nusselt number. We choose similarly as before

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{2}=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{area}},

and write
\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}\coloneqq\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{area}}(\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\raisebox{-3.65973pt}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\bullet$}}}}})
.
Using this length scale and with
Eq.

5.1
, the Nusselt number can be expressed as

\displaystyle\operatorname{Nu}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]=f^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{spheroid}}}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]\bigl(\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}],\,\operatorname{Pr},\,s,\,\theta\bigr).

(5.16)

where
s\coloneqq\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mua\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu/\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mub\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
denotes the aspect ratio of the spheroid. Prolate spheroids correspond to
s>1
, oblate spheroids to
s<1
, and the special case
s=1
represents a sphere.

We now want to approximate the Nusselt number for all
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{spheroid}}
in
Eq.

5.16
using the Ranz-Marshall correlation as the ansatz correlation
\undertilde{f}_{\mathrm{rm}}^{\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{sph}}}[\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1}(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu)]
, with
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu^{\mathrm{ref}}_{1}=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\mathcal{D}\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{diam}}
, meaning
q
depends on the aspect ratio and rotation angle. We consider aspect ratios in the range
s\in[0.1,\,10]
, spanning from extremely oblate spheroids (resembling flat plates) to highly prolate spheroids (resembling long cylinders), and rotation angles of
\theta\in[0^{\circ},\,90^{\circ}]
. For data generation, we use the parameter grid

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]

\displaystyle=\{100,\,200,\,300,\,500,\,1000,\,1500,\,2000\},

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle=\{0.1,\,0.133,\,0.2,\,0.4,\,1,\,2.5,\,5,\,7.5,\,10\},

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle=\{0^{\circ},\,15^{\circ},\,30^{\circ},\,45^{\circ},\,60^{\circ},\,75^{\circ},\,90^{\circ}\},

and fix
\operatorname{Pr}=0.71
. This results in a total of
7\times 9\times 7=441
simulations. For the sphere (
s=1
), the angle is irrelevant, reducing the total to 399 simulations.

For each parameter sample, the ISO formulation is solved using Nek5000
[
11
]
. The numerical setup is identical to that described in
Section

5.3.4
, employing the same polynomial order, time-integration scheme, and steady-state detection algorithm (see Appendix
E
). Mesh convergence studies for representative oblate and prolate cases are provided in Appendices
C.3
and
C.4
.

We plot the entire dataset of space-time averaged Nusselt numbers against Reynolds numbers in
Fig.

3(a)
, using
\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
as the characteristic length scale.
The Ranz-Marshall correlation is shown as a black dashed line for reference.
The Nusselt numbers exhibit considerable scatter: the highest and lowest curves correspond to the prolate spheroid under cross-flow and axial-flow (flow perpendicular or aligned with the long axis of the spheroid, respectively) conditions, respectively, while the sphere and the Ranz-Marshall correlation lie roughly in the middle. If the Ranz-Marshall correlation were used to estimate the Nusselt numbers with
\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
for all cases, the maximum deviations reach up to 52%.

We now proceed to learn the length scale function. We compute
q^{(i,k)}
for each case with
Eq.

5.12
, and then average over Reynolds numbers using
Eq.

5.13
to obtain
q^{(i)}
for each geometry defined by
(s,\theta)
. We plot in
Fig.

3(b)
the averaged Nusselt numbers over Reynolds numbers again, but now using the learned length scale
q^{(i)}\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
. We observe that the data points collapse very well onto the Ranz-Marshall correlation, with a maximum error of 15% for the data.

(a)

Length scale
\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}

(b)

Length scale
q^{(i)}\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}

Figure 5.3
:
Nusselt number as a function of Reynolds number from Nek5000 simulations, compared with the Ranz-Marshall correlation. (a) Using the length scale
\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
; (b) using the learned length scale
q^{(i)}\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
.

To assess the extrapolation capability of the learned length scale, we restrict the training data to
\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]=\{100,200,300,500\}
, resulting in four data points per geometry. The resulting Nusselt-Reynolds relationship is shown in
Fig.

4(a)
, where the learned length scale
q^{(i)}\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
is used. Despite the reduced dataset, the Ranz-Marshall correlation still captures the overall trend very well, with a maximum deviation of 20%.
As compared to
Fig.

3(b)
, the spread increases for
\operatorname{Re}[q\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]
larger than
\approx 500
, as data in this range were not included in the learning process.

To further test extrapolation performance, we examine selected high Reynolds number cases up to
\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]=10000
. Specifically, we consider the prolate spheroid with
s=10
under both cross-flow and axial-flow conditions, since these configurations exhibited the largest deviations from the Ranz-Marshall correlation when
\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
was used as the length scale. Together with the sphere data from
Section

5.3.4
, the results are plotted in
Fig.

4(b)
, where
q^{(i)}
was learned only from data with
\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]\leq 500
. We can see that the Ranz-Marshall correlation with the learned length scale still predicts the Nusselt number well even at higher Reynolds numbers. For the prolate spheroid under axial-flow (orange line), the scaling deviates more noticeably from the Ranz-Marshall correlation. For
s=10
, the prolate spheroid is essentially like a long cylinder but in axial-flow, resembling neither a sphere nor a flat plate. The maximum error is 32%, which is still a significant improvement over the 52% error observed when using Ranz-Marshall with
\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
as the length scale. For much larger Reynolds numbers, the accuracy is expected to deteriorate, as the scaling behavior changes in the turbulent regime (the Reynolds exponent deviates from
1/2
), whereas the Ranz-Marshall correlation only contains the
1/2
Reynolds exponent term.

(a)

Nek5000 results up to
\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]\leq 2000
.

(b)

Nek5000 results up to
\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]\leq 10^{4}
.

Figure 5.4
:
Comparison of Nusselt number versus Reynolds number from Nek5000 simulations with the Ranz-Marshall correlation. (a) Data expressed in terms of
\operatorname{Re}[q\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]
, where
q
was identified using data with
\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]\leq 500
. (b) Higher Reynolds number results for the prolate spheroid under cross- and axial-flow, and for the sphere, compared with the Ranz-Marshall correlation using the learned length scale
q\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
(with
q
identified from
\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]\leq 500
).

Using the available data
(s^{(i)},\theta^{(i)},q^{(i)})
, where
q^{(i)}
was identified from cases with
\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]\leq 500
, we construct a continuous surrogate
\tilde{q}(s,\theta)
via piecewise linear interpolation. We plot
\tilde{q}
against
\log_{10}(s)
for various angles of attack in
Fig.

5.5
. The ratio
\tilde{q}
varies fairly smoothly with both the aspect ratio and the angle of attack. A clear trend is observed for prolate spheroids, whereas the behavior for oblate spheroids is less distinct. The overall spread of
\tilde{q}
values is significantly larger for prolate than for oblate spheroids, consistent with the wider variation in Nusselt numbers observed in
Fig.

3(a)
. This difference can be attributed to the flow topology: a prolate spheroid behaves essentially like a long cylinder, where the flow can vary drastically from
\theta=0^{\circ}
to
\theta=90^{\circ}
. For
\theta=0^{\circ}
, the flow impinges directly on the front surface and no longer exhibits local flat plate behavior, whereas for
\theta=90^{\circ}
, the flow resembles a cross-flow over a cylinder, where the local boundary layer development is similar to that of a flat plate. In contrast, for oblate spheroids, for both
\theta=0^{\circ}
to
\theta=90^{\circ}
the flows exhibit regions where the flow remains locally similar to that over a flat plate, resulting in less variation in
\tilde{q}
.

Figure 5.5
:
Learned
q
(for
\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]\leq 500
) over
\log_{10}(s)
for various angles of attack
\theta
.

5.3.6
Test case: Rectangular cuboid

In
Section

5.3.5
, we demonstrated that the proposed framework can successfully learn length scale functions for the rotated spheroid family, thereby extending the applicability of the Ranz-Marshall correlation beyond spheres to highly prolate and oblate shapes. As a result, we have now effectively obtained a correlation for rotated spheroids that spans a large portion of the class of convex geometries
\mathcal{C}
.

As a final test, we consider a geometry
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mathcal{C}
that lies outside the spheroid family,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\not\subset\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{spheroid}}
: a rectangular cuboid with length
6.25\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, width
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, and thickness
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muL\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, subjected to a flow at an angle of attack of
7.5^{\circ}
. This example demonstrates that the learned spheroid correlation can be used to estimate the Nusselt number for more general shapes that remain geometrically similar to spheroids.

The cuboid is solved with the ISO formulation using Nek5000 with
\operatorname{Pr}=0.71
and
\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]=5000
, employing the same numerical setup as described in
Section

5.3.4
. A mesh convergence study for this case is provided in Appendix
C.5
. We show the temperature field in the
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
-
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muy\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
plane (
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muz\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=0
) at the final time step in
Fig.

6(a)
, where we can see significant differences on the top or bottom side due to the angle of attack. The spatially averaged Nusselt number as a function of time is shown in
Fig.

6(b)
, where time is normalized by the final time
{t}_{\mathrm{f}}
determined from the steady-state criterion. Similar as the examples before, the Nusselt number exhibits small random oscillations of small amplitude around a stationary mean value. We obtain a space-time averaged Nusselt number of
\operatorname{Nu}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]=45.71
.

To estimate the Nusselt number for the cuboid, we directly use the Ranz-Marshall correlation together with the learned length scale ratio
\tilde{q}
obtained in
Section

5.3.5
for spheroids with
\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]\leq 500
—no additional data for the cuboid is required. To identify the most representative spheroid for the cuboid, we generate a point cloud of the cuboid surface and perform a principal component analysis (PCA) to fit an equivalent spheroid, which yields an aspect ratio of
s=6.24
. The fit is shown in
Fig.

6(c)
, where we used 500 randomly generated points. Evaluating the Ranz-Marshall correlation with the learned length scale
\tilde{q}(s=6.24,\theta=7.5^{\circ})\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
, we obtain a predicted Nusselt number of
\undertilde{\operatorname{Nu}}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]=47.94
, corresponding to a relative error of
5\%
.

This shows that the Ranz-Marshall correlation combined with the learned length scale function
\tilde{q}
—trained solely on spheroids—can accurately estimate the Nusselt number of a cuboid. The cuboid is geometrically close to its best-fit spheroid, and since geometrically similar bodies share comparable characteristic length scales, they consequently exhibit similar convective heat transfer behavior.

plane (
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muz\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=0
) at the final time step.

for the cuboid.

.

\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mux\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu-\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muy\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
plane (
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muz\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu=0

.

5.3.7
Final remarks and outlook

We have demonstrated that the Nusselt number of the spheroid family
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{spheroid}}
,
covering aspect ratios from 0.1 to 10 and a wide range of angles of attack,
can be adequately approximated over a wide range of Reynolds numbers using the Ranz-Marshall correlation—originally defined for spheres—provided that an appropriate length scale function is employed.
In other words, by learning a suitable length scale function from data,
we effectively extended the applicability of the Ranz-Marshall correlation beyond spheres to the broader class of spheroids.

By performing a best-fit within
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{spheroid}}
,
the derived length scale function for the spheroid family can also be applied to convex bodies outside the spheroid family,
as demonstrated for the cuboid example, where a PCA-based geometric mapping was employed.
This generalization works because geometrically similar shapes tend to share similar characteristic length scales.
However, if a convex domain
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mathcal{C}
is highly dissimilar from any member of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{spheroid}}
—for example, a wedge-shaped body—the accuracy of the estimate will be limited.

It is worth noting that similar ideas have been explored in previous studies. For instance, Culham and Yovanovich
[
9
]
proposed a methodology based on flow path lengths to estimate Nusselt numbers for arbitrary cuboids, using the spheroid correlation they developed earlier in
[
38
]
. However, their approach relies on physical intuition to define flow path lengths and cannot readily handle rotated or arbitrarily oriented geometries.

A natural next step is to apply this framework to additional geometry families for which empirical correlations already exist.
In doing so, one could progressively extend the applicability of established correlations to a wider range of shapes,
thereby building a library of generalized, data-driven correlations.
By reusing existing correlations—which often already account for turbulence—this approach could also provide a viable pathway to describe turbulent regimes, where fully resolved simulations remain prohibitively expensive.
Importantly, the learning process itself does not require high Reynolds number data,
making it computationally efficient;
high Reynolds number simulations would primarily serve for validation.

Once a sufficiently rich set of geometry classes is available,
a classification framework for general convex geometries
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\Omega\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\in\mathcal{C}
could be envisioned.
For example, one could represent geometries as point clouds
and employ modern machine learning classifiers to automatically identify the closest member among all shape families. Although we restricted our study to convex geometries, the approach itself does not rely on convexity and can be applied to other domains, such as star-shaped bodies.

Together, these developments could transform the current ad hoc practice of selecting correlations and characteristic lengths into a systematic, data-driven, and automated framework for convective heat transfer modeling.

5.4
Realistic Fluid-Solid Material Properties

In the previous sections, we assumed that the ISO Nusselt number provides a good approximation of the CHT Nusselt number, and, hence, the Biot approximation error in
Eq.

4.4
is small. This is generally true when both
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are small, which implies a large time scale separation and a small Biot number, respectively.

However, an important practical question remains:
how small is small?
And what are typical
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
values for realistic solid-fluid combinations? To address this, we first present a numerical study in
Section

5.4.1
that investigates the influence of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
on the CHT Nusselt number and compares the results with the ISO model, followed by representative material property ratios in
Section

5.4.2
.

5.4.1
Numerical study on the influence of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
on the Nusselt number

We perform a numerical study to investigate the influence of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
on the space-time averaged Nusselt number in the CHT problem and compare the results with the ISO model. To enable a consistent comparison across different
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
values, we first run the ISO simulation for each parameter pair until the Nusselt number reaches a steady-state value, denoted
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}
. The corresponding end time is then used to run the CHT simulation, from which we compute
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
.

To determine the steady-state, we use the following method: for the current end time
{t}_{\mathrm{f}}
, we compute the time integrals

\displaystyle\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\text{iso},(i)}=\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{0}^{(0.5+0.1i){t}_{\mathrm{f}}}\operatorname{Nu}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}(t)

for
i=1,2,\dots,5
. We check the relative error

\frac{\left|\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\text{iso},(i+1)}-\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\text{iso},(i)}\right|}{\left|\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\text{iso},(i)}\right|}

for
i=1,\dots,4
and compute the average relative error. If it is below 0.5%, we consider the Nusselt number to have reached a steady state.

We consider three geometries: a sphere, a prolate spheroid, and an oblate spheroid, each defined by the equation in
Eq.

5.15
. The aspect ratios and orientations are set as follows:
s=5
with
\theta=0^{\circ}
for the prolate spheroid, and
s=0.2
with
\theta=90^{\circ}
for the oblate spheroid. The parameter ranges
r_{1}\in[0.00264,\,0.16896]
and
r_{2}\in[0.0005425,\,0.03472]
are selected, with the characteristic length taken as the square root of the surface area. Simulations are performed at Reynolds numbers of 100 and 500, with a fixed Prandtl number of 0.71. All computations are carried out using Nek5000, following the same numerical setup as described in
Section

5.3.4
.

The results are shown in
Figs.

5.7
and
5.8
, where we plot, for fixed
r_{2}
, the error over
r_{1}
and vice versa in columns, with different geometries in rows. As expected, for very small
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, the relative error between
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}
and
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{CHT}}
is negligible (around 0.1–1%). The trend with respect to
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
is not strictly monotonic: as
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
increase, the relative error generally grows, reaching up to 17% for the prolate spheroid at
\operatorname{Re}=100
, yet there exist ranges where the difference decreases with increasing
r_{1}
or
r_{2}
. Overall, the errors for the prolate and oblate spheroids are larger than for the sphere, and the influence of
r_{1}
appears to be more pronounced than that of
r_{2}
. Nevertheless, over the range of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
considered, the relative error remains below 20% for all cases, which is on the same order of magnitude as the empirical correlations commonly used in engineering practice.

To better understand why the trend is not monotonic, we consider the thermal diffusivity ratio, defined as
\sqrt{r_{1}/r_{2}}
. This ratio characterizes the relative speed of thermal diffusion in the solid compared to the fluid. The relative errors for different
r_{2}
values are plotted against this ratio in
Fig.

5.9
. Interestingly, all curves corresponding to different
r_{2}
values exhibit a minimum. However, the location and sharpness of this minimum depend on the Reynolds number and geometry: for the sphere, the minimum is broader, whereas for the oblate and prolate spheroids it is much sharper. The minimum generally occurs at a thermal diffusivity ratio of
\mathcal{O}(1)
, indicating that the thermal diffusivities of the solid and fluid are comparable. There appears to be a systematic connection between the thermal diffusivity ratio and the relative error between the ISO and CHT Nusselt numbers, although a detailed investigation of this relationship is beyond the scope of the present work.

Our numerical study provides insight into the inherent CHT-to-ISO errors.
In
Eq.

4.4
, we expressed the Biot approximation error with the CHT Biot number
B^{\mathrm{CHT}}
and an approximation
\undertilde{B}
as

\displaystyle\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}\big|\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B^{\mathrm{CHT}})-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;\undertilde{B})\big|.

This error can be further decomposed using the triangle inequality:

\displaystyle\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}\big|\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B^{\mathrm{CHT}})-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;\undertilde{B})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}\big|\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B^{\mathrm{CHT}})-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B^{\mathrm{ISO}})\big|+\max_{t\in[0,{t}_{\mathrm{f}}]}\big|\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;B^{\mathrm{ISO}})-\widetilde{u}^{\mathrm{L}}(t;\undertilde{B})\big|.

The first term represents the inherent CHT-to-ISO error, which we have quantified numerically for several representative cases in this section.
The second term accounts for the additional error introduced by further approximations of the ISO model, such as the use of empirical correlations.
In principle, one could construct comprehensive tables of the inherent CHT-to-ISO error across different parameter combinations—
(r_{1},r_{2})
, Reynolds numbers, and geometries—and use them as a reference to assess whether the ISO model is suitable for a given application.

It is important to note that even if the relative Nusselt error is small for certain combinations of
\sqrt{r_{1}/r_{2}}
with large
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, implying a small Biot approximation error in
Eq.

4.4
, both the lumping error in
Eq.

4.3
and the temporal approximation error in
Eq.

4.2
increase with
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
. Consequently, a small relative error in Nusselt numbers does not necessarily imply a small total error in the ISO+LCM temperature solution.

.

5.4.2
Material property ratios for realistic fluid-solid combinations

In many traditional engineering applications, such as the cooling of metals in air, the thermophysical property ratios between the working fluid and the solid are indeed small. However, in modern applications, solid and fluid properties can be of comparable magnitude—for example, plastic components in water, metal parts in liquid-metal coolants, or aerogels in air.

Representative thermophysical properties for selected solids and fluids are summarized in
Table

5.1
. The data were collected from
[
25
]
(unless otherwise noted) and correspond to different reference temperatures. These values are not intended for precise quantitative use but rather to indicate the order of magnitude of property ratios relevant to practical material combinations. We consider aluminum, stainless steel, polycarbonate, and silica aerogel as solid materials, and air, water, helium (used as coolant in pebble-bed reactors), and lead (also used as coolant in nuclear reactors) as fluids.

The resulting ratios
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
for all solid-fluid combinations are reported in
Tables

5.2
and
5.3
. For metallic solids in gaseous media (air or helium), both
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are very small. In contrast, for certain combinations—such as aerogel in lead or polycarbonate in water—one or both ratios are on the order of one or higher. In such cases, neither the LCM nor ISO models can be expected to approximate the full CHT behavior accurately. The latter is typically not addressed in heat transfer textbooks such as
[
24
]
.

6
Perspectives

In this manuscript, we have analyzed in detail the approximation error of the CHT problem when modeled using the ISO+LCM approach. While the CHT is the most accurate model for the thermal dunking problem, it is a coupled fluid-solid system with vastly different time scales, making it computationally expensive to solve. The ISO+LCM approximation significantly reduces the computational cost by decoupling the problem into a fluid-only system (ISO) and a solid-only system (LCM). However, this simplification introduces errors that must be carefully evaluated to ensure the accuracy of the results.

The total error can be decomposed into three main contributions: (i) the temporal approximation error arising from time homogenization, (ii) the lumping approximation error introduced by the uniform temperature assumption, and (iii) the Biot approximation error resulting from using the ISO model or empirical correlations to estimate the true Biot number. Below, we summarize the main findings and provide a practical guideline for evaluating the validity of the ISO+LCM approximation in general engineering applications.

Traditionally, for a given problem setup, engineers estimate the Biot number to determine whether the LCM is applicable. If the Biot number falls below a predefined threshold such as
Eq.

4.6
, the LCM is used. Even assuming that the Biot number could be estimated accurately, this criterion does not correctly account for the lumping error and the temporal approximation error.

Our analysis shows that the lumping error depends not only on the Biot number but also on the geometry, the thermophysical properties of the solid (
\kappa
,
\sigma
), and the flow field around the body (
\overline{\eta}
). These dependencies are summarized by the parameter
\phi
, which can be computed with
Eqs.

4.49
and
4.44
. We also derive an upper bound for
\phi
in
Eq.

4.61
, which can be evaluated even when
\kappa
,
\sigma
, and
\overline{\eta}
are not known pointwise. This bound depends only on the variances
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Omega}(\sigma-1)^{2}
and
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)^{2}
, allowing the estimation of the lumping error using only scalar quantities. These variances can often be reasonably estimated based on prior knowledge of the solid material properties, see
Eq.

4.80
, and simplified flow simulations, see Appendix
A
. Importantly, the upper bound captures the effects of spatial variations in the heat transfer coefficient
\overline{\eta}
—a quantity that would otherwise need to be obtained by solving the full CHT problem. Although its evaluation involves computing
\phi(1,1,1)
by solving the PDE in
Eq.

4.49
, as well as two eigenvalue problems for
\mu
and
\Lambda
defined in
Eqs.

4.64
and
4.65
, the computational cost remains negligible compared to that of a full CHT simulation.

Even if the Biot number and lumping error are small, the temporal approximation error—which is typically neglected in practice—may still be significant. Our numerical study in
Section

4.2.3
demonstrates that this error can grow large even for small Biot numbers, depending on the property ratio
r_{1}=(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{f}})/(\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\rho\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}}\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muc\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{s}})
. As
r_{1}
increases, time scale separation becomes less pronounced, and the initial values of the Nusselt number during short times have a significant effect on the average temperature, resulting in large temporal errors, see
Sections

4.2.1
and
4.1
. To quantify this behavior, a ratio is introduced in
Eq.

4.25
, which measures the relative response times of the fluid and solid. However, it remains unclear how large this ratio must be to ensure a small temporal error. Ideally, one would like to derive a practical criterion like

\displaystyle\frac{1}{r_{1}}\frac{\operatorname{Re}\operatorname{Pr}}{\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}\gamma}>f(\text{geometry}),

where
f(\text{geometry})
is a geometry-dependent factor. In the example considered in
Section

4.2.3
, a value of 300 is found. Such a criterion would offer a quantitative condition for the validity of the time scale separation assumption, and ensure that the temporal approximation error is small.

Finally, it is important to recognize that the Biot number estimated using the ISO model may deviate from the true value, particularly when either
r_{1}
or
r_{2}
is large. Our numerical study in
Section

5.4.1
shows that even for moderate values of
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
, the relative error between the ISO and CHT Nusselt numbers can reach up to 20%. This level of deviation may be acceptable, as it is on the same order of magnitude as the uncertainty typically associated with empirical correlations (see
Section

5.2
). However, for many modern applications, where either
r_{1}
or
r_{2}
may take even larger values, one should exercise caution.

Appendix A
Numerical Study of
\overline{\eta}
for Real Flows

To evaluate the upper bound for the lumping error in
Eq.

4.50
without detailed knowledge of the spatial quantities
\kappa
,
\sigma
, and
\overline{\eta}
, we can use the upper bound for
\phi
in
Eq.

4.61
. This bound requires only the geometry and the scalar-valued variances
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)^{2}
and
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Omega}(\sigma-1)^{2}
. The variance of
\sigma
can be computed whenever
\sigma
is piecewise constant with known values and volume fractions, see
Eq.

4.80
. By contrast,
\overline{\eta}
depends on the flow field and is generally unknown in practice.

In this section, we present a numerical study for three representative cases of realistic flows: forced convection over a cylinder with circular cross section, a sphere, and a cylinder with square cross section. We investigate the variance of
\overline{\eta}
across a range of Reynolds numbers, using both experimental data from the literature and simulation data obtained with Nek5000
[
11
]
. Based on the results, we propose a strategy to estimate
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)^{2}
for practical applications.

A.1
Empirical results

A.1.1
Cylinder with circular cross section

Several experimental studies have measured the time-averaged Nusselt number in space around a long cylinder with circular cross section in cross flow, including Giedt
[
13
, Fig. 3]
, Schmidt
[
34
, Figs. 4–6]
, and Achenbach
[
1
, Figs. 14 and 17]
, all at high Reynolds numbers. Since no raw data were provided, we extracted points from the published figures and reconstructed the curves using radial basis function regression. As all data were for high Reynolds numbers, we additionally performed simulations with Nek5000 for low Reynolds number cases. The simulations were carried out in a two-dimensional domain, corresponding to an infinitely long cylinder, as also assumed in the experiments. This approximation is appropriate at low Reynolds numbers, where three-dimensional effects are negligible.

The resulting
\overline{\eta}
curves are shown in
Fig.

A.1
. Since the cylinder is assumed infinitely long,
\overline{\eta}
is defined on the circular cross section and therefore depends only on the azimuthal angle
\theta
. Its shape varies markedly with Reynolds number (evaluated with diameter as the length scale): at low values,
\overline{\eta}
is spread out with no pronounced peak; as Reynolds increases, the profile sharpens and becomes more localized; and at even higher Reynolds numbers, a second peak emerges around
100^{\circ}
, which overtakes the leading edge as the location of maximum heat transfer.

We compute the variance
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)^{2}
for all data sets and summarize the results in
Table

A.1
. The maximum variance for each dataset is highlighted in boldface. At very low Reynolds numbers, the variance increases with Reynolds number up to the onset of vortex shedding (around
\operatorname{Re}\approx 90
), reaching its maximum at
\operatorname{Re}=79.58
. Beyond this point, the variance decreases until around
\operatorname{Re}=1.7\times 10^{5}
, as the flow becomes increasingly turbulent, and the heat transfer around the cylinder tends to become more uniform due to enhanced mixing. A secondary increase in variance is observed between
\operatorname{Re}\approx 1.7\times 10^{5}
and
1.27\times 10^{6}
, corresponding to the so-called drag crisis: the boundary layer transitions from laminar to turbulent, allowing it to remain attached over a larger portion of the surface and leading to a sudden change in both heat-transfer and drag characteristics. Nevertheless, even in this regime, the variance remains far below the maximum attained at low Reynolds numbers.

, the variance decreases with increasing Reynolds number.

A.1.3
Cylinder with square cross section

We also examine the flow around a square cylinder. For this geometry, no experimental data on the local Nusselt number were found, so we conducted simulations with Nek5000. The Reynolds number is defined using a characteristic length corresponding to a perimeter of
\pi\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, allowing direct comparison with the circular cylinder of diameter
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
. As the Reynolds numbers are low, the simulations were performed in two dimensions, and
\overline{\eta}
is defined as a function of the azimuthal angle measured from the forward stagnation point. The
\overline{\eta}
curves are shown in
Fig.

3(a)
and the corresponding variances are reported in
Fig.

3(b)
. Within the limited Reynolds number range studied, the behavior is qualitatively similar to that of the disk:
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}(\eta-1)^{2}
increases with Reynolds number up to the onset of vortex shedding at approximately
\operatorname{Re}=90
, and decreases thereafter. The values, however, are consistently higher than for the disk, which can be attributed to stronger flow separation at the square’s corners.

A.2
Interpretation

The study, though anecdotal, suggests that the variance of heat exchange around a body first increases up to a critical value and then decreases as the flow becomes more turbulent. This indicates that a satisfactory upper bound for
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\partial\Omega}(\overline{\eta}-1)^{2}
can be obtained from low Reynolds number simulations. These cases are relatively simple to compute, and one could in principle construct a database of
\delta^{\overline{\eta}}
for different geometries that could then be used to find
\phi^{\text{ub}}
in
Eq.

4.61
.

Appendix B
Numerical Treatment of Eigenvalue Problems

To evaluate the upper bound for
\phi
in
Eq.

4.61
, one linear PDE (
Eq.

4.49
) and two eigenvalue problems (
Eqs.

4.64
and
4.65
) need to be solved. The numerical treatment of
Eq.

4.49
has been discussed in
[
21
]
(for
\overline{\eta}=1
). Here, we focus on the numerical solution of the eigenvalue problems:

\displaystyle\mu\coloneqq\inf_{w\in Z_{0}(1)}\frac{a_{0}(w,w)}{\int_{\Omega}w^{2}},\quad\Lambda\coloneqq\inf_{w\in Z_{0}(1)}\frac{a_{0}(w,w)}{\int_{\partial\Omega}w^{2}},

where
Z_{0}(1)\coloneqq\{w\in H^{1}(\Omega)\ |\ \mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Omega}w=0\}
, and for
u,v\in H^{1}(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle a_{0}(u,v)\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}\bm{\nabla}u\cdot\bm{\nabla}v.

Using a discretization
X_{h}\subset H^{1}(\Omega)
with basis
\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N_{h}}
, we can approximate the eigenvalue problems by the following matrix problems:

\displaystyle\mu_{h}\coloneqq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{h}}\\
\mathbf{c}^{T}\mathbf{w}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbf{w}^{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{w}}{\mathbf{w}^{T}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{w}},\quad\Lambda_{h}\coloneqq\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{h}}\\
\mathbf{c}^{T}\mathbf{w}=0\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathbf{w}^{T}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{w}}{\mathbf{w}^{T}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{w}},

where the matrices
\mathbf{A},\mathbf{M},\mathbf{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{h}\times N_{h}}
and vector
\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{h}}
are defined as

\displaystyle A_{ij}

\displaystyle\coloneqq a_{0}(\varphi_{j},\varphi_{i})=\int_{\Omega}\bm{\nabla}\varphi_{j}\cdot\bm{\nabla}\varphi_{i},

\displaystyle M_{ij}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}\varphi_{j}\varphi_{i},

\displaystyle B_{ij}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\partial\Omega}\varphi_{j}\varphi_{i},

\displaystyle c_{i}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\Omega}\varphi_{i}.

To impose the constraint
\mathbf{c}^{T}\mathbf{w}=0
, we use a Lagrange multiplier
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and obtain the following saddle point problems:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{c}\\
\mathbf{c}^{T}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{w}\\
\lambda\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\mu_{h}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{M}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{w}\\
\lambda\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}&\mathbf{c}\\
\mathbf{c}^{T}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{w}\\
\lambda\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{h}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{B}&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{w}\\
\lambda\end{bmatrix}.

Eigenvalues are computed with SLEPc
[
17
]
using shift-and-invert method.

Appendix C
Convergence Study and Validation of Simulations

For the three-dimensional simulations considered in this work, polynomial order
p=7
is used, and a mild spectral filter is applied to maintain numerical stability over long simulation times.
Following the recommendations of
[
12
,
11
]
, the filter is applied to the highest two modes, attenuating them by 5% and 1.25%, respectively.
Because the filter amplitudes are small, they have a negligible influence on the solution and therefore do not affect the analysis of the boundary layer profiles discussed above, while ensuring numerical stability of the simulations.

To verify that the spatial resolution is sufficient, we examine the boundary layers near the fluid-solid interface.
This is done by inspecting the temperature variation along lines normal to the interface within individual spectral elements, as shown in the following subsections.
A smooth variation of temperature within each element and an absence of oscillations indicate that the polynomial representation within each element is sufficient to resolve the boundary layer accurately.

C.1
Flow Around Cylinder (CHT)

We show in
Fig.

C.1
the two-dimensional simulation mesh used for the cross-flow around a cylinder in
Section

2.1.4
(assumed infinitely long). Each quadrilateral represents a spectral element of polynomial order
p=7
. A very fine mesh is employed near the fluid-solid interface to resolve the boundary layers, as illustrated in
Fig.

1(b)
. To assess the spatial resolution,
Fig.

C.2
shows the temperature variation along the red line at the leading edge, spanning two elements at the interface. The temperature profile along this line varies linearly, confirming that the mesh resolution is sufficient to capture the boundary layer behavior accurately. A similar behavior is observed at the trailing edge (not shown here).

Entire mesh

(b)

Mesh around cylinder

Figure C.1
:
Simulation mesh for flow around a cylinder. The mesh of the fluid domain is in black and the solid domain in orange. The mesh at the interface is very fine to resolve the boundary layers.

Figure C.2
:
Temperature variation along the red line at the leading edge.

C.2
Flow Around Sphere (ISO)

We show in
Fig.

C.3
the computational mesh used for the case
\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]=10^{4}
described in
Section

5.3.4
, visualized in the plane
z=0
near the sphere surface. A close-up view of the stagnation point region is also provided. The triangular patterns visible in the slice are artefacts of the ParaView visualization and do not correspond to the actual spectral-element discretization. All grid points shown correspond to the Gauss–Lobatto integration points used within each spectral element. The temperature field along the red line within a single spectral element is plotted on the right. The temperature exhibits a slightly nonlinear variation across the element, which is accurately captured by the seventh-order polynomial representation, demonstrating that the thermal boundary layer is well resolved.

.

z=0
; center: zoomed view at the stagnation point; right: temperature variation along the red line at the leading edge.

The same mesh was also used for a lower Reynolds number case of
\operatorname{Re}[\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}]=886
, which was independently computed in
[

]
. In the length scale based on
\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
, their reported Nusselt number is

. Our simulation yields

, corresponding to a relative deviation of

. The spatially averaged Nusselt number as a function of time is shown in

Appendix

E
.

Appendix D
Time Homogenization For A Simplified Robin Heat Equation

In this section, we present a simplified version of the Robin heat equation from
Eq.

2.25
to illustrate the time homogenization procedure. We consider the
\varepsilon
-dependent problem over the time interval
[0,T]
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial u_{\varepsilon}}{\partial t}-\bm{\Delta}u_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

(D.1a)

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}u_{\varepsilon}\cdot\bm{n}+\eta\!\left(\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\right)u_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\Omega,

(D.1b)

\displaystyle u_{\varepsilon}(t=0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=u^{0}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

(D.1c)

where the subscript
\varepsilon
indicates dependence on the small parameter
\varepsilon
and
\eta
is assumed to be periodic (of period
T_{0}
) in time, and
\eta\in L^{\infty}([0,T])
. Furthermore,
\eta
is assumed non-negative for almost all times and not identically zero.
\Omega
is a sufficiently regular domain. We claim, and it is a standard result for which, for the sake of consistency, we are going to provide a proof here, that the homogenized problem reads

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\overline{u}}{\partial t}-\bm{\Delta}\overline{u}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

(D.2a)

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}\overline{u}\cdot\bm{n}+\overline{\eta}\,\overline{u}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\Omega,

(D.2b)

\displaystyle\overline{u}(t=0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=u^{0}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

(D.2c)

where
\overline{\eta}
is constant and denotes the time average of
\eta
over
[0,T_{0}]
,

\displaystyle\overline{\eta}\coloneqq\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{0}^{T_{0}}\eta,

which is strictly positive, and the oscillatory coefficient
\eta
converges weak-* in
L^{\infty}([0,T])
to
\overline{\eta}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Our goal is to show that the solution
u_{\varepsilon}
of
Eq.

D.1
converges to the solution
\overline{u}
of
Eq.

D.2
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Multiplying
Eq.

D.1a
by
u_{\varepsilon}
and integrating over
\Omega
and in time over
[0,T]
yields

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|u_{\varepsilon}(T,\cdot)|^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\bm{\nabla}u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\eta\!\left(\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\right)\int_{\partial\Omega}|u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|u^{0}|^{2}.

From this estimate we immediately obtain a bound uniform in
\varepsilon
in space

\displaystyle L^{\infty}\!\big([0,T],L^{2}(\Omega)\big)\cap L^{2}\!\big([0,T],H^{1}(\Omega)\big).

The same manipulations with
Eq.

D.2
show that:

\displaystyle\overline{u}\in L^{\infty}\!\big([0,T],L^{2}(\Omega)\big)\cap L^{2}\!\big([0,T],H^{1}(\Omega)\big).

We now consider the difference

\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon}\coloneqq u_{\varepsilon}-\overline{u},

which is also a bounded sequence in
L^{\infty}\!\big([0,T],L^{2}(\Omega)\big)\cap L^{2}\!\big([0,T],H^{1}(\Omega)\big)
and satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\partial v_{\varepsilon}}{\partial t}-\bm{\Delta}v_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega,

(D.3a)

\displaystyle\bm{\nabla}v_{\varepsilon}\cdot\bm{n}+\eta\!\left(\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\right)v_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=-\Big(\eta\!\left(\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\right)-\overline{\eta}\Big)\,\overline{u}

\displaystyle\text{on }\partial\Omega,

(D.3b)

\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon}(t=0,\cdot)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{in }\Omega.

(D.3c)

Since
v_{\varepsilon}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}([0,T],L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}([0,T],H^{1}(\Omega))
, there exists a function
\overline{v}
and a subsequence (not relabeled) such that

v_{\varepsilon}
converges weak-* to
\overline{v}
in
L^{\infty}([0,T],L^{2}(\Omega))
and weakly to
\overline{v}
in
L^{2}([0,T],H^{1}(\Omega))
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. We want to show that
\overline{v}=0
, which implies that
u_{\varepsilon}
converges to
\overline{u}
in the same topology.

Because of
Eq.

D.3a
, we infer that
\dfrac{\partial v_{\varepsilon}}{\partial t}
is a bounded sequence in

\displaystyle L^{2}([0,T],H^{-1}(\Omega)).

Since
H^{1}(\Omega)
is compactly embedded in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, and
L^{2}(\Omega)
is at least continuously embedded in
H^{-1}(\Omega)
, we can apply the Aubin–Lions Lemma
[
6
, Theorem II.5.16]
,

\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon}\to\overline{v}\quad\text{in }L^{2}([0,T],L^{2}(\Omega))\quad\text{as }\varepsilon\to 0,

that is,
v_{\varepsilon}
converges
strongly
to
\overline{v}
in
L^{2}([0,T],L^{2}(\Omega))
.

Since
v_{\varepsilon}
converges weakly to
\overline{v}
in
L^{2}([0,T],H^{1}(\Omega))
, and strongly in
L^{2}([0,T],L^{2}(\Omega))
, an interpolation argument yields

\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon}\to\overline{v}\quad\text{in }L^{2}([0,T],H^{1/2}(\Omega))\quad\text{as }\varepsilon\to 0.

Applying the trace theorem then gives the strong convergence

\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon}\to\overline{v}\quad\text{in }L^{2}([0,T],L^{2}(\partial\Omega))\quad\text{as }\varepsilon\to 0.

We can now multiply
Eq.

D.3a
by
v_{\varepsilon}
and integrate over
\Omega
and over
[0,T]
to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|v_{\varepsilon}(T,\cdot)|^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\bm{\nabla}v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\eta\!\left(\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\right)\int_{\partial\Omega}|v_{\varepsilon}|^{2}=-\overline{u}\int_{0}^{T}\left(\eta\!\left(\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\right)-\overline{\eta}\right)\int_{\partial\Omega}\,v_{\varepsilon}.

On the right hand side,
\eta\!\left(\tfrac{t}{\varepsilon}\right)-\overline{\eta}
converges weak-* to
0
in
L^{\infty}([0,T])
. Since
v_{\varepsilon}
converges strongly to
\overline{v}
in
L^{2}([0,T],L^{2}(\partial\Omega))
, it also converges strongly in
L^{2}([0,T],L^{1}(\partial\Omega))
, and hence the right hand side vanishes as
\varepsilon\to 0
. On the left hand side,
v_{\varepsilon}
converges weakly to
\overline{v}
in
L^{2}([0,T],H^{1}(\Omega))
, and strongly in
L^{2}([0,T],L^{2}(\partial\Omega))
. Observing that the leftmost term is non-negative, we may thus pass at the
\liminf
in the left hand side and obtain:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}|\bm{\nabla}\overline{v}|^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\overline{\eta}\int_{\partial\Omega}|\overline{v}|^{2}=0.

As all terms on the left are non-negative and
\overline{\eta}>0
, this implies
\overline{v}\equiv 0
. Using uniqueness of the limit of subsequences and compactness, we conclude that the full sequence converges.

Appendix E
Algorithm for Determination of Steady State of ISO Simulation

To determine the space-time averaged Nusselt number
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}
from the ISO simulation, it is essential to ensure that the flow has reached a statistically stationary state. To minimize computational cost, the simulation should be terminated as soon as
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}
no longer varies significantly with time.

The dominant vortex shedding oscillation frequency, denoted by
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muf\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{vs}}
, is estimated using the Strouhal number. The Strouhal number is defined as

\displaystyle\operatorname{St}=\frac{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muf\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{vs}}\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}},

(E.1)

where
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muf\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{vs}}
is the dimensional vortex shedding frequency,
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\ell\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
the characteristic length, and
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muv\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\infty}
the far-field velocity.
In our nondimensionalization based on the solid diffusive time scale (see
Eq.

2.3
), the corresponding nondimensional frequency becomes

\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{vs}}=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muf\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{vs}}\,\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0mu\tau\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu_{\mathrm{diff}}=\operatorname{St}\,\frac{r_{2}}{r_{1}}\,\operatorname{Re}\,\operatorname{Pr}.

(E.2)

A representative value of
\operatorname{St}=0.2
is adopted for all cases, consistent with experimental observations for bluff bodies such as spheres
[
23
]
and cylinders
[
24
]
over a broad range of Reynolds numbers (based on diameter).

We use a sliding window to average in time. The sliding window must be sufficiently wide to capture several oscillations of the instantaneous Nusselt number. From
Eq.

E.2
, the oscillation period follows as

t_{\mathrm{vs}}=1/f_{\mathrm{vs}}.

The initial width of the sliding window is set to
5\,t_{\mathrm{vs}}
, ensuring that at least five oscillation cycles are included in each average.
A new average is computed after shifting the window forward by a “step size” of
0.5\,t_{\mathrm{vs}}
; this shift defines one “step”. At each step, we compute
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}
over the current window and monitor its temporal convergence. The simulation is considered statistically stationary once the average relative change in
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}
over the last five consecutive steps falls below
0.1\%
, after which it is terminated.

To exclude the influence of the initial transient phase, the convergence criterion is applied only after the simulation time exceeds
7.5\,t_{\mathrm{vs}}
.
To further ensure convergence of the sliding-window averages, beginning at
t=5\,t_{\mathrm{vs}}
, the window width is gradually increased by
0.05\,t_{\mathrm{vs}}
every time the simulation time advances by
0.5\,t_{\mathrm{vs}}
, while the step size remains fixed at
0.5\,t_{\mathrm{vs}}
.
Because the flow and thermal responses vary across geometries, the precise values of the initial window width and step size were occasionally adjusted empirically.

For validation, we compared our implementation against the results of
[
4
]
for flow over a sphere at a Reynolds number (based on diameter) of
\operatorname{Re}=500
. The reference value reported there is
\operatorname{Nu}\approx 13.27
, while the present algorithm yielded
\overline{\operatorname{Nu}}_{\partial\mathrm{avg}}^{\mathrm{ISO}}=13.31
, corresponding to a relative deviation of only
0.3\%
. The temporal evolution of the spatially averaged Nusselt number, together with the final window width, were shown in
Fig.

C.4
. The difference in the Reynolds and Nusselt values arises from the different choices of characteristic length scale—here the diameter
\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu
, and there
\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}
. The two length scales are related by
\sqrt{\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muA\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu}=\mkern 1.0mu\underline{\mkern-1.0muD\mkern-1.0mu}\mkern 1.0mu\sqrt{\pi}
.
